biodegradability. Use of plants for the synthesis of metallic nanoparticles has
attracted more attention in recent years due to its rapid, environmentally friendly,
pathogenic and economical protocol, providing a single-step mechanism for the
biological process (Chandrakala et al. 2022). Different parts of a plant can be used
such as roots, stems, leaves, ﬂowers, fruits, and seeds. Plants are rich in metabolites
like alkaloids, phenoloids, and phenolic acids. These compounds can act as reducing
agents and stabilizers and prevent particles from agglomeration while controlling
their size. Table 2.1 is a summary of the latest works done in the ﬁeld of metal NPs
synthesis from plants. Biological synthesis of NPs by microorganisms is also
feasible. Using their enzymes, bacteria are capable of converting metal ions into
metal atoms. This method is promising for synthesizing NPs because it requires low
energy and is capable of controlling the synthesis process. For example, Ag NPs
were synthesized from the source of Streptacidiphilus durhamensis HGG16n with a
size range of 8–48 nm. Similarly, Bacillus endophyticus and Deinococcus
radiodurans were capable of producing Ag NPs with different morphologies and
sizes (Chandrakala et al. 2022). In different studies, Psychrophilic bacteria,
Phaeocystis antarctica, Pseudomonas meridiana, Arthrobacter kerguelensis,
Arthrobacter gangotriensis, Bacillus indicus, Bacillus subtilis, Klebsiella pneumo-
nia, and Bacillus cecembensis were employed to biosynthesize Ag NPs with sizes
ranging from 6 to 13 nm. It seemed that the generation and stability of Ag NPs
depended on temperature, pH, or kind of bacteria from which the supernatant was
obtained. Several shapes and sizes of Ag NPs were found in the ﬁnal product. These
30                                                                M. Fatemi and Z. Bahrami


included hexagonal, spherical, triangular, circular, and cuboidal which depended on
the culture medium utilized for the growth of bacteria (Alsamhary 2020; Chopra
et al. 2022; Ibrahim et al. 2019; Ibrahim et al. 2021; Saleh and Alwan 2020). The
same way as Ag NPs, synthesis of Au NPs from bacteria is also possible. Various
types of bacteria were used for this purpose including Bacillus subtilis 168, Micro-
coccus yunnanensis strain J2, Shewanella algae, Rhodopseudomonas capsulate,
Plectonema, Rhodobacter, Delftia sp, and Marinobacter algicola. By controlling the
synthesis parameters such as temperature, pH, and concentration, NPs with different
sizes (100–5 nm) and various morphologies (spherical, octahedral, cubic, periplas-
mic) were obtained (El-Bendary et al. 2022; Feng et al. 2008; He et al. 2007; Jafari
et al. 2018; Sayadi et al. 2021; Wu and Ng 2017). Kumar et al. prepared Au NPs
using culture supernatant of Delftia sp. strain KCM-006 and used them as a carrier
for the anticancer drug Resveratrol. Spherical shaped Au NPs with an approximate
size of 11.3 nm and a zeta potential of -25 mV showed good stability. The designed
release system was pH-sensitive and showed the highest release rate (95%) in the
acidic medium (pH = 5.2) (Kumar et al. 2014).

2.1.1.2 Metal Oxide NPs

2.1.1.2.1 Sol-Gel Method
Sol-gel is one of the common and well-known bottom-up methods in the liquid
media which is used to produce various types of nanomaterials. Fundamentals of
sol-gel method are: production of a sol and its conversion into a gel. Sol which has
been derived from the word “Solution” is a colloidal mixture, a blend of solid
particles less than 100 nm in a liquid phase. Due to the Brownian motion of
nanoparticles, they do not precipitate and would be dispersed in the liquid phase.
Generally, two types of precursors are used in the sol-gel process: metal salts and
metal alkoxides. Partial or complete hydrolysis also occurs in this technique. For
example, in the synthesis of silica (SiO2) NPs, tetraethyl orthosilicate (TEOS) is used
as a precursor. This compound has 4 Si-OR groups. If all four OR groups convert
into OH groups, the hydrolysis will be complete, but if the number of conversions is
less than 4, the hydrolysis will be partial (see Fig. 2.2).
    At this stage, the sol is formed and then it must be converted into a gel based on
condensation reactions. These reactions are reversal of the hydrolysis in such a way
that in condensation, two small molecules bond to each other in order to make a
larger molecule, while in hydrolysis, the molecules are decomposed. During the
condensation process, temperature conditions and pH changes are the effective
parameters. There are two types of condensation reactions: alcoholic and aqueous.
If the water is removed in the condensation reaction, it is called aqueous, and if the
alcohol is removed, it is called alcoholic. The result of both is the same, formation of
a bond between two silicon groups through an oxygen bridge. Such a structure is
called a gel. The initial gel is very voluminous. It ﬁlls the entire volume of the
reaction chamber and traps the solvent inside it. For this, the initial gel is called a wet
gel. As long as the solvent is inside the gel, it will not have a strong structure, so it
must be dried. Before the drying stage, aging is a must. At this step, the gel has a
2   Synthesis of Different Types, Shapes, and Sizes of Nanocarriers. . .              31




Fig. 2.2 Schematics of sol-gel synthesis method


chance to form new bonds and thus becomes stronger. This process may last a few
hours to several days (Navas et al. 2021). The last step in the sol-gel method is gel
drying. This process can occur in three ways: evaporation, supercritical drying, and
freeze drying. Depending on the drying procedure used, the ﬁnal product will be
different. During the evaporation, the gel is dried at room temperature, and solvents
such as water and ethanol are easily evaporated and removed. In this condition, the
capillary pressure within the gel will increase and cause the network to shrink. As a
result, the gel will collapse and its volume will reduce 5–10 times the wet gel. The
ﬁnal product obtained this way is called xerogel, which has an integrated structure
without any porosity. In supercritical drying, an autoclave reactor is used. The
critical temperature is determined according to the phase diagram of the used
solvent. First of all, the gel is put inside the autoclave chamber and then the autoclave
is placed inside the oven. When the solvent’s temperature reaches the critical
temperature, the pressure inside the chamber increases gradually. After the super-
critical conditions is reached, the solvent within the gel turns into a supercritical
ﬂuid. By making the temperature ﬁxed and gradual reduction of the pressure, the
supercritical ﬂuid turns into gas and leaves the system. In this case, the porous
structure of the wet gel is preserved and this causes the formation of an aerogel.
Aerogel is a very lightweight porous structure which is considered as a category of
3D nanomaterials. Use of aerogel as a drug delivery system is one of the signiﬁcant
applications of this nanomaterial. Due to the high critical temperature of water (374 °
C) and ethanol (240 °C), other solvents with lower critical temperatures are also used
in the supercritical drying. Carbon dioxide is one of the potential solvents for
replacing water or ethanol, because it has a critical temperature close to the ambient
temperature (~31 °C). In this case, the gel’s drying method is called cold supercriti-
cal drying. Freeze drying is the counterpoint of supercritical drying. In this tech-
nique, ﬁrst the solvent within the wet gel freezes and then is sublimed away slowly at
low temperatures. The solvent removal does not cause network shrinkage and thus
preserves the structural porosity. The ﬁnal product of this technique is called cryogel,
which is similar to the aerogel regarding the structural properties (Bokov et al. 2021).
The important inﬂuential parameters in the sol-gel method are: type of the precursor,
32                                                               M. Fatemi and Z. Bahrami


hydrolysis rate, pH, and calcination temperature. If the precursor’s hydrolysis rate is
very high, precipitation will occur before the sol is formed. In order to control the
hydrolysis rate and thus the sol formation, chelating agents such as acetic acid,
maleic acid, citric acid, tartaric acid, oxalic acid, and polyacrylic acid are used. When
chelating agents are present, a compound forms between the precursor and the
chelating agent before the hydrolysis process. The hydrolysis rate of this compound
is slower than that of the precursor. Therefore, the sol has enough time to form.
Conversely, the chelating agent may inﬂuence controlling the size and morphology
of the ﬁnal product. The effect of the chelating agent’s concentration change can be
studied using a parameter called complex coefﬁcient (X). This parameter is obtained
by dividing the chelating agent’s concentration by the concentration of metal ions. In
the synthesis of a certain type of NPs, various chelating agents will work differently.
This fact was investigated in the synthesis of zirconia (ZrO2) nanoparticles (Kazemi
et al. 2020). In this study, citric acid, sucrose, and fructose were used as the chelating
agents and zirconium hydroxide was the precursor. Compared with the other two
chelating agents, when the fructose was used, ZrO2 nanoparticles had the lowest size
(39 nm), the highest surface area (27 m2 g-1), and a tetragonal crystal phase. But, in
the presence of citric acid and sucrose, a mixture of monoclinic and tetragonal
crystalline phases was obtained. In order to remove the chelating agent, rinsing or
calcination can be used. Calcination temperature affects the particle size and crystal-
linity. In a study in 2021, TiO2 nanoparticles were synthesized using titanium
tetraisopropoxide, isopropanol, and acetic acid as precursor, solvent, and chelating
agent, respectively. The resulting sol turned into a gel after 12 h. Following the
drying at 110 °C, the obtained gel was calcined at 400 °C, 600 °C, and 800 °C for
5 h, in order to investigate the effect of calcination temperature. XRD results showed
that as the temperature increases, the thermodynamically more stable crystalline
phase of rutile is the main phase in the synthesized samples. In addition, with the
temperature rise, more growth is possible due to the increase in the amount of energy
available for the initial nuclei, and the particles size also increases as a result
(Saikumari et al. 2021). The effect of pH on size, morphology, and crystallinity of
zinc oxide (ZnO) nanoparticles was investigated by Alias et al. (2010). In order to
synthesize ZnO nanoparticles, a certain amount of zinc acetate was dissolved in
methanol. After a clear solution was obtained, the pH was adjusted in the range of
6–11 using NaOH. In this pH range, particles with a size range of 50–36 nm were
synthesized. Increasing the pH from 6 to 11, increased the crystallinity while
decreased the size and agglomeration of the particles.

2.1.1.2.2 Solvothermal/Hydrothermal Method
Solvothermal method is among the most important liquid phase techniques used for
the synthesis of nanomaterials. In general, high temperature synthesis can reduce the
particles size. However, most of safe, cost-effective, and readily available solvents
such as water have a relatively low boiling point and cannot be used in high
temperatures under normal conditions. In order to address this limitation, an auto-
clave reactor is used. This way, the solvents can be used at temperatures higher than
their boiling point without their volume change. Sealing the reaction chamber’s cap
2   Synthesis of Different Types, Shapes, and Sizes of Nanocarriers. . .            33


prevents the solvent’s vapor from releasing and at the same time increases the vapor
pressure within the chamber. Therefore, the synthesis is possible at temperatures
higher than the boiling point of the solvent and under pressures higher than the
ambient pressure. Simplicity is another advantage of this method. Depending on the
ﬁnal product, an appropriate precursor is selected and dissolved in a proper solvent.
Then the resulting solution is transferred to the reaction’s chamber and placed in an
oven with predetermined temperature for a speciﬁc time period. After this period and
when the autoclave is cool, the reaction chamber’s contents are removed and
centrifuged. The ﬁnal product will be in a solid phase and will not need any other
puriﬁcation. However, sometimes calcination is done in order to achieve a certain
crystal phase. If water is used as the solvent, the technique will be called hydrother-
mal, while in the solvothermal technique non-aqueous solvents such as ethanol,
methanol, ethylene glycol, 1,4-butanediol, and toluene are used (Choudhury et al.
2022). In this technique, factors such as type of the precursor, reaction’s time and
temperature, and additives can affect the ﬁnal product characteristics, namely size,
morphology, crystallinity, and crystal phase. Lots of metal oxide nanoparticles have
been synthesized with this method, including Al2O3, CuO, Fe2O3, NiO, ZrO2, TiO2,
BaTiO3, SrTiO3, and Fe3O4. These nanoparticles can be synthesized by both hydro-
thermal and solvothermal methods (Li et al. 2016). Following is a summary of
related studies. Ahmadi et al. (2012) synthesized superparamagnetic Fe3O4 NPs
using FeCl2, FeCl3, and NaOH with a molar ratio of 1:2:8 under hydrothermal
conditions at temperatures 100 °C, 150 °C, and 200 °C for 30 min. Results showed
that increasing the synthesis temperature caused particles to grow. In addition,
saturation magnetization and coercivity of the obtained NPs also increased. Temper-
ature rise did not have any effects on the NPs morphology and all the synthesized
samples were spherical in shape. In 2018, Kim et al. (2018) synthesized
superparamagnetic Fe3O4 NPs with solvothermal method using different
concentrations of iron (III) chloride, trisodium citrate dihydrate, and sodium acetate
in ethylene glycol as the solvent at 200 °C and for 10 h. Resulted NPs were spherical
in shape and the particles size increased to ~300 nm with increasing the iron salt
concentration. The synthesized NPs showed superparamagnetic properties, high
saturation magnetization, high stability, and hydrophilicity. Temperature and time
are the most signiﬁcant parameters in the solvothermal/hydrothermal method. In
2020, Mohan et al. (2020) investigated the effect of these two parameters on the
synthesis of ZnO NPs. They used zinc nitrate as the precursor. In order to investigate
the effect of temperature, time was ﬁxed and the synthesis was carried out at
temperatures 100 °C, 125 °C, and 150 °C for 2 h. To study the effect of time, the
reaction temperature was ﬁxed at 120 °C and the synthesis was performed for 1, 3,
and 5 h, respectively. XRD results showed that increasing the temperature decreased
the crystallite sizes while increasing the time caused the crystallites to grow. All of
the synthesized samples had the same wurtzite crystal phase. SEM micrographs
showed that the particles size and morphology were strongly inﬂuenced by the
synthesis temperature and time. At the temperatures 100 °C and 125 °C, ZnO
ﬂower-like nanoparticles were synthesized. By increasing the temperature to 150 °
C, the morphology changed and spherical nanoparticles were obtained. ZnO
34                                                              M. Fatemi and Z. Bahrami


nanorods were synthesized at a ﬁxed temperature (120 °C) and different times. Time
increase caused the initial nuclei to grow and the rods to get longer. In recent years,
use of supercritical water for synthesis of metal oxide NPs by hydrothermal method
has attracted many attention. In the critical point of water (374 °C and 22.1 MPa) its
characteristics change, and this allows the synthesis of nanomaterials. Batchwise and
continuous methods are the two potential techniques for the synthesis of NPs using
supercritical water. The batchwise method is very similar to the conventional
hydrothermal method. The only difference is that the temperatures needed for the
synthesis are higher than the critical temperature of water. The autoclave used must
be equipped with a pressure gauge. In order to avoid corrosion of the inner surface of
the autoclave, which is in contact with supercritical water, it can be coated with a thin
layer of nickel or platinum (Yoko et al. 2018). In continuous method, ﬁrst the
precursor is dissolved in water and heated a bit. In a separate step, water is converted
into the supercritical water stream and then it is mixed with the precursor solution.
The resulting mixture is transferred to the autoclave chamber and synthesis is
performed under supercritical conditions. It has been proved that mixing the super-
critical water with a lower temperature solution inﬂuences the ﬁnal product
properties strongly. The mixing rate must be higher than the rate of the chemical
reaction that leads to the ﬁnal product. Following the synthesis process termination,
the contents within the reaction chamber are quickly cooled down and after that the
precipitate is separated. Compared with the batchwise method, the NPs obtained
from the continuous method are smaller and have a narrower particle size distribu-
tion (Yoko et al. 2020). Using the supercritical water, synthesis of a wide range of
metal oxides including AlOOH, α-Fe2O3, Fe3O4, Co3O4, NiO, ZrO2, and TiO2 with
a size range of 20–200 nm is achievable (Yoko et al. 2018).

2.1.1.2.3 Co-precipitation Method
The same way as metal NPs, metal oxide NPs can also be synthesized using
co-precipitation method in aqueous and non-aqueous media. Fe3O4 NPs are
among the most signiﬁcant metal oxide NPs synthesized with this method in an
aqueous medium. There are many reports regarding their synthesis (Ba-Abbad et al.
2022; Dheyab et al. 2020; LaGrow et al. 2019; Panariello et al. 2022; Slimani et al.
2021; Wen and Jin-Ming 2014). Typically, iron (II) and iron (III) salts with a molar
ratio of 1:2 in the presence of an alkalizing agent such as NaOH or ammonia are
used. The reaction medium’s pH is usually adjusted in the range of 9–14. In order to
avoid the oxidation of iron ions and synthesis of Fe2O3 instead of Fe3O4, the reaction
is carried out under an inert atmosphere such as N2 gas. The ﬁnal product is a dark
brown or black precipitate. Although the co-precipitation method is a simple and
relatively fast technique for synthesis of Fe3O4 nanoparticles, reaching suitable
conditions for mass production of NPs with controlled size and morphology is still
a challenge and needs more detailed studies. Therefore, in recent years, there have
been many studies related to the improvement of the co-precipitation method
effective parameters. In 2022, the effect of additives on Fe3O4 particles morphology
and size was investigated. In this study, ammonium hydroxide and sodium hydrox-
ide were used as additives. The results showed that in the presence of ammonium
2   Synthesis of Different Types, Shapes, and Sizes of Nanocarriers. . .            35


hydroxide, the synthesized NPs were the same in size with a spherical shape, while
when using sodium hydroxide, the morphology changed from spherical to cubic. In
addition, compared with the ammonium hydroxide, sodium hydroxide caused the
growth of initial nuclei and formation larger NPs. Size of the synthesized NPs in the
presence of NH4OH and NaOH was 10 ± 2 nm and 32 ± 2 nm, respectively.
Although both samples showed ferromagnetic properties, NPs with cubic morphol-
ogy had a higher saturation magnetization. The reason is the effect of structural
characteristics on magnetic properties of NPs (Ba-Abbad et al. 2022). In another
study conducted by Dheyab et al. in 2020, the effect of citric acid as a surface
modiﬁcation agent on size, morphology, magnetic properties, stability, and hydro-
philicity of Fe3O4 NPs was investigated. Surface modiﬁcation was implemented in a
single step simultaneous with NPs synthesis. The operating temperature was 65 °C
with a relatively short time interval (30 min) compared with the conventional
co-precipitation method. In this study, surface modiﬁed Fe3O4 NPs ~19 nm in
size, with spherical morphology and a narrow particle size distribution were
synthesized. Zeta potential increase from -31 mV in pristine NPs to -45 mV in
the surface modiﬁed NPs indicating the stability was increased. The latter had a
relatively good saturation magnetization and due to the reduced agglomeration and
increased stability, was suggested for biomedical applications (Dheyab et al. 2020).
With the aim of achieving a scalable method, Gavriilidis et al. used microwaves with
co-precipitation method for the synthesis of Fe3O4 NPs. For this, a ﬂow reactor was
used and the effect of residence time and microwave power was investigated. An
increase in both parameters led to NPs size increase. By controlling the synthesis
conditions, NPs with a size range of 35–70 nm were synthesized. The presence of
stabilizing agents such as citric acid and dextran reduced the synthesis time. Capac-
ity of mass production in this method was evaluated by increasing the dimensions of
the reactor used. The best production rate obtained was 3.3 g/h (Panariello et al.
2022).

2.1.1.2.4 Combustion Method
Combustion synthesis (CS) is a technique for achieving a rapid and energy-efﬁcient
synthesis. A typical CS procedure utilizes a self-sustained exothermic reaction
among well-mixed reactants to achieve the rapid and economical synthesis of
particulate products. CS is classiﬁed into self-propagating high temperature synthe-
sis (SHS) and solution combustion synthesis (SCS). In conventional SHS, the
reactants are all in a solid state, still suffering from an inhomogeneous mixing. In
order to reduce the particles size, additional treatments such as mechanical activa-
tion, intensive milling, or chemical dispersion are required after SHS, which increase
the preparation cost and steps. On the contrary, SCS begins with an aqueous
solution, which guarantees a molecular-level mixing of reactants. As a result, SCS
serves as a promising route for rapid and direct synthesis of nanomaterials, espe-
cially for oxides, due to its simplicity, energy- and time-effectiveness, and low cost.
Controllability over the ﬁnal product phases and morphologies is the general difﬁ-
culty encountered in SCS, which is the result of the rapid and uncontrollable
combustion procedure (Wen and Jin-Ming 2014). This synthesis method starts
36                                                             M. Fatemi and Z. Bahrami


with dehydration and thermal decomposition of the homogeneous solution and
involves several thermally coupled exothermic reactions, which result in the forma-
tion of at least one solid product and a large amount of gas. It is worth nothing that
this process allows for the synthesis of a variety of nanomaterials, including oxides,
metals, alloys, and sulﬁdes, which can be used in many important applications
(Varma et al. 2016). SCS systems are classiﬁed based on the chemical composition
of fuel, oxidizer, and solvent. Various types of organic fuels or their mixtures are
typically dissolved in a solvent with metal nitrate hydrates as oxidizer. The preva-
lence of this class of oxidizer is probably explained by their good solubility in water
and relatively low decomposition temperature that leads to the formation of active
oxygen. Organic fuels such as urea, glycine, sucrose, and citric acid are the source of
carbon and hydrogen and, in many cases, form complexes with metal ions, thus
facilitating homogeneous mixing of cations in solutions or gels. The ideal fuel
should have high solubility in the solvent and low decomposition temperature
(below 400 °C), yield no other residual mass, be compatible with metal nitrates
(reaction should not lead to explosion), and be readily available or easy to prepare. In
most works related to SCS, water is used as a solvent. However, occasionally
kerosene, alcohols, or formaldehyde are also utilized (Varma et al. 2016).
Masoudpanah et al. investigated the role of fuel on morphologies of ZnO via
solution combustion route. Poly(vinyl-pyrrolidone) (PVP) was used as fuel at
different ratios to oxidant. The increase in fuel led to the transformation of giant
pyramid-like particles to hexagonal nano-disk ones. N2 adsorption–desorption
isotherms showed that the speciﬁc surface area increased from 23 to 41 m2/g at
the higher fuel contents. By considering the decrease in particle size, the band gap
energy value was turned from 3.02 to 3.13 eV (Vasei et al. 2020). Nanocrystalline
MgO particles were prepared through combustion method using magnesium nitrate
as oxidizer and hexamine as a fuel. The materials obtained by combustion method
were subsequently annealed at 800 °C for 3 h to improve the crystallinity and phase
purity. The cubic crystal structure with average crystalline size of 22 nm was
obtained for the MgO NPs. The SEM micrographs showed that the MgO
nanomaterials are seemingly porous and highly agglomerated with ﬁne particles
(Balamurugan et al. 2014). Copper oxide NPs were produced using the solution
combustion technique with ascorbic acid as the fuel. The synthesized NPs were
characterized by XRD, SEM, FT-IR, and UV–Vis spectrophotometer. The XRD
results conﬁrmed the monoclinic crystal phase. FT-IR absorption exhibited the
existence of metal oxygen stretching bond at 422 cm-1. SEM results showed that
the particles were highly porous in nature with irregular shape. The band gap was
found to be 1.65 eV from UV–Vis studies (Harini et al. 2022). A facile method for
synthesis of ZnO NPs by rapidly heating a paste of zinc nitrate and sucrose on the hot
plate at 500 °C was reported by Islam et al. (2019). In detail, the desired amounts of
sucrose and Zn(NO3)26H2O salt were dissolved in distilled water. The beaker was
put on the preheated hot plate. Within 1–2 min, the mixture turned into a viscous
solution and immediately after that the viscous solution started to decompose and
generated brownish yellow gases. After 3–4 min, a blackish brown foam was
obtained from the decomposition of zinc nitrate salt. Afterward, the blackish
2   Synthesis of Different Types, Shapes, and Sizes of Nanocarriers. . .        37


brown hot foam/powder was transferred from the beaker to the same bare hot plate.
The blackish powder turned into yellowish-white powder of ZnO NPs. The trans-
mission electron microscopy images revealed the spherical shape of the NPs with an
average size of 35 nm. The band gap and the speciﬁc surface area of the ZnO were
measured to be ~3.32 eV and 80.11 m2/g, respectively (Islam et al. 2019).

2.1.1.2.5 Sonochemical Synthesis
High intensity ultrasound can be used for the production of novel materials.
Sonochemistry originates from the extreme transient conditions induced by ultra-
sound, which produces unique hot spots that can achieve temperatures above
5000 K, pressures exceeding 1000 atmospheres, and heating and cooling rates in
excess of 1010 K s-1. Several phenomena are responsible for sonochemistry and in
speciﬁc the production or modiﬁcation of nanomaterials during ultrasonic irradia-
tion. The most notable effects are consequences of acoustic cavitation (the forma-
tion, growth, and implosive collapse of bubbles) and can be categorized as primary
sonochemistry (gas-phase chemistry occurring inside collapsing bubbles), secondary
sonochemistry (solution-phase chemistry occurring outside the bubbles), and physi-
cal modiﬁcations (caused by high-speed jets or shock waves derived from bubble
collapse) (see Fig. 2.3) (Xu et al. 2013).
   Fe3O4 NPs can easily be synthesized from an inorganic iron precursor through
decomposing iron salts using a sonochemical method via very high pressures and
temperatures generated by ultrasonic irradiation. Due to the strong inﬂuence of NPs
size on magnetic and structural properties of Fe3O4 NPs, the size selection propor-
tional to the desired magnetization is very signiﬁcant (Ali Dheyab et al. 2021).
Synthesis of Fe3O4 NPs with ethylenediamine as a novel precipitating agent by
ultrasonic treatment (40 kHz, 150 W) was reported by Boustani et al. (2020).
Different parameters such as the effect of reaction temperature, reaction time, and
precipitating agent were investigated. It was demonstrated that ethylenediamine




Fig. 2.3 Primary and secondary sonochemistry for preparation of nanomaterials
38                                                            M. Fatemi and Z. Bahrami


leads to the synthesis of larger magnetite NPs with the desired saturation magnetiza-
tion (Ms). In another study, monodisperse magnetite nanocubes with a uniform
particle size of about 80 nm were synthesized in an aqueous medium by single
reaction sonochemical method using inexpensive and nontoxic metal salt (FeSO4
7H2O) as a reactant. The crystallinity of the magnetite nanocube enhanced by
annealing treatment up to 600 °C with uniform shape and size distribution. The
magnetic characterization of the NPs revealed saturation magnetization of 91 emu/g
at 5 K for the as-synthesized sample and 94.8 emu/g for the sample annealed at the
temperature of 600 °C in a vacuum chamber (Abbas et al. 2013). Due to the high
magnetization, these NPs can be used for various applications, such as MRI and drug
delivery (Ali Dheyab et al. 2021). In another work a pH-triggered release system
based on core@shell mesoporous magnetic nanoparticles (MNP@mSiO2) obtained
using a simple and rapid ultrasound-assisted method was reported. A solution
containing MnSO4 and FeSO4 in deionized water was prepared. The prepared
solution was sonicated (130 W, 20 kHz). After 10 min of continuous irradiation,
10 mL of NaOH 2N solution was poured into the reactor, and the appearance of a
dark brown color indicated the formation of magnetic nanoparticles. The coating of
silica was performed based on the hydrolysis and condensation of TEOS. Performed
characterizations revealed magnetic cores of Fe2.9Mn0.1O4 (38 ± 6 nm) with speciﬁc
loss power values adequate for hyperthermia (463 W/g) were surrounded by a
hydroxyl group (–OH) functionalized mesoporous silica shell (10 ± 2 nm) with a
large surface area (269 m2 g-1). MNP@mSiO2 were loaded with DOX and amino-
silane groups, providing pH-triggered DOX release at acidic media, driven by
dipolar intermolecular interactions (Fuentes-García et al. 2021).

2.1.1.2.6 Green Synthesis
Another approach for producing metal oxide NPs is the green synthesis method.
Numerous studies have addressed this approach, and some of the most recent
ﬁndings are summarized in Table 2.2.

2.1.1.3 Polymeric NPs
Polymeric NPs are solid colloidal particles that span sizes from 10 to 1000 nm
(1 μm). They are widely utilized due to their exceptional characteristics and high
surface areas. These nanoparticles competently take drugs, DNA, and proteins to
their target cells or organs. They are generally synthesized from preformed polymers
or by direct polymerization of monomers. Regardless of the synthesis method, the
ﬁnal products are obtained as aqueous colloidal suspensions. The methods used to
synthesize these NPs from preformed polymers are emulsiﬁcation-solvent evapora-
tion, salting-out, dialysis, ionic gelation, and solvent emulsiﬁcation-diffusion. Con-
versely, they can be synthesized directly by polymerization of monomers using
various polymerization techniques such as microemulsion, nanoemulsion,
surfactant-free emulsion, and interfacial polymerization (Castro et al. 2022; Khan
et al. 2023). The most important technique for synthesis of polymeric NPs is the
microemulsion method, which will be discussed in the following.

2.1.1.3.1 Microemulsion Method
The term microemulsion applies to a mixture with at least three components: an oily
phase, an aqueous phase, and a surface-active species, which is called surfactant.
Sometimes the fourth component, i.e., co-surfactant can/must be present. Depending
on the ratios between the components, in the two extremes, the microstructure of the
microemulsions varies from very tiny water droplets dispersed in the oil phase (w/o
microemulsion) to oil droplets dispersed in the water phase (o/w microemulsion).
The microstructure of the mixture changes continuously from one to another
extreme, namely from a spherical to cylindrical, tubular, and interconnected contin-
uous oil and water phases separated with a very thin layer of surfactant molecules, in
the middle, which is deﬁned as a continuous microemulsion. The microemulsions of
each kind are thermodynamically stable and transparent solutions. There are main
differences between emulsions and microemulsions in terms of structure and stabil-
ity (Asgari et al. 2019; Najjar 2012).
    Surfactants have a critical role in microemulsion formation. They are molecules
with at least two parts: one part soluble in polar solvents (hydrophilic) and the other
part insoluble in the polar solvent (hydrophobic). Because of this double character,
the term amphiphile is also used for surfactants. The polar part of the surfactant
molecule is referred to as the head, and the non-polar part of the molecule as the tail.
Having these two parts with opposing solubilization abilities gives the surfactant
molecules unique capabilities, such as the tendency to adsorb at the surfaces and
interfaces, which results in the decrease in the surface tension, and also formation of
the aggregates inside the solutions, resulting in the formation of the microemulsions.
40                                                             M. Fatemi and Z. Bahrami


This double character of the surfactant molecule enables it to orient in the desired
way while in contact with the two phases with different hydro/lipophilic properties,
or to make aggregates inside of the solution with hydro- or lipophilic parts directed
toward the media. Such aggregates can solubilize an oil in the aqueous phase
(micelles) or water in the oily phase (reversed micelles). Usually, many surfactants
can be used to form microemulsion, including cationic surfactants such as
cetyltrimethylammonium bromide (CTAB), anionic surfactants such as bis
(2-ethylhexyl) sulfosuccinate (AOT), sodium dodecyl benzene sulfonate (SDBS)
and lauryl sodium sulfate (SDS), and nonionic surfactants such as Triton X-100 and
sorbitan monooleate Span 80, nonylphenyl ether (NP-5) or polyoxyethylene
(9) nonylphenyl ether (NP-9) (Asgari et al. 2019; Najjar 2012). With this introduc-
tion in mind, subsequent section presents some examples of the latest works done on
synthesis of polymeric NPs by microemulsion method. Using reverse
microemulsion method and zoledronic acid (Zol) and calcium as model
bisphosphonate and metal molecules, respectively, Cui et al. prepared DOPA
(an anionic lipid)-coated Zol-Ca nanocomposites (Zol-Ca@DOPA). Results showed
that these composites are spherical, around 15 nm in size, monodispersed, and
uniform. Based on such Zol-Ca@DOPA nanocomposites, developing a novel nano-
particle formulation for targeting delivery of Zol to subcutaneous tumors in order to
explore the potential of using the Zol to directly inhibit tumor cell growth in bone
diseases is possible (Li et al. 2017). In another study, Luo et al. prepared
1-hexadecyl-3-methylimidazolium bromide C16mimBr/butan-1 ol/cyclohexane/
water ionic liquid microemulsion. They studied the effects of n-alkyl alcohols,
alkanes, water content and temperature on the properties of microemulsion by
dilution experiment. The microregion of microemulsion was identiﬁed by pseudo-
ternary phase diagram and conductivity measurement. Then starch NPs were
synthesized by water in oil (W/O) microemulsion-cross-linking methods with
C16mimBr as surfactant. Synthesis of starch NPs with a mean diameter of
94.3 nm and narrow particle size distribution (SD = 3.3) was conﬁrmed by dynamic
light scattering (DLS). Scanning electron microscope (SEM) micrographs revealed
that starch NPs were spherical granules with a size of ~60 nm. In addition, the results
of Fourier transform infrared spectroscopy (FT-IR) and X-ray diffraction (XRD)
veriﬁed the formation of cross-linking bonds in starch molecules. Finally, the drug
loading and releasing properties of starch NPs were investigated with methylene
blue (MB) as a drug model (Wang et al. 2016). In the work done by Zhao et al.
bisphosphonate functionalized coordination polymer nanoparticles (CPNs) were
prepared by W/O microemulsion for the treatment of bone metastatic breast cancer.
CPNs are a class of burgeoning materials which are self-assembled from metal ions
and organic bridging ligands. They have shown a great potential in drug delivery
systems, because of their tunable compositions, sizes, shapes, biological compatibil-
ity, stability in physiological environments, and intrinsic biodegradability. In this
study, the particle size was regulated by adjusting the volume ratio of water phase to
oil phase in microemulsion. Herein, the ultra-small CPNs, ~55 nm were used for
enhancing the targeting efﬁciency of nanoparticles by regulating its sizes (He et al.
2017). Luo et al. used a [C3OHmim]Ac/TX-100 + 1-butanol/cyclohexane ionic
2   Synthesis of Different Types, Shapes, and Sizes of Nanocarriers. . .            41


liquid microemulsion cross-linking technique which allowed the formation of spher-
ical octenyl succinic anhydride (OSA) starch-based nanoparticles with concentrated
size distribution and nanosize diameters. This was done by tailor-made polar phase
[C3OHmim]Ac with high dissolving capacity that was conducive to the uniform
distribution of starch molecules, and composite surfactant TX-100/1-butanol that
lowered the interfacial tension much more than the traditional surfactants. Regarding
the drug loading properties of starch nanoparticles, it was found that the drug loading
and encapsulation efﬁciency were inﬂuenced by loading time, loading temperature,
and particle size as well as drug concentration. OSA-modiﬁed starch nanoparticles as
drug carriers showed an increased initial release rate and total release amount in the
release experiment of indomethacin (Qi et al. 2017).


2.1.2     1D Nanomaterials

1D nanomaterials possess unique shape-dependent properties and can be classiﬁed,
according to their shapes, to nanorods, nanotubes, nanowires, self-assembled
nanochains, etc. The biological characteristics of these nanomaterials, including
high drug loading efﬁciency, prolonged blood circulation, the ability to capture
cancer cells, unique cellular uptake mechanisms, efﬁcient photothermal conversion,
and material tunability have aided in extending their potential to biomedical
applications, particularly in cancer therapy and diagnosis (Shin et al. 2023). Consid-
erable research has been conducted on new routes to controllable synthesis of 1D
nanomaterials. Template synthesis is the most considerable method for this goal
which we will discuss it in the following sections.

2.1.2.1 Template Synthesis Method
Template synthesis is a very effective method for synthesis of nanomaterials that has
been widely used in recent years. Synthesis using a pre-existing nanostructured
template is one of the most effective strategies for achieving high degrees of
synthetic control. Typically, nanomaterials synthesized by templating strategies
hold a well-deﬁned size, shape, and conﬁguration, which usually beneﬁts from the
directing effect of the templates. The general route for the template synthesis of
nanostructured materials includes the following steps: (1) template preparation,
(2) directed synthesis of target materials using the template, and (3) template
removal (if necessary). In nanoscale synthesis, theoretically, a template can be any
substance with nanostructured features. Thus, there are abundant types of materials
that can be taken into consideration. Some are naturally occurring (such as
nanostructured minerals, biological molecular assemblies, and organs), while others
are sourced from synthetic nanostructures which usually beneﬁt from pre-existing
synthesis methods (both direct synthesis and template synthesis), including
top-down and bottom-up approaches. Their physical properties can vary greatly,
from soft materials such as molecular assemblies to hard materials such as colloidal
particles, porous membranes, and patterned solid surfaces. With regard to their
chemical properties, they can either behave as a pure physical directing agent,
42                                                               M. Fatemi and Z. Bahrami


which we refer to as a physical template, or as a physical director which is also
involved in a chemical transformation during the synthesis, which we refer to as a
chemical template. For the synthesis of target materials, synthetic strategies based on
physical templates include a variety of methods such as surface coating and channel
replication. In cases of chemical template synthesis, chemical transformations during
the synthesis may include isomerization, addition, substitution, and elimination of
chemical species. As a result, nearly all types of materials can be utilized to form 1D
nanomaterials. In order to remove the template after synthesis, as long as the
physical and chemical properties of the resultant materials can be preserved, both
physical methods such as dissolution, and chemical methods including calcination
and etching can be applied according to the nature of the templates (Liu et al. 2013b;
Xie et al. 2016). The hard template is a rigid material, whose stable structure directly
determines the size and morphology of the sample particle. A wide choice of hard
templates is available, such as polymer microspheres, porous membranes, plastic
foam, ion exchange resin, carbon ﬁber, and porous anodic aluminum oxide (AAO).
Because of their special structures and the effect on particle size restriction, they play
an important role in many ﬁelds. The soft template does not have a ﬁxed rigid
structure. Common soft templates are surfactants, polymers, and biopolymers. The
soft template has broad prospects for development in the synthesis of 1D
nanomaterials because of advantages such as good repeatability and simplicity of
the process (Li et al. 2022; Xie et al. 2016). As an example, Zhao et al. clariﬁed the
template-induced mechanism for hydroxyapatite (HAp) nanomaterials synthesized
using a three-phase reaction system. HAP nanocrystals were initially grown via an
emulsion-hydrothermal synergistic strategy in an aliphatic-ethanol (EtOH)-water
three-phase mixture system. Four kinds of eighteen-carbon (C18) aliphatic
amphiphiles were used including stearic acid, oleic acid, stearyl alcohol, and oleyl
alcohol. The two fatty acids both formed entangled long wormlike micelle
structures, and the fatty alcohols formed globular or ellipsoidal micelles in the
mixture solution. Ultra-long HAp nanowires were successfully fabricated using
fatty acids and nanorods were obtained using stearyl alcohol (Zhao et al. 2022). In
another work, monodisperse SiO2 nanorods with hollow structure were prepared
using FeOOH as a hard template. With a FeCl3 concentration of 0.1M, the average
length and width of the prepared hollow SiO2 nanorods were 397 nm and 116 nm,
respectively, and the average length-diameter ratio was equal to 3.4. The average
size of the nanopores of the amorphous SiO2 shell was measured as about 2.5
nm. Increasing the concentration of Cl ions improved length-diameter ratios of the
FeOOH nanorod template and hollow SiO2 nanorods (Yang et al. 2021). Pure silica
nanotubes with controlled diameter and length were also synthesized using a novel
and modiﬁed template-sol-gel method. The consistency and order of silica nanotubes
mainly depended on the AAO template that was used during the preparation process.
The AAO membrane was chemical etched using different concentrations of phos-
phoric acid. The obtained silica nanotubes had diameters in the range of 220–280 nm
and thicknesses about 70–90 nm. Scanning transmission electron microscopy
(STEM) images showed the arrangement of the hollow cylindrical silica nanotubes
(Ananth et al. 2016). Hu et al. successfully synthesized, for the ﬁrst time,
2   Synthesis of Different Types, Shapes, and Sizes of Nanocarriers. . .           43


mesoporous TiO2 nanotubes (m-TiO2-NTs) through a dual-template method. This
method used multi-walled carbon nanotube (CNT) as a hard template to control the
morphology and cetyltrimethylammonium bromide (CTAB) as a soft template to
form the mesoporous structure. The m-TiO2-NTs had a multiple channel tubular
structure and higher speciﬁc surface area than TiO2 nanoparticles (TiO2-NPs)
(He et al. 2019). In a study by Zeng et al., silver nanowires (Ag NWs) were
successfully synthesized using a polyvinylpyrrolidone (PVP)-free hydrothermal
method with an Alpinia zerumbet leaf chunk as a reducing agent and template.
The Ag NWs had a diameter of ~77 nm and a length of ~10 μm. During the
hydrothermal process, the biomass initially served as a reducing agent in order to
reduce silver ions. As the reaction proceeded, the biomass formed a pipe-shaped soft
template by hydrothermal carbonization. Silver ions were adsorbed and reduced
along the pipe-shaped soft templates to form silver nanorods, and adjacent nanorods
were merged to Ag NWs. Thus, Ag NWs were grown along the pipeline soft
template based on the oriented attachment mechanism (Li et al. 2023). Apart from
the template synthesis method, there are also other routes for synthesis of 1D
nanomaterials that are summarized in Table 2.3.


2.1.3     2D Nanomaterials

The biomedical applications of 2D nanomaterials such as graphene-based materials,
including drug delivery, have grown rapidly in the past few years. Graphene and
graphene oxide (GO) have been extensively explored as some of the most promising
materials for biomedical applications due to their unique properties like
two-dimensional planar structure, large surface area, chemical and mechanical
stability, superb conductivity, and good biocompatibility. These properties result
in potential applications for the design of advanced drug delivery systems and
delivery of a broad range of therapeutics. Despite these, graphene is challenging to
employ in biomedical applications due to its high hydrophobic nature; therefore, its
derivatives, like graphene oxide are frequently utilized as preferable materials. GO
possesses many oxygen-based moieties compared to graphene, namely epoxy and
hydroxyl groups on the sheet’s basal plane and carbonyl, carboxyl, hydroxyl,
lactone, and phenol structures toward the sheet edge (Liu et al. 2013a). The synthesis
of GO can essentially be divided into two main categories: “bottom-up” methods
where simple carbon molecules are used to construct pristine graphene, and “top-
down” methods where layers of graphene derivatives are extracted from a carbon
source, typically graphite. It has been shown that bottom-up synthesis techniques
(such as chemical vapor deposition, epitaxial growth on silicon carbide wafers, etc.)
is time-consuming and faces challenges to scalability (Smith et al. 2019).
    The most common GO synthesis method is the "Hummer method.” In this
method oxidation of graphite using the concentrated sulfuric acid with potassium
permanganate and sodium nitrate as catalysts leads to the synthesis of graphene
oxide. It was demonstrated that the oxidation using sodium nitrate would emit toxic
gasses such as NO2 and N2O4. Therefore, the replacing use of NaNO3 with H2SO4,

H3PO4, and double the amount of KMnO4 was proposed in the improved Hummer’s
method. In addition, the obtained GO has more hydrophilicity and conductivity
(Zaaba et al. 2017). Gu et al. reported a new graphene-based co-delivery nanosystem
of a cell-membrane-targeted anticancer protein and a chemotherapeutic agent for
combination cancer treatment. This designed nanocarrier was composed of graphene
oxide (GO), polyethylene glycol (PEG) linker, and a furin cleavable peptide, which
was achieved by step-by-step site-speciﬁc conjugations. For carboxylation of GO,
sodium hydroxide, sodium chloroacetate (ClCH2COONa), and HCl were used step
by step under ultrasonication in an ice-water bath. A nanosized GO sheet was
obtained after ultrasonication and puriﬁcation, which had an average size of
152 nm. Carboxylated GO functionalized by PEG and peptide and used as a carrier
for DOX delivery (Jiang et al. 2015). In another work, Cao et al. reported the
synthesis of near-infrared (NIR)-responsive PEGylated graphene oxide incorporated
with core-shell gold nanorods/mesoporous silica nanoparticles. The modiﬁed Hum-
mer method was used for the fabrication of graphene oxide. The core-shell
nanoparticles were obtained in two steps: seed-mediated method and hydrolysis of
TEOS via sol-gel. The nanocarriers were fabricated by adding core-shell
2   Synthesis of Different Types, Shapes, and Sizes of Nanocarriers. . .            45


nanoparticles into PEGylated graphene oxide suspension in a dropwise manner after
stirring for 24 h at room temperature (Qi et al. 2019). Other 2D nanomaterials that
can be used for drug delivery are nanosheets. Synthesis of folic acid-functionalized
polyethylene glycol coated ZnO nanosheet was reported by Kannan et al. for the ﬁrst
time. The PEG-ZnO nanosheet was fabricated by Borassus ﬂabellifer fruit extract via
green synthesis at 160 °C for 6–7 h. The surface functionalization of the ZnO
nanosheet was carried out using folic acid, 1-ethyl-3-(3-(dimethylamino) propyl)-
carbodiimide, and N-hydroxysuccinimide in DMSO as a solvent. The average size
of the functionalized nanosheet was about 100 nm (Vimala et al. 2017). Molybde-
num disulﬁde (MoS2) has been extensively used for biomedical applications due to
its excellent photothermal conversion ability. In the work done by Williams et al.,
they reported a nanoplatform based on folic acid (FA) targeted dual-stimuli respon-
sive MoS2 nanosheets and explored this for the treatment of FA-receptor positive
human breast cancer. MoS2 was exfoliated from MoS2 ﬂakes in hexane under a N2
atmosphere and ultrasonication. The nanosheets had a uniform diameter (133 nm)
and could be loaded with the anticancer drug doxorubicin (DOX) to a high capacity
(151.4 mg/g). The release of DOX was greatly accelerated at pH 5.0 as compared to
pH 7.4 (Zhang et al. 2019).


2.1.4     3D Nanomaterials

Different types of 3D nanostructures such as aerogels, mesoporous silica materials,
metal-organic frameworks, ﬂower-like structures, and nanocomposites can be used
as drug release systems. Generally, by changing the effective parameters in common
synthetic methods such as sol-gel, polyol, hydrothermal, etc. such structures can be
produced. Sometimes, a combination of different synthetic methods is used.
Table 2.4 represents a summary of some widely used routes for the synthesis of
3D nanomaterials. As an example, Moghaddas et al. reported the synthesis of
carboxylic acid-functionalized silica aerogel nanostructure as a carrier for celecoxib
delivery. The simple ambient pressure drying method was used for preparing
hydrophobic silica aerogel. The silica sol was obtained using sodium silicate solu-
tion and tartaric acid as the starting materials at pH 4–5. The gelation of the sol was
carried out in the sealed reactor at 55 °C. Water was exchanged with 2-propanol and
n-hexane in the ﬁnal gel. The silica aerogel was prepared after calcination at 500 °C
for 3 h. The carboxylic acid functionalization was done by N-(2-aminoethyl)-3
aminopropyltrimethoxysilane and succinic anhydride at 60 °C for 24 h. Silica
aerogel possessed uniform nanoparticles in a porous nanostructure with an average
size of 24 ± 4 nm and 37 ± 5 nm before and after functionalization, respectively
(Jabbari-Gargari et al. 2021).

Synthesis of Nanocarriers Loaded
with Anti-Cancer Drugs by Using                                                                3
Functionalization or Conjugations,
Encapsulation Methods

Vijaya Ravinayagam and B. Rabindran Jermy



   Abstract

   Recently, inorganic porous nanocomposites are gaining importance in biomedical
   applications. This is due to several advantages such as high surface area,
   designable pore structures, and robust mechanical/chemical stability with
   adjustable optical and electrical properties. Inorganic nanoparticles are interesting
   as nanocarriers for drug delivery due to the chemical, mechanical strength,
   microbial resistance, and biocompatibility. The presence of large surface area,
   structured pores of tunable sizes, ability to functionalize with different chain
   length of chemical moieties has been an attractive feature of nanoparticles in
   nanotherapeutic area of research. Drug loading on porous structured silica
   requires ambient condition that minimize the drug disintegration. An effective
   nanocarrier needs to exhibit both spatial placement (target speciﬁcity) and tem-
   poral delivery (sustainability and controllability). In this chapter we have
   discussed several nanocarriers based on structured silica, metal oxides, carbon,
   liposomes, nucleic acids, and polymeric species based therapeutic tools has been
   reported for treating chronic diseases such as cancer, cardiovascular, and
   diabetics. In addition, hexagonal shaped mesoporous silica (SiM41 and
   SBA-15), cubic shaped mesosilica (SBA-16 and MCM-48) with high textural
   properties are reported for multifunctional drug delivery applications are also
   discussed.

     Keywords

     Nanocarriers loaded with anti-cancer drugs · Functionalization · Conjugations ·
     Encapsulation methods


3.1       Introduction

Engineering a stimuli responsive drug delivery system offers a controlled drug
release, improves the therapeutic efﬁciency, and reduces the drug dose. Three
class of materials explored are classiﬁed into organic (Dendrimer, micelles,
polymers, and liposomes), inorganic (mesoporous silica, metal oxides, graphene
oxide, carbon nanotubes), and biological systems (Viosomes, extracellular vesicles)
(Tian et al. 2022). Recently, inorganic porous nanocomposites are gaining impor-
tance in biomedical applications. This is due to several advantages such as high
surface area, designable pore structures, and robust mechanical/chemical stability
with adjustable optical and electrical properties (Khakpour et al. 2023). Inorganic
nanoparticles are interesting as nanocarriers for drug delivery due to the chemical,
mechanical strength, microbial resistance, and biocompatibility. The presence of
large surface area, structured pores of tuneable sizes, ability to functionalize with
different chain length of chemical moieties has been an attractive feature of
nanoparticles in nanotherapeutic area of research. Drug loading on porous structured
silica requires ambient condition that minimize the drug disintegration. An effective
nanocarrier needs to exhibit both spatial placement (target speciﬁcity) and temporal
delivery (sustainability and controllability) (Di Stefano 2023). Several nanocarriers
based on structured silica, metal oxides, carbon (Debnath and Srivastava 2021),
liposomes, nucleic acids (Guo et al. 2022), and polymeric species based therapeutic
tools has been reported for treating chronic diseases such as cancer, cardiovascular,
and diabetics (Li et al. 2019). Hexagonal shaped mesoporous silica (SiM41 and
SBA-15), cubic shaped mesosilica (SBA-16 and MCM-48) with high textural
properties (surface area ranging between 500 and 1100 m2/g) are reported for
multifunctional drug delivery applications. The nanotherapeutics transformed from
single drug delivery system to multifunctional system (Fig. 3.1).
    Amidoxime ligand modiﬁed mesoporous SBA-15 as nanocarrier platform has been
reported for doxorubicin drug loading and release (Fig. 3.2). The grafting of
amidoxime on the surface of SBA-15 had little impact on the morphology of
nanocarrier, while increases the binding functional moieties such as amine and
hydroxyl groups to facilitate effective interaction with DOX. The presence of such
reactive functional moieties enables a pH responsive drug delivery and effective
against MCF-7 cells for potential application in cancer therapy (Ulagesan et al.
2022). Atiyah et al. (2022) reported a comparative study of curcumin loading on
SBA-15 and amine (aminopropyltrimethoxysilane) functionalized SBA-15. Several
parameters such as nanocarrier concentrations, curcumin concentration, contact
time, and pH on curcumin loadings were studied. The curcumin loading efﬁciency
almost doubled from 45% to 89.7% with amine functionalization on SBA-15.
3   Synthesis of Nanocarriers Loaded with Anti-Cancer Drugs by. . .             55




Fig. 3.1 Nanoparticles and their classiﬁcations (Seidu et al. 2022)


Kinetics study revealed a higher correlation coefﬁcient using Langmuir isotherm.
Drug delivery study showed that SBA-15 exhibits high curcumin release of 82.6%,
while amine functionalization regulates the curcumin in a sustained manner to lower
release percentage of 41.2%. Three-dimensional porous SBA-16 silica loaded with
biocompatible ZnO was reported to be an effective carrier for chemo drug
temozolomide. Characterization of nanoformulation using various techniques
shows the deposition of nanosized ZnO alongside retainment of mesoporous
characteristics with high percentage of temozolomide loading (Fekri et al. 2022).
SBA-16 functionalized with silane coupling agent 3-aminopropyltriethoxysilane was
found to be effective for drug delivery of nonsteroidal anti-inﬂammatory drug
indomethacin (C19H16ClNO4). Importantly, the wrapping of polymer gelatin and
glutaraldehyde through condensation process exhibited a pH release behavior
corresponding to the pH environment of colon cancer (Yu et al. 2023).
   Three-dimensional Ia3d cubic structured silica designated as Korea Advanced
Institute of Science and Technology-6 material (KIT-6) containing guanidine and
curcumin has been reported for potential breast cancer treatment. In the ﬁrst step,
KIT-6 was hydrothermally synthesized using Pluronic P123 template. After calcina-
tion, the sample was pegylated (20%) and treated with aminopropyltriethyoxysilane
1-H-pyrazole-1-carboxamidine hydrochloride and diisopropyl ethylamine to form
25% guanidine (~2.0 mmol/g). Then acetone containing curcumin was added under
inert atmosphere, rinsed with excess curcumin and dried. The ﬁnal loading of
curcumin was found to be 1.67 mmol/g. The KIT-6 based formulation exhibited
high loading capacity (50%), efﬁciency (100%) and colloidal stability with zeta
potential value 24 ± 5 mV. Textural analysis using nitrogen adsorption technique
56                                                              V. Ravinayagam and B. R. Jermy




Fig. 3.2 Different types of structured mesoporous silica with different shaped pore architectures
(Tella et al. 2022)


revealed a major change in the surface area, pore volume and pore size of KIT-6 to
ﬁnal formulation. For instance, the surface area of KIT-6 decreases from 834 m2/g to
234 m2/g, while pore volume decreases from 1.81 cm3/g to 0.219 cm3/g, respec-
tively. The nano formulation exhibited a high pH sensitive curcumin release of
82.26% at pH 5.4 than 41.19% at pH 7.4 at 96 h in comparison to raw curcumin. The
nanoformulation showed a strong cytotoxicity against MCF-7 cells (live cells of only
7.62% after 48 h) than normal cells (Ma’mani et al. 2014).
   Spherical mesoporous silica (~150 nm) functionalized with pH sensitive poly-
peptide Poly-L-Histidine/tamoxifen has been reported for effective delivery of
doxorubicin (DOX). The study relates the effect of DOX release through
pre-surface and post-surface grafting technique. In the pre-surface grafting, the
DOX was diffused into the pores of structured silica and then grafted with polypep-
tide and tamoxifen. In post-surface grafting, the silica was functionalized with the
polypeptide and tamoxifen in the ﬁrst step and followed by loaded with DOX. The
polypeptide with pKa value of 6.5 has been reported to act as an effective gate keeper
due to solubility transition (Najaﬁ Rad and Farzad 2022). At the normal pH 7.4, the
peptide condenses, block the silica pore, and safeguard the DOX. However, at the
3   Synthesis of Nanocarriers Loaded with Anti-Cancer Drugs by. . .                  57


tumor acidic pH condition of 5.0, the polypeptide gets protonated and due to
repulsive forces, an expansion occurs leading to the release of DOX. In addition,
the solubility of DOX improves due to acidic environment and targeted release
improves the therapeutic efﬁciency. The drug release study of DOX loaded silica
without modiﬁcation exhibited high release pattern (33.6% at pH 7.4) and (42.3% at
pH 5.0). In case of functionalized mesoporous silica, a less DOX release (18% at
pH 7.4) and (23% at pH 5.0) was observed (Table 3.1; Fig. 3.3). This conﬁrms the
effective gate keeper role of grafted Poly-L-Histidine (Day et al. 2021).
    AlMofty et al. (2023) has reported the targeted therapeutics based on cerium
impregnated spherical silica and halloysite nanoformulations. Cerium nanoparticles
are interesting, rare metals that show antioxidant and anti-inﬂammatory properties.
The presence of variable oxidation states such as Ce3+ and Ce4+ helps to reactive
with reactive oxygen species. Characterization tools such as diffuse reﬂectance
spectroscopy and transmission electron microscope revealed the presence of Ce3+
and Ce4+ species (particle size ~5–10 nm) at 250 nm and 300 nm, respectively. The
dual drugs (cisplatin and curcumin) functionalization was established using Fourier
transform infrared (FTIR) spectroscopy. Drug release study was performed using
dialysis membrane for 72 h. The result showed halloysite based formulation exhibits
a high release of cisplatin of about 26% than spherical silica (21.4%).
    Tumor speciﬁc MSN has been developed through conjugation with siRNA
multidrug resistant protein 1 (MRP1) and myricetin with loading efﬁciency of
93.3% and 36.7%, respectively. The presence of folic acid receptor facilitates
endocytosis via folate receptor. The nano formulation with hydrodynamic diameter
of 109.8 nm shown to improve the therapeutic efﬁciency in treating non-small cell
lung cancer. The presence of folic acid and MRP-1 facilitated apoptosis and
upregulate Caspase-3 and PARP. A reduction in the colony formation and cell
death was observed lung cancer cells (A549 and NCI-H1299). In vivo study has
indicated a tumor shrinkage from 1800 mm3 to about 450 mm3 (Yinxue et al. 2020).
    Biocompatible mesoporous silica with controlled particle size using
tri-ethanolamine has been reported to deliver DOX to targeted therapeutics of breast
cancer. For high drug loading, the surface of silica was functionalized with silane
chemistry (APTES, EDC/NHS), further modiﬁed with ﬂuorescence Rhodamine B
isothiocyanate and cancer drug Trastuzumab. The surface functionalization led to
high loading of DOX (57.4%) and exhibited therapeutic effect on breast cancer cells
HER2 overexpressed SKBR3 (Mozafarinia et al. 2021). Glutathione responsive
MSN linked with disulﬁde bonds was shown to load DOX effectively along with
photosensitizer chlorine e6. The wrapping with biocompatible carboxymethyl
chitosan on the nano formulation has shown to exhibit particle monodispersion,
robust dimensional stability, and anti-cancer efﬁcacy against MCF-7 cells. The
photodynamic therapy of nano formulation well exhibited with laser irradiation (<
660 nm). An effective antitumor effect was observed both in in vitro and in vivo
(Zhu et al. 2022). Glycosylated-MSN with prodrug capecitabine capable of deliver-
ing 5-ﬂuorouracil has been reported with high textural characteristics (particle size of
245.24 nm, pore diameter of 8.12 nm and pore volume of 0.73 cm3/g). Surface
modiﬁcation with capping (chitosan-glucuronic acid) and receptor (lectin)

demonstrated high pH stimuli prodrug release and uptake in HCT 116 cells. In vivo
study revealed a tumor shrinkage and reduction of clusters of abnormal tube-like
glands (aberrant crypt foci), abnormality in cells and inﬂammation (Narayan et al.
2021). Triple responsive MSN to delivery DOX was reported using disulﬁde bond
interaction with polycaprolactone modiﬁed non-ionic copolymer Pluronic F127
micelle PEO100-PPO65-PEO100 (thermos sensitive gatekeeper). Glutathione stimuli
assists the uncapping of micelle from MSN, while the tumor acidic pH combined
with hyperthermia (40 °C) facilitated the quick release of drug (DOX) for anti-cancer
activity (Long et al. 2022). In a similar stance, the presence of disulﬁde bond
incorporated into organosilica bound mesoporous nanoparticles exhibited a highly
targeted therapeutic efﬁcacy of DOX. Glutathione-S-transferase fused cell-speciﬁc
afﬁbody at the external surface and presence of DOX at the internal pores facilitated
prompt disintegration and release of DOX (Yang et al. 2023) (Fig. 3.4).


3.1.1         Functionalized Graphene Oxide

3.1.1.1 Conjugation and Functionalization of Graphene Oxide Based
          Nanocarriers
Graphene oxide (GO), a two-dimensional nanomaterial in the oxygenated form has
attracted huge interest in the ﬁeld of biomedical applications (Fig. 3.5). The presence
62                                                              V. Ravinayagam and B. R. Jermy




Fig. 3.4 Types of functionalization available for liposome, mesoporous silica, and dendrimer
using different chemical moieties (Seidu et al. 2022)




Fig. 3.5 Graphene oxide for targeted anti-cancer applications


of hydrophobic large surface area and noncovalent interactions (π-π stacking) can be
exploited for hydrophobic drug encapsulation, loading, and controlled drug delivery
(Naline et al. 2022).
    Generation of oxygenation through exfoliation and oxidation process offers
exceptional scope for chemical functionalization and polymer wrapping. Further-
more, unlike liposomes and lipid nanoparticles, coupling drugs with GO was
effective due to the presence of carboxylic functional moieties at the surface of
GO.             n-(3-(dimethylamino)-propyl)-n-ethylcarbodiimide               (EDC),
n-hydroxysuccinimide (NHS), and thionyl chloride (SOCl2) can be used as coupling
agents and improve the functionalization properties. Interestingly, a certain cytotox-
icity of GO was reported to reduce signiﬁcantly by the polymer (polyethylene
glycol) wrapping around GO (Fig. 3.6).
3   Synthesis of Nanocarriers Loaded with Anti-Cancer Drugs by. . .                        63




Fig. 3.6 GO functionalization using folic acid and EDC/NHS agents (de Sousa et al. 2018)


    In the ﬁrst step, conjugation of folic acid with polyethylene glycol (FA-PEG) was
carried out. For functionalization, GO was crosslinked with EDC/NHS, then treated
with FA-PEG. Then the hydrophobic drug camptothecin was loaded on the
nanocarrier using DMSO solvent, followed by washing with methanol/deionized
water mixture (de Sousa et al. 2018). Aromatic rings of GO tend to favor the
adsorption of camptothecin via hydrophobic adsorption process and stacking
through π–π interactions between aromatic rings. The presence of folic acid reduces
the loading capacity of camptothecin from 52.3% to 37.8%, while loading efﬁciency
decreases from 53.2% to 32.6%, respectively. This shows the functionalization with
folic acid reduces the interaction of camptothecin. In the drug release study, the
nanoformulation demonstrated a pH sensitive drug release. For instance, the lactone
ring of camptothecin hydrolyze readily in normal physiological condition to carbox-
ylate form and exhibit higher release than at pH 5.0. Nevertheless, the carboxylate
form was inactive and non-toxic to tumors. In the tumor acidic pH, the carboxylate
transforms into lactone form and becomes active against cancer cells. In case of folic
acid functionalization, high camptothecin release of about 83% was observed, while
it reaches plateau after 100 h. In case of tumor pH of 5.0, a slower and sustained
release of about 48% was observed after 48 h. The release reached to about 60%
even after 100 h. Functionalization of camptothecin to GO without folic acid
strengthen the drug interaction, reducing the release to 56% in 48 h at pH 7.4. At
pH 5.0, the formulation further reduced the drug release to about 29% and 41% after
48 h and 200 h, respectively. The study indicates that GO exert a strong interaction
with drug, while folic acid functionalization triggers high drug release and promote
cell death by apoptosis (de Sousa et al. 2018).
    GO has some of the limitations including aggregation with physiological sources
(salt and proteins) and non-speciﬁc protein binding in physiological conditions.
Wrapping with hydrophilic polymer around GO have been reported to overcome
such limitation partly and also improve the drug solubility. Some of nanocarriers
reported includes pegylated MnFe2O4/GO (Almohazey et al. 2023),
3-aminopropyltriethoxysilane, folic acid, caffeic acid and chitosan wrapped GO
64                                                              V. Ravinayagam and B. R. Jermy




Fig. 3.7 GO/PEI/cisplatin/topotecan for mitochondria targeting cancer cells (Mallick et al. 2018)


(Tousian et al. 2022), GO/chitosan/dextran/DOX (Xie et al. 2016), hyperbranched
polyglycerol functionalized GO via glycidol polymerization for curcumin release
(Paknia et al. 2022), etc. Recently, biocompatible metal oxides (Fe3O4, ZnO, TiO2,
CuO) based nanocarriers have received attention for nanotherapeutic applications.
Such nanoformulations shows biodegradable character and exhibit high colloidal
stability. Wanas et al. (2023) have shown that folic acid functionalized metal oxide
such as ZnO has been effectively used in vivo tumor imaging. Functionalization of
metal oxides (ZnO or TiO2)/GO nanocomposite with cationic hydrophilic branched
polyethylenimine followed by loading of quercetin (49.39%) and curcumin (48.89%)
exhibited a pH sensitive site-speciﬁc drug release and anti-cancer activity against
breast cancer cell line (Matiyani et al. 2023). Combination of GO with cationic
hydrophilic polymer such as polyethylenimine and chemodrugs such as cisplatin and
topotecan enabled a mitochondria target speciﬁc drug delivery system. The presence
of polymer induces the positive charge and able to reach into mitochondria at 6 h
duration and generate reactive oxygen species to impart cancer cells death (Mallick
et al. 2018) (Fig. 3.7).
   GO based nano formulation conjugated with mitochondria targeting ligands and
external coverage of polyglycerol for delivery DOX. The formulation exhibited a
synergistic hyperthermia and chemotherapy effect. The presence of positive charge
prompted the cellular internalization, while near-infrared irradiation combined with
the cellular level release of DOX into inhibited both biochemical energy molecule
“adenosine triphosphate” process and cell processes governed by mitochondria
(Tu et al. 2018, 2020). Photothermal application of pegylated GO has been reported
with physiological ﬂuid stability and biocompatibility. Folic acid/pegylation/GO
3   Synthesis of Nanocarriers Loaded with Anti-Cancer Drugs by. . .                        65




Fig. 3.8 Multifunctional drug delivery system GO/Polyglycerol/DOX for multiple drug resistance
cancer treatment (Tu et al. 2020)




Fig. 3.9 Folic acid/pegylation/GO formulation for phototherapy

formulation with particle size ranging at about 30 nm ± 5 nm shown to convert the
NIR light in to heat suitable for breast cancer therapy (Figs. 3.8 and 3.9).

Nano-Drug Carriers for Chemotherapeutic
Agents Delivery in Cancer Disease                                                                  4
Treatment

Priyanca Ahlawat, Rohit Kumar, Akhilesh Kumar,
and Piyush Kumar Gupta




   Abstract

   Cancer is a leading cause of mortality in the non-communicable disease
   category. Researchers are on a constant quest to identify novel drug targets
   as well as therapeutic molecules for its treatment. However, innumerable
   chemotherapeutics that are experimentally evaluated are unable to reach bench
   to bedside due to many drawbacks. These include poor bioavailability, lower
   bioretention, and non-speciﬁc effects along with systemic toxicity. Additionally,
   these caveats lead to a long and intense treatment regimen that leads to poor
   patient compliance. Active efforts are underway to identify bioactives that do not
   fall short on these points. However, catering to such limitations, the introduction
