F. A. Khan (ed.), Nano Drug Delivery for Cancer Therapy,
https://doi.org/10.1007/978-981-99-6940-1_4
70                                                                   P. Ahlawat et al.


     of nanomedicine in the cancer chemotherapeutic regimen has opened many
     avenues and has helped in accelerating the cancer treatment. Nanomedicine has
     encompassed all the aforementioned lacunae and turned them into
     opportunities for the development of novel and versatile nanoformulations. In
     this chapter, we extensively discuss the recent advancements in the development
     of many varieties of nanosystems that have been evaluated for their anti-
     cancerous efﬁcacy and propose bright future prospects for such nanoplatforms.

     Keywords
     Nano-carriers · Chemotherapeutic agents · Drug Delivery · Cancer treatment




4.1       Introduction

Cancer is a continued world health problem that has lasted centuries. With
advancements in science and technology, the biology of cancer is being studied in
depth. The scientiﬁc community has reached a consensus that this disorder has
showcased manipulative behavior over the years, and hence its treatment has been
explored from a versatile approach. Many compounds that target all the vital cell
organelles and cell viability and proliferation mechanisms have been identiﬁed or
synthesized. Although the overall survival of cancer patients has increased, most
remain precarious and difﬁcult to treat. Oncologists have struggled to identify the
neoplasm and its metastasis to prescribe the best possible chemotherapeutic
combinations for tumor regression. The situation remains grim in the twenty-ﬁrst
century, and newer and ﬁner interventions are being sought.
   Cancer researchers have been on an unending quest to identify the ultimate
molecule or a compound that holds therapeutic efﬁcacy to destroy tumor progression
completely. Although innumerable novel drug candidates have been developed,
their certain biological shortcomings, such as bioretention, elimination, or metabo-
lism, discourage their progression through clinical trials.
   That is why so many experimentally proven compounds are sieved to reach US
FDA approval. Even the compounds that have been relatively fortunate to reach the
bench to bedside have been highly patient and non-compliant. This is because
chemotherapeutics tend to unleash systemic organ toxicities ranging from mild
manifestations like skin rashes, alopecia, and fatigue that affect the emotional and
social quotient of the patient to severe and fatal effects such as hematopoietic
suppression, thrombocytopenia, neurological issues, immunological and renal sup-
pression, among others. These major side effects restrict the continuation of the
treatment regimen and burn a hole in the pockets of cancer patients to an unknown
extent during the therapy.
   These lacunae in conventional therapy have been a cue to the emergence of
nanotechnology in cancer management and to their rescue comes the
4   Nano-Drug Carriers for Chemotherapeutic Agents Delivery in Cancer. . .          71


nanoformulations that eliminate such limitations and broaden chemotherapeutics’
prospects. Elucidation of tumor biology fundamentals immensely supported this
ﬁeld’s advancement and led to the development of a variety of nanoplatforms.
   At present, cancer therapy lacks speciﬁcity in its ability to selectively target
cellular proteins and other molecules due to the variability of these molecular
ﬁngerprints concerning the mutational state of the tumor. Nanomedicine has taken
care of the disadvantages mentioned above of chemotherapeutics in terms of the fact
that it has been developed to improve bioactive/drugs bioavailability in the systemic
circulation, especially for the hydrophobic compounds, which is provided by the
sustained and controlled release proﬁle of the encapsulated drug. Secondly, it has
selectivity over the free drugs and hence can be used as per the tumor histopathology
type. Thirdly, nanoformulations can render longer chemotherapeutic effects with
minimum side effects due to their higher mean retention time and negligible drug
leaching. Finally, they can enhance patient compliance and lower the economic
burden.
   Therefore, this chapter deals with the advancements in the ﬁeld of cancer-speciﬁc
nanomedicines. The discussion elaborates on the rationale of the nanosystem devel-
opment, the selection of experimental assays for their anticancer capacity evaluation,
and the extent to which combinatorial formulations synergistically enhance the
antiproliferative effects of the chemotherapeutics.


4.2       Lipid-Based Nanotherapy

Many pharmaceutically relevant compounds are generally insoluble or partially
water soluble. Not only that, they are physiologically and chemically unstable or
render severe systemic toxicities. Among other therapeutic bioactive compound
carriers are lipid-based nanoparticles (LBNPs). Their utilization in chemotherapeutic
cancer regimens as a drug carrier has enhanced the antitumor efﬁcacy of many
chemotherapeutics. LBNPs can be developed using naturally available lipids with
great biological stability, etc. LBNPs were widely studied against a variety of cancer,
both in vitro and in vivo, with encouraging outcomes in certain clinical trials. The
different types used are enlisted below (Fig. 4.1). Lipids play a crucial role in the
composition of cell membranes in the human body. Lipids encompass several
organic compounds, namely fatty acids, waxes, neutral fats, and steroids, including
cortisone. Compound lipids include several types of molecules, including
glycolipids, lipoproteins, and phospholipids, which are intricately associated with
other chemical entities. Lipids can be characterized as hydrophobic or amphipathic
compounds and their amphiphilic nature allows for the construction of vesicles,
massive uniﬂagellar liposomes, or a membrane. A biological lipid generally
comprises two types of building subunits: ketoacyl and isoprene groups.
72                                                                             P. Ahlawat et al.




Fig. 4.1 The overall arrangement of solid lipid NPs, which have beneﬁts over liposomes and lipid
emulsions, is represented schematically (adopted from Tekade et al. 2017)


4.2.1     Solid Lipid Nanoparticles

Innumerable studies have indicated the beneﬁcial use of solid lipid NPs (SLNs) as
drug delivery vehicles for chemotherapeutic anticancer compounds. The drug
carriers under consideration are colloidal NPs, with sizes ranging from 50 to
1000 nm. SLNs consist of a solid matrix core made up of biodegradable lipids,
which are enveloped by a hydrophilic surfactant. They have demonstrated desirable
biocompatibility and low systemic toxicity. SLNs also show sustained drug release
by lipid matrix erosion, degradation, or diffusion. Thus, they have improved the
limits of conventional anticancer compounds’ therapeutic efﬁcacy and
bioavailability.
   For example, a recent study conducted by Alajami et al. (2022) showcased the
advantages of utilizing a colon delivery system consisting of SLNs loaded with
celecoxib (CXB) to further enhanced its anticancer properties. The ultrasonic melt-
emulsiﬁcation technique was utilized in the fabrication of SLNs. The cytotoxic
effects were assessed by employing an MTT test in three cancer cell lines: HT29
(colon cancer), Daoy (medulloblastoma), and HepG2 (hepatocellular carcinoma)
cells. The utilization of SLNs resulted in a signiﬁcant enhancement of CXB activity
against a colon cancer cell line that exhibits resistance to COX-2, as well as two
additional cancer cell lines that also display resistance. This development facilitated
the administration of increased dosages of CXB without the occurrence of undesir-
able side effects associated with the COX-2 inhibitor. Therefore, it is apparent that
this particular system possesses the capacity to be transformed into a viable
4   Nano-Drug Carriers for Chemotherapeutic Agents Delivery in Cancer. . .        73


therapeutic alternative for both preventing and treating colon cancer. The anticancer
effect of andrographolide (ADG) against many cancer cell lines is extensively
acknowledged. However, the compound’s elevated hydrophobicity, coupled with
its limited bioavailability, imposes constraints on its potential therapeutic use. Li
et al. (2022) demonstrated that ADG-SLN has enhanced inhibitory efﬁcacy against
head and neck cancer and precancerous cells in comparison to free ADG. The
observed result can be related to the increased cellular uptake and intracellular
absorption of ADG-SLN. Moreover, ADG-SLN promoted cell cycle arrest and
apoptosis in HIOEC, Leuk1, HN6, and HN30 cells.
    The administration of cytotoxic drugs for brain cancer treatment is associated
with an inadvertent impact on healthy cells, owing to the nonselective nature of the
therapeutic strategy. Hence, a research investigation was undertaken to formulate
tween 80-coated SLNs containing a folic acid-doxorubicin (FAD) mixture. The
purpose of this formulation was to enable the targeted delivery of drugs to brain
cancer cells. The U87 MG (Uppsala 87 Malignant Glioma) cell line exhibited both
cytotoxic effects and internalization of SLN-C in U87 MG brain cancer cells. The
ﬁndings reafﬁrmed that SLNs coated with tween 80 demonstrate promise in selec-
tively delivering doxorubicin to brain cancer cells (Jain et al. 2022). Ahmed et al.
(2020) employed a solvent emulsiﬁcation process to fabricate SLNs loaded with
brigatinib (BG). Subsequently, their team conducted an MTT experiment utilizing
the optimized SLNs denoted as BS5. The study revealed that the BG-loaded SLNs
containing BS5 exhibited enhanced cytotoxic effects on the A549 lung cancer cell
line in comparison to both the BG solution and the blank SLN. Therefore, it was
determined that the modiﬁed BS5 formulation exhibited a comparatively higher
level of cytotoxicity and was able to achieve its effectiveness with a 74.91% lower
dosage in comparison to the drug suspension (BG) used alone. The results provide
evidence in favor of the proposition that drug delivery through sentinel lymph nodes
(SLN) might potentially augment cytotoxic effects. Another group successfully
formulated a dry powder inhaler dose form that incorporates geﬁtinib-loaded
SLNs (Gef-G-SLNs) targeted to lung cancers using glucosamine. They exhibited
the enhanced internalization of G-SLNs in A549 cells in comparison to non-targeted
SLNs using ﬂow cytometry and ﬂuorescence microscopy techniques (Satari et al.
2020).


4.2.2     Liposomes

Liposomes are nano-sized synthetic vesicles with a spherical shape that can be
developed using cholesterol and non-toxic phospholipids derived from natural
sources. Liposomes, due to their distinct size, hydrophobic and hydrophilic
properties, as well as their biocompatibility, have emerged as one of the potential
drug delivery mechanisms.
   Xu et al. (2022) conducted a study whereby they fabricate a codelivery system
with extended circulation. This system used a combination of doxorubicin (DOX)
and schizandrin A (SchA) to target MCF-7/ADR cells. The DOX-SchA long-
74                                                                     P. Ahlawat et al.


circulating liposome (DOX-SchA-Lip) was synthesized via the ammonium sulfate
gradient technique. Both drugs were simultaneously encapsulated into the distearoyl
phosphatidylethanolamine-polyethylene glycol (DSPE-mPEG2000) liposome.
The liposome showed efﬁcacy in inducing early apoptosis in HepG2/ADR cells,
and the administration of DOX-Sc resulted in a blockage of the cell cycle in the
S-phase. The efﬁcacy of DOX-SchA- DOX-SchA-Lip in inducing tumor cell death
and overcoming multidrug resistance (MDR) was demonstrated in a way that was
dependent on the dosage administered. Another research group explored the
possibilities of the liposomal delivery system against resistant cancer cells. In this
study, a liposome system was developed to simultaneously encapsulate a chemo-
therapeutic drug, namely doxorubicin hydrochloride (DOX), and a commonly used
autophagy inhibitor, namely chloroquine phosphate (CQ), at a weight ratio of 1:2.
The objective of this study was to examine the mechanism by which this liposome
system may reverse drug resistance. The results of the MTT assay revealed that
liposomes containing both DOX and CQ, when administered to DOX-resistant
human breast cancer cells (MCF7/ADR), exhibited a half-maximal inhibitory con-
centration (IC50) of 4.7 ± 0.2 μM. This value was much lower, about 5.7 times than
the IC50 observed for free DOX (26.9 ± 1.9 μM). The liposomal co-encapsulation of
DOX/CQ exhibited enhanced antitumor effectiveness in both the MCF-7/ADR
multicellular tumor spheroid model and transgenic zebraﬁsh models as compared
to the administration of liposomal DOX or DOX alone. The results of this study
indicate that the co-encapsulation of chloroquine (CQ) with doxorubicin (DOX) into
liposomes resulted in a notable enhancement of DOX sensitivity in cancer cells that
had developed resistance to DOX (Gao et al. 2017).
   In a study conducted by Zhu et al. in 2023, it was shown that Ginsenosides
Rg3-based liposomes containing Paclitaxel (referred to as Rg3-PTX-LPs) could
selectively target MCF7/T cancer cells and the tumor microenvironment (TME)
concurrently. This targeted delivery mechanism was principally facilitated by the
recognition of GLUT-1. The drug resistance reversal capacity and in vivo antitumor
efﬁcacy of Rg3-PTX-LPs exhibited notable enhancements in comparison to standard
cholesterol liposomes. The remodeling processes of Rg3-PTX-LPs inside the TME
encompass the suppression of IL-6/STAT3/p-STAT3 pathway activation, resulting
in the conversion of protumor M2 macrophages into an anticancer M1 phenotype.
Furthermore, these processes encompass the inhibition of myeloid-derived suppres-
sor cells (MDSCs), the decrease in tumor-associated ﬁbroblasts (TAFs) and collagen
ﬁbers inside the TME, and the facilitation of apoptosis in neoplastic cells. Hence, by
the concurrent targeting of tumor cells and modulation of the TME, Rg3-PTX-LPs
exhibited a noteworthy tumor inhibition rate of 90.3%.
   In another study conducted by George et al. (2022b), a polyethylene glycol-
modiﬁed nanoscale liposomal formulation (referred to as LipoRV) of a novel
anthraquinone derivative was synthesized. The study demonstrated the therapeutic
efﬁcacy of LipoRV on several triple-negative breast cancer (TNBC) cell lines. The
use of LipoRV has shown a high level of effectiveness in suppressing the progres-
sion of the cell cycle, triggering programmed cell death, and diminishing the
extended capacity for proliferation in triple-negative breast cancer (TNBC) cells.
4   Nano-Drug Carriers for Chemotherapeutic Agents Delivery in Cancer. . .           75


4.2.3     Lipid Emulsions

Lipid emulsions are generally used to enhance the therapeutic window of hydropho-
bic drugs. However, their lower retention and bioavailability restrict their wide
applications. Therefore, scientists have further explored them to form nanoemulsions
to support the purpose of cancer treatment.
    In a study conducted in 2022, Bo et al. synthesized lipid nanoemulsions (LNs)
that could simultaneously transport paclitaxel (PTX) and docosahexaenoic acid
(DHA), with the addition of folic acid (FA) as a surface adornment. They examined
the potential antitumor effects of these PTX/DHA-FA-LNs in the context of breast
cancer. The PTX/DHA-FA-lipid NPs (LNs) demonstrated a correlation between
dosage and cytotoxicity, as well as a heightened incidence of apoptosis in MCF-7
cells. The results of the cellular uptake investigation showed that the MCF-7 cells
and M2 macrophages rapidly internalized these LNs in an in vitro setting. Further-
more, the speciﬁc impact of PTX/DHA-FA-LNs was facilitated by the process of FA
receptor-mediated endocytosis, and the level of its toxicity to cells was directly
related to its effectiveness in being taken up by the cells. In 2022, Song and his group
introduced a new type of NPs called core-shell lipid-gelatin-epigallocatechin-3-
gallate hybrid NPs (LGE-N). These NPs aimed to enhance the stability and effec-
tiveness of epigallocatechin-3-gallate (EGCG) in combating tumors. The cytotoxic-
ity of the EGCG solution (EGCG-S) and LGE-N was evaluated using a conventional
3-(4,5-dimethylthiazol-2-yl)-2, 5-diphenyltetrazolium bromide test. The results of
in vitro cytotoxicity experiments demonstrated that LGE-N displayed a reduced half
maximum inhibitory concentration in comparison to EGCG-S when tested on the
MCF-7 breast cancer cell line.


4.3       Carbon-Based Nanotherapy

Researchers have discovered that NPs such as carbon nanotubes or carbon
nanoﬁbers (CNTs/CNFs) could be used as carriers for direct drug delivery, espe-
cially in conjunction with or together with other chemotherapy drugs. CNTs/CNFs
are tubes made of carbon which has nanometer dimensions. The dimensions of
CNTs expressed in various ratios with 10–15 nm diameters. Most of these carriers
are multi-wall carbon nanotubes (MWCNT) generally utilized for cancerous cell
targeting.


4.3.1     Carbon Nanotubes/Carbon Nanofibers

A study in 2023 was aimed to evaluate the antiproliferative effects of CNTs in
combination with anticancer drugs against human colorectal carcinoma. A variety of
carbon nanotube (CNT) samples were synthesized, including distinct functional
groups (-O, -N, -S) and exhibiting diverse textural characteristics. The samples
that exhibited the highest degree of interaction with anticancer drugs and showed
76                                                                     P. Ahlawat et al.


enhanced efﬁcacy in the treatment of HT-29 cells were the samples that were
modiﬁed with nitrogen and sulfur groups (Abreu et al. 2023).
   The research was conducted to evaluate the impact of carbon nanoﬁbers (CNFs)
and multi-walled carbon nanotubes (MWCNTs) on cellular functionality, cellular
absorption, and their capacity to augment the responsiveness of urological cancer
cells to the standard chemotherapeutic agent’s cisplatin and carboplatin. The cellular
function was shown to be somewhat impaired by both carbon nanoﬁbers (CNFs) and
carbon nanotubes (CNTs) at concentrations ranging from 1 to 200 mg/mL. Notably,
CNFs exhibited a greater detrimental effect compared to CNTs. The combined use of
carbon nanoﬁbers (CNFs) and carbon nanotubes (CNTs) signiﬁcantly improved the
efﬁcacy of cisplatin and carboplatin in terms of cellular survival. This enhancement
ranged from 1.2- to 2.8-fold in prostate, bladder, and cisplatin-resistant prostate
cancer cells, surpassing the effects observed when these drugs were used
individually.
   A signiﬁcant observation was made in the study conducted by Ringel et al. 2014,
about the potential role of albumin in facilitating the internalization of carbon
nanomaterials into cells. The co-localization of albumin and carbon nanomaterials
on the cellular membrane was substantiated by the utilization of ﬂuorescence
microscopy. The use of transmission electron microscopy revealed that cancer
cells internalized carbon nanomaterials, leading to a heightened probability of
elevated concentrations of chemotherapeutic drugs. In summary, the utilization of
carbon nanomaterial-based applications presents a promising approach for
addressing chemoresistance by enhancing the susceptibility of cancer cells to tradi-
tional chemotherapeutic agents (Ringel et al. 2014).
   In 2017, Erdmann et al. conducted a study to examine the chemosensitizing
capabilities of carbon nanoﬁbers (CNFs) and carbon nanotubes (CNTs) with the
conventional medicines docetaxel (DTX) and mitomycin C (MMC). The DU-145
prostate cancer cells were subjected to treatment with DTX and MMC either alone or
in conjunction with the carbon nanomaterials. The vitality of prostate cancer (PCa)
cells was reduced to 94% and 68% when treated with DTX and MMC alone,
respectively. However, when CNFs were coupled with the therapy, the cellular
viability decreased to just 30%. Therefore, the concurrent administration of tradi-
tional drugs with carbon nanomaterials exhibits a greater capacity to decrease the
dosage of chemotherapy treatments, thereby restricting the occurrence of systemic
toxicities caused by the pharmaceuticals.


4.3.2    Carbon Dots

Carbon dots, often known as C-dots, are a novel nanomaterial that exhibits remark-
able adaptability. Their ﬁrst discovery dates back to the year 2004, as seen in
Fig. 4.2. C-dots have gained signiﬁcant prominence as a versatile platform for the
conjugation of receptors and chemotherapeutic medicines, primarily owing to their
abundant surface functional groups, such as carboxylic and amino groups (Li et al.
2015).
4   Nano-Drug Carriers for Chemotherapeutic Agents Delivery in Cancer. . .             77




Fig. 4.2 Top-down and bottom-up approaches for the synthesis of C-dots. (Adopted from Ross
et al. 2020)


   Cationic carbon dots (CCDs) have been identiﬁed as promising NPs for thera-
peutic applications due to their ability to internalize into cells and traverse the
nuclear membrane. The research performed by Chen et al. (2023) involved the
preparation of cationic carbon dots (Y15-CDs) produced from 1, 2, 4, and
5-benzene tetramine, which is known as Y15 and acts as a focal adhesion kinase
inhibitor. The results of cytotoxicity experiments conducted on human foreskin
ﬁbroblasts (HFF), prostate cancer (PC3), and osteosarcoma cells (U2OS)
demonstrated the nuclear localization properties and anticancer efﬁcacy of
Y15-CDs. One signiﬁcant observation is that Y15-CDs demonstrated enhanced
cytotoxicity toward cancer cells (PC3 and U2OS) compared to normal cells (HFF),
suggesting that Y15-CDs may hold promise as a component of cancer chemothera-
peutic treatments. Another study was conducted to develop a new type of hybrid
liposome nanocarrier consisting of poly(vinylidene ﬂuoride-tetraﬂuoroethylene),
graphene quantum dots (GQDs), and silibinin, which were activated by ultrasound.
These NPs were designed to have piezoelectric properties. The cytotoxicity
experiments conducted on MCF-7, 4 T1, and NIH3T3 cell lines showed that the
presence of NPs did not have a signiﬁcant impact on cell viability at concentrations
up to 125 μg/mL (Ramedani et al. 2022).
   In a study, monodisperse and spherical mesoporous silica NPs (MSNs) were ﬁrst
synthesized and loaded with hydrothermally synthesized anticancer carbon dots
(CDs). Using anti-MUC1 aptamer, the formulation was made targeted toward cancer
cells. Potent and selective anticancer activity was obtained against MCF-7 and
MDA-MB-231 cancer cells. The apoptotic-mediated cell death was recognized as
the primary cytotoxic mechanism of the MSNs-CDs (Kajani et al. 2023).
   Photodynamic therapy (PDT) has considerable potential as an innovative
approach to cancer treatment. Nonetheless, the clinical use of photosensitizers is
hindered by the constraints imposed by hydrophobic interactions and inadequate
water solubility. In a research endeavor undertaken in the year 2022, the authors
undertook the integration of carbon dots with various porphyrins to enhance the
inherent characteristics of porphyrins. Subsequently, the researchers proceeded to
assess the effectiveness of these modiﬁed porphyrins as photodynamic therapy
78                                                                               P. Ahlawat et al.


(PDT) drugs. The biocompatibility was improved by the enhancement of covalent
bonds between cyclodextrins (CDs) and porphyrins. The produced conjugates also
exhibited pH sensitivity, resulting in a reduction in the hemolysis ratio. This
characteristic makes them a promising option for photodynamic therapy (PDT).
The biocompatibility of porphyrins was enhanced by the integration of carbon
dots, resulting in a reduction in their toxicity (Sajjad et al. 2022).
   According to reports, Tian and colleagues from the group of 2023 have
synthesized three pentacyclic triterpenoids (PTs), namely glycyrrhetinic acid (GA),
ursolic acid (UA), and oleanolic acid (OA). These compounds possess anticancer
properties but suffer from hydrophilicity when used as raw materials. To overcome
this limitation, the researchers have employed cyclodextrins (CDs) to enhance the
properties of the PTs. The carboxyl edges of the examined samples showed remark-
able water solubility and a notable ability to selectively target mitochondria in tumor
cells. This selectivity can be attributed to the increased susceptibility of tumor cell
mitochondria to damage caused by reactive oxygen species (ROS) and their elevated
levels of internal oxidative stress. The induction of cell death in cancer cells by
PT-CDs occurs through three distinct mechanisms, including apoptosis, ferroptosis,
and autophagy. Signiﬁcantly, the functionalized PT-CDs demonstrated remarkably
low systemic toxicity.


4.4       Dendrimers Based Nanotherapy

Dendrimers are another type of polymeric structure that is used to synthesize drug-
conjugated nanoformulations. The branching steps represent the generations in the
dendrimeric structure, and they can be combined with any form of speciﬁc moieties
or compounds to enhance the therapeutic efﬁcacy of the bioactive (Fig. 4.3)
(Choudhary et al. 2017).




Fig. 4.3 A basic structure of a dendrimer (adopted from Choudhary et al. 2017)
4   Nano-Drug Carriers for Chemotherapeutic Agents Delivery in Cancer. . .         79


    The synthesis of amine-terminated nanodiamonds (NP) was achieved by the
covalent modiﬁcation of carboxylic acid functionalized nanodiamonds
(ND-COOH) with poly(amidoamine) dendrimer (PAMAM) in a conducted study
by Patil et al. 2022. This research focuses on the physical encapsulation of
cabazitaxel (CTX), a drug with low hydrophilicity, using NP-formed ND-
PAMAM-CTX (NPC) as the encapsulating agent. Signiﬁcant internalization of
NPC was found in both the cytoplasm and nucleus of U87 cells. The study
demonstrated an increase in cell mortality and a decrease in the expression of tubulin
protein in cells treated with NPC. Therefore, this work has brought attention to the
potential of a recently developed nanocarrier and can offer valuable insights for the
treatment of glioblastoma multiforme (GBM) in the future (Patil et al. 2022).
    Another investigation was undertaken to examine the effects of the covalent
conjugation of PAMAM G3 dendrimer with two recognized anticancer drugs,
namely celecoxib and simvastatin. The study involved in vitro testing of these
conjugates on human squamous carcinoma (SCC-15-15) and glioblastoma (U-118
MG) cells, as well as normal human ﬁbroblasts (BJ). The conjugates that were
acquired were further modiﬁed with biotin and R-glycidol to enhance their binding
afﬁnity toward cancer cells. The conjugates, consisting of two celecoxib and four
simvastatin residues, exhibited remarkable efﬁcacy and substantially reduced the
viability of SCC-15 and U-118 MG cells even at extremely low doses. The potential
of biotinylated and R-glycosylated PAMAM G3 dendrimers, which have been
modiﬁed with both celecoxib and simvastatin, shows that they are effective drugs
for the treatment of cancer. (Wróbel et al. 2022).
    A research group developed an innovative class of dendrimers known as enzyme-
triggerable cell-penetrating peptides (ETCPP) that incorporate a camptothecin (CPT)
warhead. Among the recently synthesized dendrimer conjugates of ETCPP, the
high-generation dendrimer known as BL_Oc-SS-CPT had the most pronounced
cytotoxic action against a range of cancer cells, surpassing the effectiveness of
CPT by a factor of 3–10. Signiﬁcantly, BL_Oc-SS-CPT demonstrated exceptional
in vivo tumor targeting capacity, together with notable antitumor effectiveness and
minimal safety proﬁles (Mai et al. 2022).
    In a study conducted to examine the impact of functionalizing poly(amidoamine)
(PAMAM) dendrimer G3 with L, F, or both L and F on breast cancer cells exhibiting
distinct receptor statuses (ER-positive MCF-7, triple-negative MDA-MB-231, and
HER2-positive SK-BR-3 cells). The conjugation of L- and F-PAMAM resulted in an
enhanced cytostatic and cytotoxic effect compared to the individual administration
of L and F. This enhanced effect was followed by an increase in autophagy levels.
The formulation also resulted in the eradication of doxorubicin-induced senescent
breast cancer cells by apoptosis-mediated analysis, irrespective of receptor status.
Therefore, these innovative nanoplatforms have signiﬁcant potential in the ﬁeld of
breast cancer care (Lewińska et al. 2022).
80                                                                            P. Ahlawat et al.




Fig. 4.4 Advantages and versatility offered by protein-based nanoparticles (adopted from Habibi
et al. 2022)




4.5       Protein-Based Nanotherapy

Protein-based nanosystems are biocompatible and biodegradable, with a long half-
life and high purity than any other nanomaterial-based drug delivery carriers
(Fig. 4.4). These targeted protein-based nanosystems can be designed by utilizing
ligand-receptor interactions owing to their high-precision speciﬁc binding ability,
and such interactions as avidin-biotin binding.
    In 2022, Khella et al. performed an investigation to assess the efﬁcacy of
polymeric NPs loaded with CA in enhancing the anticancer effects on MCF-7 breast
cancer cells and Caco-2 colon cancer cells. The compound CA was enclosed within
NPs composed of bovine serum albumin (BSA), chitosan (CH), and cellulose (CL).
The NPs loaded with CA-BSA (CA-BSA-NPs) exhibited a highly favorable formu-
lation with superior loading capacity. The anticancer efﬁcacy of the CA-BSA-NPs
was assessed by the quantiﬁcation of cell viability, apoptosis rate, and the expression
of GCLC, COX-2, and BCL-2 genes in MCF-7 and Caco-2 cell lines. The results of
the cytotoxicity experiment, namely the MTT assay, indicated that CA-BSA-NPs
exhibited increased anticancer activity against MCF-7 and Caco-2 cell lines in
comparison to free CA and BSA-NPs. Additionally, the ﬁndings obtained from the
apoptosis test indicated that the Caco-2 cells were arrested at the G2/M phase, while
the MCF-7 cells were also arrested at the G2/M phase when treated with
CA-BSA-NPs. The gene expression study conducted using RT-PCR demonstrated
an observed increase in the expression of the GCLC gene, while a decrease in
expression was seen for the BCL-2 and COX-2 genes in cells subjected to treatment
4   Nano-Drug Carriers for Chemotherapeutic Agents Delivery in Cancer. . .         81


with CA-BSA-NPs. In summary, CA-BSA-NPs have been identiﬁed as a potentially
effective NP formulation for the treatment of breast and colorectal cancer.
    In another study, the utilization of pH-responsive albumin NPs containing doxo-
rubicin was applied. The drug release exhibited limited efﬁcacy under physiological
settings; however, these NPs demonstrated full release of their chemotherapeutic
payload at a little lower pH, about one unit below the physiological pH, mimicking
the conditions seen in the tumor microenvironment. The results of in vitro
experiments revealed that albumin NPs containing DOX had much greater apoptotic
activity against human lung cancer cells, with a four-fold increase compared to other
tests. The capacity of albumin to inhibit the aggregation of partly hydrophobic DOX
and thereafter release it over an extended duration, without any immediate release,
elucidated its potential to enhance the efﬁcacy of DOX against lung cancer cells
(Aziz et al. 2022).
    The efﬁcacy of NPs containing albumin visnagin (AV) has also been assessed in
triple-negative breast cancer cell lines. To evaluate the effectiveness of NPs in
inducing cell death in MDA-MB-468 cell lines, many approaches were employed.
These techniques included the MTT test, dual AO/EB staining, DCFH-DA assay,
Annexin-V-FITC/PI staining, propidium iodide staining, analysis of apoptotic
proteins, assessment of antioxidant enzymes, and study of the PI3K/Akt/mTOR
signaling pathway. The introduction of NPs resulted in a reduction in cell viability
accompanied by an elevation in reactive oxygen species (ROS) levels. Additionally,
NPs disrupted membrane integrity, a drop in antioxidant enzyme levels, induction of
cell cycle arrest, and activation of the PI3K/Akt/mTOR signaling cascade, ﬁnally
culminating in cellular demise. According to Alsrhani et al. (2023), AV NPs have
signiﬁcant potential as a robust therapeutic alternative for combating cancer. In an
additional intriguing investigation, the researchers created NPs loaded with DTX,
namely DTX@HSA-(PLA)1–3 NPs. The murine and human oral cancer cell lines,
MOC2 and FaDu, were subjected to testing. Monolayers and multicellular spheroids
were utilized to evaluate the cellular uptake, amount of cell viability, and induction
of apoptosis after treatment with NPs. The treatment with DTX@ HSA-(PLA)2 NPs
resulted in increased expression of apoptotic markers, cell cycle arrest in the G2/M
phase, DNA damage, and mitochondrial depolarization compared to the administra-
tion of free DTX and DTX@HSA NPs in both cell lines (Kumbham et al. 2022).
    Chitosan as a polymer has been actively evaluated as a drug delivery system. A
novel NP system based on modiﬁed chitosan, which exhibits pH-responsive behav-
ior, has been produced to actively load doxorubicin (DOX). The cytotoxicity of the
NPs was evaluated on the MCF-7 BrCA cell line at several pH levels. The results
obtained from the in vitro experiments demonstrated that the cytotoxicity of
DOX-loaded NPs against MCF-7 BrCA cells was much greater in comparison to
free DOX (Matalqah et al. 2022). Another research group endeavored to selectively
attack breast and prostate cancer cells by employing chitosan-based NPs that were
loaded with very effective steroidal chemicals with anticancer properties. The
formulations showed efﬁcacy against estrogen receptor-negative breast cancer
cells (MDA-MB-231) and androgen receptor-negative prostate cancer cell lines
(PC-3). The chitosan NPs, which have been loaded with steroids, exhibit signiﬁcant
82                                                                      P. Ahlawat et al.


cytotoxic effects against MDA-MB-231 and PC-3 cell lines. Importantly, these NPs
do not demonstrate any hormone-related action, suggesting that they are both safe
and effective in their intended use (Kuzminac et al. 2022).
    Zein is a prolamin protein variant obtained from corn that has received recogni-
tion from the United States Food and Drug Administration (FDA) as a biologically
safe substance. The plant protein in question has garnered signiﬁcant attention in
academic studies due to its notable characteristics, including low immunogenicity,
edibility, amphipathic, biodegradability, biocompatibility, and resilience to gastro-
intestinal conditions. The beneﬁts render zein-based research particularly advanta-
geous in terms of enhancing oral bioavailability and attaining prolonged and speciﬁc
drug release for anticancer agents. Maytansine, often known as DM1, exhibits strong
antineoplastic properties. According to reports, Yu and his group 2020 created zein
NPs (ZNPs) loaded with DM1 to achieve targeted delivery to tumors and minimize
the hazardous side effects associated with DM1. The researchers discovered that
A549 cells exhibited a signiﬁcant decrease in activity when exposed to DM1-loaded
ZNPs. This result was attributed to the ZNPs’ capacity to facilitate cellular internali-
zation. Additionally, the researchers have shown that ZNPs loaded with DM1 have
superior effectiveness in inhibiting tumor growth compared to DM1 alone. There-
fore, it has been demonstrated that DM1-loaded ZNPs are a favorable choice for
chemotherapy in the treatment of non-small cell lung cancer. Dong et al. (2016)
demonstrated effective encapsulation of DOX within zein, resulting in the formation
of spherical NPs. The observed release behavior of the system was pH-responsive, as
evidenced by the accelerated release of DOX in acidic buffer solutions (pH 5.0 and
6.5) compared to neutral solutions (pH 7.4). The impact of nano-encapsulation on
the inhibition of HeLa cell growth was also investigated. The ﬁndings suggest that
the utilization of DOX-loaded zein NPs (DOX-zein-NPs) resulted in enhanced
efﬁcacy in eliminating cancer cells at lower concentrations of DOX, in comparison
to the use of free DOX.
    Curcumin in casein NPs (CasNPs) have been developed to evaluate their anti-
cancer efﬁcacy. Cur-CasNPs exhibit pH-dependent release properties, exhibiting
increased drug release in mildly acidic conditions. Furthermore, they have exhibited
augmented cytotoxicity toward cancer cells (MCF-7) in comparison to normal cells
(CHO). Furthermore, it should be noted that the CasNPs are non-toxic to the
biological system. Additionally, these NPs have demonstrated signiﬁcant cellular
internalization and have shown an increased level of toxicity toward MCF-7 cells
when compared to pure drugs (Barick et al. 2021). In 2023, Khatun and the group
reported the encapsulation of 10-hydroxycamptothecin in biocompatible casein NPs.
The biocompatibility of the synthesized CS and CCS NPs was found to be excellent
when tested on ﬁbroblast cell lines L929, NIH-3 T3, and zebraﬁsh embryos. The
researchers observed an increased internalization of CCS NPs in 4 T1 cells, resulting
in a cancer cell toxicity of around 80–85%. Observation of NPs exhibiting
sub-cellular mitochondrial localization, together with modiﬁcations in mitochondrial
membrane potential, lysosomal localization, and formation of reactive oxygen
species leading to cancer cell death, has been reported. In addition, the study
revealed growth inhibition of 4 T1 cell colonies and the presence of antimetastatic
4   Nano-Drug Carriers for Chemotherapeutic Agents Delivery in Cancer. . .          83


activity. It is due to an elevation in the levels of the CHOP protein, which acts as a
reliable indicator for cell death resulting from endoplasmic reticulum stress. The
three-dimensional model of 4 T1 cells demonstrated extensive inﬁltration into the
tumor and showed notable therapeutic effectiveness for CCS NPs. The ﬁndings of
this study have emphasized the potential of utilizing casein-based nanoformulation
as a viable and cost-effective approach for cancer treatment in triple-negative breast
cancer (TNBC) patients.


4.6       Synthetic Polymer-Based Nanotherapy

Apart from naturally occurring polymers, nanotherapy based on synthetic polymers
has been greatly explored. These include PLGA [poly (lactide-co-glycolide)], PLA
[polylactic acid], PBCA [Poly(butyl cyanoacrylate)], etc. They are synthetically
produced and are then developed into various nanosystems.


4.6.1     PLGA Nanoparticles

PLGA is a biodegradable copolymer ester of two α-hydroxyacids (lactic and glycolic
acids) and has been approved by the FDA for drug delivery. There are several
physicochemical properties of PLGA that impact the release behavior of drugs
from its nanocarrier form. In 2023, Hu et al. synthesized hyaluronic acid-coated
Olaparib-loaded PEI-PLGA NPs, which are referred to as HA-Ola-PPNPs. The
objective of this study was to investigate the potential anticancer efﬁcacy of these
nanoparticles against triple-negative breast cancer. The overexpression of Bax,
Cytochrome C, and Caspase 3 demonstrated the induction of cell death by
HA-Ola-PPNPs (Hu et al. 2022). According to Simitcioglu et al. (2022), a study
was conducted to examine the impact of various molecular weights (Resomer
502 and 504) and terminal groups (Resomer 502H and 504H) of polylactic-co-
glycolic acid NPs on their characteristics, as well as their effects on prostate cancer
cells and healthy cells. The researchers aimed to investigate the inﬂuence of these
Resomers on the properties of the NPs and their interactions with both cancerous and
non-cancerous cells. The researchers provided evidence that the groups exhibiting
large molecular weight had a discernible preference for cytotoxicity.
   To enhance the systemic administration of FZ, a group of researchers undertook
the task of transforming fat-soluble medicines into hydrophilic forms. As part of
their investigation, they developed FZ-encapsulated poly(D, L-lactide-co-glycolide)
acid (PLGA) NPs, referred to as FZ-PLGA-NPs. The study conducted by Chang
et al. (2023) showed that the use of FZ-PLGA-NPs resulted in a considerable
reduction in cellular proliferation inside the EOC cell line. In their study conducted
in 2023, Kumari et al. developed a formulation consisting of poly (lactide-co-
glycolide) (PLGA) NPs encapsulating docetaxel (DTX), with the monoclonal anti-
body Cetuximab (Cet) attached to the surface. This research aimed to explore the
potential of this targeted therapeutic approach for the treatment of lung cancer. The
84                                                                    P. Ahlawat et al.


cellular uptake in lung cancer cells that overexpressed EGFR (speciﬁcally A549 and
NCI-H23) was boosted. The NPs exhibited enhanced therapeutic efﬁcacy against
non-small cell lung cancer (NSCLC) cell lines as well.
   Another research group successfully synthesized nano-encapsulated
hydroxytyrosol using a copolymer known as poly-lactide-co-glycolide-co-
polyacrylic acid (PLGA-PAA). The estimation of the antiproliferative potential
was conducted using an MTT test. The assessment of gene expression levels for
CDKN1A, CDKN1B, and CCND1 was also conducted. The results obtained from
the MTT experiment indicate that the nano-capsulated hydroxytyrosol exhibited
more cytotoxicity toward the colorectal cancer cell line compared to free
hydroxytyrosol. The study demonstrated an increase in the expression of
CDKN1A and CDKN1B genes, as well as a decrease in the expression of the
CCND1 gene, within colorectal cancer cells (Sani et al. 2022).


4.6.2    PLA and PBCA Nanoparticles

Poly(lactic acid) (PLA), poly(butyl cyanoacrylate) (PBCA), and their copolymers
are among the most frequently used biodegradable polymers for encapsulated drugs.
PLA is widely used for many biological applications due to its biocompatible and
biodegradable properties. PLA and PBCA have been widely used in different
biomedical applications like tissue engineering, fracture repairs, orthopedics, drug
delivery carriers, and antimicrobial agents. In 2022 Wadhawan and their research
group successfully produced polymeric NPs composed of polylactic acid-poly
(ethylene glycol) (PLA-PEG) copolymers, encapsulated with biosurfactants. This
was achieved by the utilization of the double emulsion solvent evaporation tech-
nique, with folic acid (FA) serving as the ligand for targeted delivery. The
researchers observed that the presence of FA resulted in an increase in both the
absorption and cytotoxicity of NPs loaded with biosurfactant in MDA-MB-231
cancer cells, as compared to NPs loaded with plain biosurfactant. Apoptosis induc-
tion was furthermore seen in cancer cells that were subjected to NP treatment
(Wadhawan et al. 2022).
   In 2022, Zhang et al. synthesized Gemcitabine-loaded nanospheres by emulsion
polymerization (GEM-PBCA-NP), and then, anti-MUC1 and anti-human CD199
monoclonal antibodies were coupled separately. Both NPs demonstrated signiﬁ-
cantly higher cytotoxicity as well apoptosis rates against human pancreatic cancer
cell lines. Even the tumor inhibition rate was higher in tumor-bearing mice for the
nanoformulations (Zhang et al. 2022). Another study reported the development of an
orthogonal experimental design (OED) for the preparation of PBCA NPs. The
results of the in vitro investigation utilizing SH-SY5Y neuroblastoma cells indicated
that osthol-loaded NPs exhibited a notable increase in the intracellular absorption
process and cytotoxicity when compared to free osthol. Therefore, the osthol-PBCA
NPs have promising potential for the delivery of hydrophobic drugs (Zheng et al.
2022).
4   Nano-Drug Carriers for Chemotherapeutic Agents Delivery in Cancer. . .        85


4.7       Metal-Based Nanotherapy

Metals, including iron, zinc, copper, gold, silver, and others, alongside mesoporous
silica NPs (MSNs), exhibit considerable potential as drug delivery materials owing
to their diminutive dimensions, inert characteristics, and remarkably expansive
speciﬁc surfaces that can be modiﬁed with therapeutic agents and targeting entities.


4.7.1     Mesoporous Silica Nanoparticles (MSNs)

Mesoporous silica nanoparticles (MSNs) are promising nanomaterials for use in
biomedical applications. The functionalization properties of MSNs make them
capable of co-delivery, controlled delivery, and stimuli-responsive delivery. Multi-
drug resistance is a common reason behind the failure of chemotherapy. Therefore,
Wang and group 2014 designed mesoporous silica NPs (MSNs). They tested the
efﬁcacy of DOX in the breast cancer cell line MCF-7/MDR1. The authors showed
that the drug resistance in the human breast cancer cell line MCF-7/MDR1 could be
overcome by treatment with DOX encapsulated within mesoporous silica NPs.
   Research conducted in 2022 reported a novel method for synthesizing
mesoporous silica NPs (MSNs) characterized by a distinctive core-shell structure
resembling an egg yolk. The study conducted by Tiburcius et al. (2022) assessed the
efﬁcacy of Dox-laden mesoporous silica NPs (MSNs) in inhibiting the growth of
prostate cancer cells. Two distinct prostate cancer cell lines, namely PC-3 and
LNCaP, were utilized for this evaluation. The results indicated that the optimized
MSN samples loaded with the drug exhibited more cytotoxicity compared to other
formulations. Another research group conducted an intriguing experiment in which
they examined the effects of ﬂuorescent silicon NPs (DOX-NPs) on the MCF-7
breast cancer cell line. The researchers assessed the cytotoxicity of DOX-NP, as well
as its impact on the cell cycle and the production of CD44 antigen. CD44 is a
molecule that plays a role in cellular adhesion and the invasion of tumors. The
evaluation includes a comparison between DOX-NP, free DOX, and SiNPs used
independently. A particular capacity to secrete a small number of extracellular
vesicles (EVs) expressing CD44+ was identiﬁed in both CD81-negative and
CD81-positive populations. The modulation of CD44 levels on the cell surface in
cancer cells has signiﬁcant relevance in altering signaling networks that promote
tumor growth (Sola et al. 2022).


4.7.2     Iron Oxide Nanoparticles

Among the wide amount of drug nanocarriers, magnetic iron oxide nanoparticles
(IONs) stand out from the crowd and constitute robust nanoplatforms since they can
achieve high drug loading as well as targeting abilities stemming from their remark-
able properties (magnetic and biological properties). Iron oxide nanoparticles have
been the most extensively studied inorganic nanocarrier systems for drug delivery.
86                                                                     P. Ahlawat et al.


These are biodegradable, biocompatible, and efﬁciently cleared from the human
body via the iron metabolism pathways. In a study, Rostami et al. (2022) presented a
conﬁrmation of the anticancer properties of magnetic Fe3O4 NPs coated with
L-lysine (referred to as F-Lys NPs) and loaded with Tamoxifen (TMX), speciﬁcally
targeting breast cancer cells. To assess the cytotoxicity of the NPs that were speciﬁ-
cally engineered, a combination of MTT and apoptosis tests, real-time PCR, and cell
cycle analysis were utilized. An increase in the concentration of NPs and the
duration of treatment resulted in greater suppression of cell growth and activation
of apoptosis in cells treated with F-Lys-TMX NPs. The study revealed a decrease in
the gene expression levels of ERBB2, cyclin D1, and cyclin E, whereas an increase
in the expression levels of caspase-3 and caspase-9 was seen. The drug release
mechanism has been the subject of research, which has consistently shown a
pH-dependent release pattern of TMX. The ﬁndings of the cell cycle investigation
revealed that the application of F-Lys-TMX NPs led to the cessation of cellular
progression in the G0/G1 phase, therefore, advocating for the integration of metal
NPs into the cancer therapy protocol. In another study, iron oxide (Fe3O4) NPs were
coated with hyperbranched polyglycerol (HPG) and folic acid (FA). The
FA@HPG@Fe3O4 NPs were seen to be internalized to a greater extent in both
HeLa cells and murine L929 ﬁbroblasts compared to the HPG@Fe3O4 NPs. Fur-
thermore, the proportion of MRI signal enhancement was reduced with incubation of
HeLa cells with NPs, with the most signiﬁcant modiﬁcation found following incu-
bation with FA@HPG@Fe3O4 NPs (Ramezani Farani et al. 2022).


4.7.3    Zinc Oxide Nanoparticles

Zinc oxide (ZnO) NPs have emerged as a versatile nanocarrier to deliver the
therapeutic moiety effectively to the targeted site. Speciﬁcally, when the drug
alone is not capable of entering the targeted cells, the drug-loaded NPs penetrate
into the cells through various intracellular endocytic pathways. ZnO NPs have
become one of the most popular metal oxide nanoparticles in biological applications
due to their excellent biocompatibility and low toxicity. Thomas et al. (2022)
conducted a synthesis of ZnO NPs using Solanum nigrum and observed consider-
able anticancer effects against HeLa cell lines via the apoptotic mechanism. The
evaluation of the cytotoxicity of zinc oxide (ZnO) NPs was conducted using several
experimental approaches, such as the MTT test, wound healing assay, DAPI
staining, and acridine orange and ethidium bromide double staining. The utilization
of the reverse transcriptase-PCR methodology was performed to examine the
patterns of expression of β-catenin, p53, caspase-3, and caspase-9. The results of
the study indicate that ZnO NPs obtained from Solanum nigrum exhibit cytotoxic
effects on the HeLa cell line, with the extent of toxicity depending on the dosage
administered. The outcome is attained by the inhibition of β-catenin and the aug-
mentation of p53, caspase-3, and caspase-9 levels.
   In 2022, George et al. fabricated zinc oxide NPs (ZnO NPs) by a green synthesis
approach employing R. fairholmianus root extract. Subsequently, the impact of these
4   Nano-Drug Carriers for Chemotherapeutic Agents Delivery in Cancer. . .           87


NPs on MCF-7 cells was assessed. The treatment with R. fairholmianus capped zinc
oxide NPs (RFZnO NPs) resulted the decreased in cellular proliferation,
accompanied by an increase in cellular cytotoxicity and levels of reactive oxygen
species (ROS). An increase in programmed cell death, known as apoptosis, was
seen, as shown by elevated levels of cytoplasmic cytochrome c and caspase 3/7.
These biomarkers are commonly used to assess the occurrence of apoptosis. Fur-
thermore, an increase in the expression of pro-apoptotic proteins, namely p53 and
Bax, was seen, whereas a decrease in the expression of the anti-apoptotic protein
Bcl-2 was noted. In summary, the results of this work indicated that RFZnO NPs can
induce apoptosis in breast cancer cells via activating a mitochondria-mediated
caspase-dependent apoptotic pathway (George et al. 2022a).


4.7.4     Inert Metals Nanoparticles

Among all types of noble metal-based nanoparticles, gold nanoparticles (Au NPs)
have shown a great capacity for use as potential drug delivery carriers. The thera-
peutic efﬁcacy of gold NPs (AuNPs), gold NPs loaded with Galangin (AuNPs-
GAL), and free Galangin as an antiangiogenic agent against breast cancer cells was
investigated in a reported study. Various physicochemical and molecular
methodologies have been employed to investigate the anticancerous properties
of AuNPs-GAL on MCF-7 and MDA-MB-231 cells. These methodologies encom-
pass the characterization of the treated cancer cells, assessment of their cytotoxicity,
evaluation of their scratch wound healing capabilities, and examination of the gene
expression levels of VEGF and ERKI. The ﬁndings derived from the MTT assay
demonstrated a decrease in cellular proliferation that was contingent upon both the
duration of exposure and the concentration of the treatment. Moreover, the data
suggested a synergistic effect (Qaddoori and Al-Shmgani 2023).
   So far, no previous research has provided empirical data on the in vitro anti-
human pancreatic cancer capabilities of the newly formulated chemotherapeutic
drug including silver NPs and Berberis thunbergii leaf (AgNPs). The objective of
this study was to examine the potential anti-human pancreatic cancer characteristics
of silver nitrate (AgNO3), aqueous extract from B. thunbergii leaves, and silver NPs
(AgNPs). To assess these properties, the researchers employed the
3-(4,5-dimethylthiazol-2-yl)-2,5-diphenyl-2H-tetrazolium bromide (MTT) test on
commonly used human pancreatic cancer cell lines. The treatment with AgNPs
exhibited low cell viability and was shown to be dependent on the dosage when
tested against PANC-1, AsPC-1, and MIA PaCa-2 cell lines. Therefore, the research
provides evidence in favor of employing silver AgNPs as a viable therapeutic
approach for combating pancreatic cancer, as suggested by Guo et al. (2022). In a
separate investigation, the MTT test was employed to evaluate the cytotoxic effects
of CP, green AgNPs, and CP-AgNPs on PC-3, a cell line derived from human
prostate cancer. The cytotoxic impact of CP-AgNPs on PC-3 cells was shown to be
much higher compared to CP and AgNPs alone. The IC50 values for CP-AgNPs,
CP, and AgNPs were determined to be 27.05 μg/mL, 57.64 μg/mL, and 125.4 μg/
88                                                                    P. Ahlawat et al.


mL, respectively. Therefore, our results indicate the potential use of CP-AgNPs as a
more effective anticancer drug in comparison to free CP (El-Sheikh et al. 2022).
   The magnetic CuFe2O4 material has an intrinsic protease activity that enables the
hydrolysis of bovine serum albumin (BSA) and casein molecules under physiologi-
cal circumstances. Protease is known to have a signiﬁcant role in several pathophys-
iological mechanisms and is involved in every phase of tumor advancement. In a
conducted study, it was observed that CuFe2O4 demonstrated an inhibitory effect on
the proliferation of A549, SKOV3, HT-29, BABL-3 T3, and HUVEC cells. Notably,
CuFe2O4 exhibited heightened sensitivity toward SKOV3 ovarian cancer cells,
surpassing the effectiveness of conventional chemotherapy drugs oxaliplatin and
artesunate by approximately 30-fold in inhibiting the growth of SKOV3 cells.
Furthermore, CuFe2O4 was shown to modulate the intracellular protein expression
of MMP-2, MMP-9, F-actin, and NF-kB, which are commonly linked to overall
protein degradation. The compound CuFe2O4 also demonstrates the ability to
impede cell motility. The CuFe2O4 NPs are very desirable for their uses in cancer
treatment due to their exceptional magnetic characteristics, proﬁcient protease-
mimicking capacity, and notable antitumor efﬁciency (Chen et al. 2022).


4.8      Combinatorial Nanoparticles

Combination therapy of multiple drugs through a single system exhibits high thera-
peutic effects. Combination therapies of small-molecule drugs are common in
clinical practice and it is anticipated that packaging into single macromolecular
carriers will enable drug release in precisely balanced ratios and rates, and in
selectively targeted tissues and cells. This vast level of pharmacological control is
intriguing, especially from the perspective of tailoring personalized treatments with
maximized therapeutic synergy for individual patients. In 2022, Esmaeili et al.
conducted a signiﬁcant study in which they developed a cancer nano theranostics
system using a mesoporous silica@chitosan@gold (MCM@CS@Au) nanosystem.
This nanosystem was speciﬁcally designed to target MUC-1 positive tumor cells,
and aptamers were employed for this purpose. Moreover, the nanosystem was
utilized for the loading of curcumin. The researchers discovered that the energy
transfer occurring between the base pairs inside the hairpin structure of double-
stranded DNA aptamers serves as a quenching mechanism for MCM@CS@Au
ﬂuorescence. This ultimately leads to the development of an optical biosensor with
a binary “on/off” response. In contrast, the inclusion of chitosan NPs that are
responsive to changes in pH offers an intelligent nanosystem for enhanced delivery
of curcumin to speciﬁc cellular targets. The drug release and ﬂuorescence recovery
(“On” state) occur as a result of aptamer binding to the MUC-1 receptor in a low pH
environment. The observed toxicity outcomes indicate that the nanosystem exhibited
a higher degree of toxicity toward MUC-1-positive tumor cells compared to MUC-1-
negative cells, hence demonstrating its capacity for selective targeting. Hence, our
nanosystem possesses the potential to serve as an intelligent nanotheranostic system
4   Nano-Drug Carriers for Chemotherapeutic Agents Delivery in Cancer. . .         89


for the detection and monitoring of speciﬁc biomarkers (such as MUC-1), non-inva-
sive ﬂuorescence imaging, and precise administration of curcumin to selected sites.
   In a recent study, researchers reported on the use of mesoporous silica NPs
(MSNs) that were coated with a dual ﬁlm consisting of calcium carbonate
(CaCO3) and a lipid bilayer. These NPs, referred to as MSNs@CaCO3@liposomes,
demonstrated the ability to provide sustained drug release speciﬁcally targeted to the
tumor microenvironment. Additionally, this novel approach resulted in increased
biocompatibility. The calcium carbonate coating, which is sensitive to changes in
pH, served to seal the pore channels of mesoporous silica NPs (MSNs). This enabled
the release of drugs to be triggered by pH variations when the NPs were internalized
by cancer cells. The DOX-loaded MSNs@CaCO3@liposomes (referred to as
DOX/MSNs@CaCO3@liposomes) exhibited non-speciﬁc protein adsorption, and
the hemolysis test indicated improved biocompatibility. The in vitro drug release
investigation showed that the DOX/MSNs@CaCO3@liposomes exhibited a delayed
release of DOX speciﬁcally at pH 5.0, whereas no release was seen at pH 7.4. The
assessment of the anticancer efﬁcacy of DOX/MSNs@CaCO3@liposomes showed a
favorable capacity for tumor regression. Hence, it is plausible to consider dual-ﬁlm-
coated mesoporous silica NPs (MSNs) as a promising option for the development of
a drug delivery system targeting tumors (Wang et al. 2022).
   The interaction between proteins and NPs is of utmost importance in facilitating
drug binding and maintaining its stability, hence improving the effectiveness of
targeted drug delivery. The present work aimed to investigate the relationship
between whey protein β-lactoglobulin (BLG) conjugated with gold NPs (AuNPs)
and two speciﬁc compounds, curcumin (CUR) and gemcitabine (GEM). The cyto-
toxicity experiments were conducted with breast cancer cell lines known as MCF-7.
The examination of these conjugates may be deemed relevant in the context of
targeted drug delivery investigations within the ﬁeld of cancer research (Waghmare
et al. 2022).
   In a study, Jaiswal and group (2022) reported the synthesis of two bi-functional
nanoconjugates, namely AuNR@EGFP-BSAFA and AuNR@RNaseA-BSAFA,
which consisted of gold nanorods (AuNR) conjugated with proteins. These
nanoconjugates were coated with folic acid-modiﬁed bovine serum albumin
(BSAFA), which served as a biomimetic protein corona. The purpose of this study
was to demonstrate the targeted delivery of enhanced green ﬂuorescent protein
(EGFP) and therapeutic ribonuclease A enzyme (RNase A) to the cytosol while
preserving their functional properties. The use of Folate receptor-targeting BSAFA
on the surface of AuNRs promotes the process of receptor-mediated internalization,
subsequently leading to the liberation of EGFP and RNase A within HT29 cells. The
presence of green ﬂuorescence distributed throughout the cytoplasm of the cell
suggests that the AuNR@EGFP-BSAFA complex has effectively delivered EGFP
to the cytosol. The therapeutic administration of RNase A using AuNR@RNaseA-
BSAFA in multicellular 3D spheroids of HT29 cells results in a signiﬁcant decrease
in cellular RNA ﬂuorescence intensity, reducing it to 38%. This drop indicates the
degradation of RNA and eventual cell death. The current AuNR-protein
nanoconjugates demonstrate promise as a possible platform for cancer therapy due
90                                                                     P. Ahlawat et al.


to their variable nanoformulation strategy, which encompasses anisotropic particle
shape, diverse protein types, and targeted delivery capabilities in their
functional form.
    The research team has successfully developed an acidity-responsive nanovehicle
as a delivery mechanism for doxorubicin (DOX). The aforementioned outcome was
attained by the collaborative assembly of amphiphilic PEGylated chitosan20k and
hydrophobic poly(lactic-co-glycolic acid) (PLGA) segments at a pH condition of
8.5. The ﬁndings from the in vitro trials investigating cellular uptake and cytotoxic-
ity indicate that the DOX-PC20kPNs were successfully internalized by prostate
TRAMP-C1 cancer cells. Furthermore, the free drug was promptly dispersed across
various cellular compartments. This phenomenon facilitated the accumulation of the
drug in the nucleus, hence augmenting its anticancer efﬁcacy. In addition, it was
shown that the presence of hydrated polyethylene glycol (PEG) shells on
DOX-PC20kPNs led to a signiﬁcant reduction in their internalization by
macrophage-like RAW264.7 cells (Huang et al. 2022).
    Another study endeavor was done to examine the enhanced cytotoxic impacts of
polymer-coated gold NPs (GNPs) containing 5-ﬂuorouracil (5-FU) in the treatment
of lung adenocarcinoma. Based on the ﬁndings obtained from the MTT assay, it was
shown that the cytotoxic impact of PFGNPs on the A549 cells was 82.89%, which
was higher in comparison to the 74.91% cytotoxic effect exhibited by the 5-FU
solution. The present study investigated the impact of PFNPs, FGNPs, PFGNPs, and
5-FU on the expression of EGFR and KRAS genes. Our ﬁndings revealed that
PFNPs exhibited the highest level of efﬁcacy among the tested NPs and chemother-
apeutic agent, 5-FU. In conclusion, this strategy has promise as a chemotherapeutic
alternative that offers a reduction in toxicity and an enhancement in efﬁcacy (Gupta
et al. 2022).


4.9      Conclusion

The previous decade has seen tremendous upgradation and innovation in the ﬁeld of
nanomedicine against cancer. A plethora of novel nanoplatforms or nanosystems has
been developed to cater to the treatment of various carcinoma. Many biological
phenomena speciﬁc to tumorigenesis and tumor microenvironment have been stud-
ied to exploit them for the enhancement of nanoformulations’ chemotherapeutic
efﬁcacy. The NPs have not only been developed from a variety of naturally occur-
ring as well synthetically produced materials but also have been purposefully
optimized and modiﬁed to expand the therapeutic window of conventional chemo-
therapeutic drugs such as enhancing their bioavailability, targetability as well as
minimizing their systemic toxicities The incorporation of different shapes of NPs,
utilization of inert metals and/or elements, magnetic metals, naturally occurring
proteins as well as synthetic polymers have been tested in permutations and
combinations to formulate the ﬁnest nanosystems to be utilized either in combination
or as an adjunct therapeutic modality in cancer management regimen.
4   Nano-Drug Carriers for Chemotherapeutic Agents Delivery in Cancer. . .                       91


4.10      Future Perspectives

Nanomedicine is an extremely dynamic ﬁeld of research and entrepreneurship. It has
time and again proven its utility in cancer treatment. Scientists have been actively
stretching the limitations as well as the possibilities of the nanomaterials to formulate
highly speciﬁc and versatile nanosystems as in the case of theranostics. Various
molecules such as DNA, RNA, inert metals, and even carbon are being evaluated
extensively to support cancer management and improvement in patient compliance.
Additionally, it is a ripe time to develop new and sensitive methodologies for the
synthesis and testing of novel and combinatorial NPs as this area grows and caters to
the intersection of various disciplines as well as pathologies. This ﬁeld has a huge
potential to contribute not only to human welfare but also to strengthen industry and
academic collaboration generating advancement in science and human resource.

Testing of Nanocarriers Loaded
with Chemotherapeutic Agents                                                              5
in the Animal Models of Cancers: Preclinical
Trials for Efficacy and Safety Profiling
of Nanocarriers

Laiba Iftikhar, Mishaal Fareed, Naeem Ullah Khan, and Farah Rehan


   Abstract

   Researchers are focusing on nanotechnology use in cancer treatment for decades.
   The primary processes used in nanotechnology involve the segregation, combin-
   ing, and modiﬁcation of materials by a single atom or molecule. It offers a
   distinctive strategy and sophisticated technology for treating cancers through
   early detection, forecasting, prevention, individualized therapy, and medical
   care. Chemotherapeutic medicines that are hard to penetrate blood vessels are
   encased in nanoparticles between 50 and 400 nm, their small nano size making it
   hard for them to cross a healthy person’s blood vessel walls and cells. Polymers,
   liposomes, micelles, dendrimers, and carbon nanotubes are among various nano-
   delivery carriers being employed against cancer. These nanocarriers can be
   delivered through multiple routes of drug delivery, such as oral, injection,
   intraocular, and parenteral, and later tested in animal models for their preclinical
   testing. This chapter focused on the use of various animal models been used so far
   for the testing of these nanocarriers to assess their efﬁcacy in preclinical setup.
   Animal models are classiﬁed according to the method by which cancer is induced
   in the animal. Spontaneous tumor models are the most primitive models. The
   study of carcinogenesis has beneﬁted greatly from the use of mouse models of
   human cancer. The zebraﬁsh model is a low-cost, high-throughput animal model
   that can be used to study the mechanisms behind tumor formation, metastasis, and
   therapeutic response as well as forecast the efﬁcacy of personalized cancer
   treatment. The hu-PBL model is now being used in numerous areas of cancer
   research, including investigations into malignancies of the thyroid, thyroid gland,
   breast, nasopharynx, and other cancers. However, the creation of secure and
