     effective transport vectors is a key obstacle to the success of gene therapy using
     nanoparticles. There is at least a theoretical possibility that NPs could cause
     protein unfurling, crosslinking, or unfortunate enzymatic movement, leading to
     unfavorable and unexpected outcomes. Safety proﬁling of nanoparticles ensures
     that the treatment is effective and safe for patients. Without proper safety
     proﬁling, there is a risk of harmful side effects and potential damage to healthy
     cells. Before testing on humans, in vivo, safety proﬁling technique is used to
     assess the possible harmful effects of nanoparticles on healthy cells in case they
     occurred.

     Keywords
     Cancer treatment · Nanotechnology · Nanoparticles · Chemotherapeutic drugs ·
     Animal models · Zebraﬁsh model · Mouse model · Hu-PBL model · Safety
     proﬁling


5.1        Cancer

5.1.1      Historical Turning Points in Cancer

Although cancer is as ancient as people, pale pathologic research demonstrates that
tumors were present in animals long before humans set foot on Earth. In his Materia
Medica, Pliny the Roman scientist (AD 23–79) compiled a list of cancer therapies.
His most effective therapy consisted of a boiling concoction made up of egg white,
honey, powdered falcon feces and sea crab ash (Hajdu 2012). Aretaeus
(AD 81–138), an Egyptian physician who lived and worked in Alexandria, provided
the earliest comprehensive explanation of the causes, symptoms, and treatments of
uterine cancer. There are two different sorts of cancer, according to what he noted in
his notes: one is hard and ulcerated to the touch, while the other is odorous and
ulcerated (Wang and Thanou 2010). Hippocrates used the word “Carcinos” to
denote both ulcerative and non-ulcerative tumors. Greek language uses the word
“cancer” to refer to a crab, which makes sense given that the disease has crab-like
spreading projections that resemble ﬁngers. The Latin word for crab, cancer, was
later used to translate the Greek phrase by the Roman physician Celsus (25 BC–
50 AD). Galen (130–200 AD), a different Greek physician, called tumors oncos
(Greek for swelling). Although a comparison of Hippocrates and Celsus’ crab is still
used to explain dangerous cancers (Cheong et al. 2011).


5.1.2      Treatment of Cancer

Cancer treatment depends on how enormous it has been spread, plus understanding
how we can overcome it. We can go through various modalities, just as cancer drugs,
chemotherapy, surgery for cancer, radiotherapy, hormone therapy, bone marrow,
5   Testing of Nanocarriers Loaded with Chemotherapeutic Agents in the Animal. . .   99


and stem cell transplants, immunotherapy, and individualized and complementary
therapies.


5.1.3     Introduction to Nanotechnology

The deﬁnition of nanotechnology is “technology at the nanoscale.” It is the twenty-
ﬁrst century’s most promising technology. The deﬁnition of “nano” is one billionth
of a meter (10-9 m). It includes the creation of nanoscale materials, their characteri-
zation, engineering, and size-controlled application. Numerous biological
applications of nanotechnology make use of the physical and chemical features of
nanoparticles. The creation and use of chemical, physical, and biological systems at
scales ranging from single molecules to submicron dimensions, as well as the
integration of the resulting nanomaterials into more intricate systems, are all
included in this (Nasrollahzadeh et al. 2019). Synthetic approaches for the fabrica-
tion of nanoengineered materials can be classiﬁed into two groups: “top-down” and
“bottom-up” approaches. In the top-down method, externally controlled and larger
devices are used to create nanoscale devices to direct their assembly. To cut, grind,
and shape materials into the appropriate arrangement or shape when using a
top-down approach, traditional workshop or microfabrication techniques are fre-
quently used. In the bottom-up approach, devices, and molecular components which
do not require any external agent assembly are used to construct devices and
materials. They chemically assemble by recognizing molecules of their own type.
This approach starts with the collection and combination of atoms and molecules to
build complex structures (Silva 2004).


5.2       Applications of Nanotechnology

Nanotechnology application is a young ﬁeld with many possible applications.
Recently, the use of nanotechnology in the treatment of cancer has received a lot
of attention. It offers a distinctive strategy and sophisticated technology for treating
cancers through early detection, forecasting, prevention, individualized therapy, and
medical care (Misra et al. 2010). Chemotherapy, radiation therapy, and surgery are
the only cancer treatments that are commonly used, but these are not always the best
options due to their numerous drawbacks. Delineating tumor margins, diagnosing
recurrent tumor cells and micrometastase, and determining if a tumor has been
completely removed therefore are urgent needs and have signiﬁcant potential.
Nanotechnology can be used in many ﬁelds related to health care services including
cancer therapy.

•   Nanotechnology can be used for better cancer diagnosis.
•   Nanotechnology can provide better drug delivery to tumor/cancer cells.
•   It can provide molecular targeted cancer therapy.
•   This technology works better with dyes which allows better tumor cells detection.
100                                                                     L. Iftikhar et al.


5.2.1     Nanomaterials Used for Cancer Therapy

There are various nanomaterials that are broadly used for the treatment of cancer.
Some of these nanomaterials are;

1.   Polymeric nanoparticles.
2.   Lipid-based nanomaterials.
3.   Nano-emulsions.
4.   Carbon nanomaterials.

5.2.1.1 Polymeric Nanoparticles
These are colloidal macromolecules of 10–1000 nm size. Polymeric nanoparticles
are the drug carriers used for the sustained release of chemotherapeutic agents to
target cancerous sites (Society 2018). To prevent inﬂammation and toxicity, poly-
meric nanoparticles made of polystyrene, PMMA, polyacrylamide, and
polyacrylates are utilized. Chemotherapeutic chemicals can enter malignant regions
thanks to leaky blood vessels in tumor-initiated angiogenesis. In comparison to
conventional particles, polymeric nanoparticles, and their associated chemical
medicines can more readily collect at target areas because of reduced lymphatic
drainages. Due to PNPs’ high surface-to-volume ratio, attaching targeting polymers
to the surface is not difﬁcult. Polymers’ bioavailability can be raised by encasing
them in polysorbates, according to research.

5.2.1.2 Lipid-Based Nanomaterials
The three primary types of lipid-based nanoparticles are liposomes, solid lipid
nanoparticles (SLNs), and nanostructured lipid carriers (NLCs). The typical lipo-
some size range is 20 nm to more than 1 μm (Batist et al. 2009). The main uses of
liposomes are co-delivery and sustained release. Advanced solid tumors respond
better to new liposomes containing chemotherapy drugs loaded with both ﬂoxuridine
and irinotecan (Batist et al. 2009). Because of their low toxicity, biocompatibility,
and non-immunogenicity, liposomes shield loaded pharmaceuticals from degrada-
tion. However, due to their shorter half-life, unsatisfactory stability, poor potency,
and quick elimination by MPS, their use is restricted. SLNs are nanocarriers with
particles as small as 1–100 nm (Fig. 5.1). Drugs are enclosed in SLNs’ micelles-like
structure, which has a non-aqueous core (Kraft et al. 2014). SLNs outperform
liposomes in terms of stability and sustained release. SLNs and liposomes are less
effective than NLCs. They can be given via oral, parenteral, inhalational, and ocular
routes among others.

5.2.1.3 Nanoemulsions
Oil, emulsifying agents, and an aqueous phase combine to generate nanoemulsions
(NE). The size of nanoemulsions can vary from 10 to 1000 nm. It is common
practice to use drug nanocarriers known as nanoemulsions, which are typically
solid spheres with an amorphous, lipophilic surface and a negative charge. The
three most prevalent types of nanoemulsions that can be produced are bi-continuous
5   Testing of Nanocarriers Loaded with Chemotherapeutic Agents in the Animal. . .           101




Fig. 5.1 Nanomaterials employed for drug delivery for cancer treatment (Credit: Lôbo et al. 2021)


nanoemulsions, water in oil nanoemulsion systems, and oil in water nanoemulsion
system (Jaiswal et al. 2015). Nanoemulsions provide a variety of beneﬁts over the
majority of lipid-based nanomaterials and nanoparticles, including optical clarity,
thermodynamic stability, a sizable surface area, ease of manufacturing, biodegrad-
ability, and the ideal drug release proﬁle. The NE drug carrier system loaded with
spirulina polysaccharides and PTX indicated that it may enhance the anti-tumor
activity of PTX by regulating immunity via Toll-like receptor 4/nuclear factor kappa
B (TLR4/NF-B) signaling pathways (Du et al. 2020).

5.2.1.4 Carbon Nanoparticles
“Carbon nanomaterials” (CNMs) are materials with various categories based on the
element of carbon. In comparison to metal-based nanomaterials, CNMs are consid-
ered to be safer and more biocompatible (Saleem et al. 2018). Due to their inherent
hydrophobicity, CNMs can load chemical medicines by “stacking” or hydrophobic
interactions, making them effective drug delivery platforms (Ou et al. 2016).
Graphene, fullerenes, carbon nanotubes, carbon nanohorns, carbon quantum dots,
and graphyne (GDY) are among the carbon nanomaterials that have undergone
extensive research in the area of cancer therapy. Graphene is a great substrate for
drug administration, PDT, and PTT because it has a high surface-to-volume ratio and
a high no. of branches that contain oxygen. Studies carried out on animals showed
that CNTs could cause ﬁbrosis, granuloma development, persistent inﬂammation,
and pathophysiology similar to mesothelioma.
102                                                                       L. Iftikhar et al.


5.2.2    Use of Nanocarriers in Cancer Therapy

Chemotherapy drugs are toxic to carcinomas. These medications are hazardous to all
cells, even healthy ones, due to their high toxicity and lack of selectivity. All
medications that are too tiny to ﬁt inside molecules cause this poisonous response.
Almost all parts of the organism are endothelial. With careful management, both the
target area and other unaffected areas can be reached. a sickness that therefore results
in a lot of drug-related side effects. A colloidal nanoparticle system is a potential
tactic that could enhance the therapeutic efﬁcacy of chemotherapeutic medicines
while lowering agents and their negative effects.
   Since these medications are unable to traverse the blood vessel walls of a healthy
person’s system when they are enclosed in nanoparticles of 50–800 nm (the distance
between these cells is just 15–30 nm). This is distinct from what usually occurs.
Instead of the resulting healthy zones, areas that are inﬂamed or even have tumors
will have endothelial cells that are less closely packed together. They further kill the
tumor cells owing to their enhanced penetration and retention effect (EPR) resulting
release of cancer therapeutics inside the tumor (Prabhakar et al. 2013). Allows for the
precise targeting of nanocarriers to particular cancer cells with an active mode of
action based on the expressed molecule on the tumor surface. Drug distribution to
the site of the disease in a precise and effective way is one of the pillars of
nanomedicine. In general, there are numerous ways to accomplish this, such as
transdermal, nasal, and oral administration methods. Most importantly intrave-
nously. However, the encapsulation or association of a certain type of nanoparticles
can frequently boost the efﬁcacy of medications while somewhat reducing their
effectiveness.
   Here, we provide an overview of nanoparticles for cancer imaging and treatment
that have been tried and tested in clinical settings. Many various kinds of
nanoparticles are presently being researched for cancer, according to both clinical
and preclinical investigations. Other kinds of nanoparticles are now in the early
stages of development and could eventually enter the preclinical stage for cancer
imaging and therapy. We want to draw attention to the potential value of diagnostic
nanoparticles among all of these other nanoparticles.


5.2.3    Opportunities and Challenges of Nanoparticles for Cancer
         Therapy

Nanomedicine is a great alternative to traditional cancer treatment, but it doesn’t
overcome all of the limitations of oncology. Nanoparticles used in cancer therapy
can serve as carriers for drugs, nucleic acids, contrast media or enhance the radiation
sensitivity of cells. Signiﬁcant improvements in drug delivery efﬁciency and direct
targeting to tumors have been achieved by using nanoparticles as drug carriers.
However, nanoparticles alone do not signiﬁcantly improve the effectiveness of
chemotherapeutic agents. Most nanomedicines can reduce the toxicity of chemo-
therapy but do not impair the effectiveness of the therapy. Tumors exhibit unique
5   Testing of Nanocarriers Loaded with Chemotherapeutic Agents in the Animal. . .   103


pathophysiological features that differ from normal tissues, such as: B. extensive
angiogenesis, damage to vascular structures and lymphatic drainage. Nanoparticles
exploit these anatomically impaired properties of the tumor and result in signiﬁcant
leakage of the nanoparticles into the tumor tissue. Due to slow vascular reﬂux of into
cancerous tissue and lymphatic clearance, nanoparticles are stored in the tumor. This
is known as improved permeability and retention. Similarly, speciﬁc targeting of can
be achieved by targeting tissues in the vicinity of the tumor (e.g., Kupffer cells in the
liver).
    Nanoparticles can be delivered through multiple routes of drug delivery, such as
oral, injection, intraocular, parenteral, etc. It has been noted that nanoparticles are
better option than microparticles (>1 μm) as carriers of drugs with greater cellular
uptake demonstrated. These features allow ﬁner control of drug targeting and
delivery to malignant tissues (Awasthi et al. 2018). Improving nanoparticle’s capac-
ity to use the immune system to deliver chemotherapy to sensitize lymph nodes,
which will then deliver chemotherapy to certain metastatic locations, is an utmost
need. Hence, the most cutting-edge approach to treating cancer, however, is gene
therapy, which eliminates the illness’s origin by changing the genetic makeup
(Pedziwiatr-Werbicka et al. 2021). Delivery of these experts via a nanometer-scale
carrier will increase their power in a few ways, including by protecting them from
enzymatic contamination and delaying elimination (Nabil et al. 2019).


5.3       Preclinial Testing of Nanocarriers

Preclinical in vivo model systems (animal model) are used to investigate safety and
efﬁcacy underpin advancements in cancer therapy. Animal models include animal
experimental objects established in medical research and similar materials that can
simulate the human body (Li et al. 2021). Workers at laboratories take care of the
animals, promote top-notch research, and meet other particular requirements. Stud-
ies of the tumor microenvironment generally reveal similarities between oncology
treatment models and the genetic landscape of clinical cancer. For biomedical
research, the following animal model is ideal:

• Mimics human disease at the molecular level.
• Is reliable and predictable.
• Makes signiﬁcant differences in survival, and Allows for accurate treatment
  assessment.
• Easily visualized.
• Setting like human disease appearing in the background (Obeid et al. 2018).

   Animal models are classiﬁed according to the method by which cancer is induced
in the animal. The simplest models are those that represent spontaneous tumors.
Although these models mimic natural human diseases, they have not kept pace with
the development of experimental treatment programs. It was therefore necessary to
move away from clinical concerns and take a further step towards artiﬁciality in
104                                                                     L. Iftikhar et al.


search of a satisfactory test method. Cancer can be reproducibly induced in animals
by exposing them to various drugs and now by transplanting tumor cells or tissues
(Phillips and Roth 2019).


5.3.1    Uses of Animal Model

• Animal models are an excellent tool to explore the pathophysiology of human
  diseases, the principles of disease prevention and therapy, and they are a constant
  source of new ideas for cancer research.
• Humans and animal models differ from one another in terms of physiology,
  genetics, and immunity.
• As a result, it is critically important for the advancement of biomedicine to design
  animal models that can take the place of humans in preclinical research and more
  accurately represent human biological traits.
• It is impossible to accurately portray the biological processes of human tumor
  tissue using the cell line-derived xenograft (CDX) model and traditional in vitro
  culture, which reduces the effectiveness of cancer research.
• PDX model is high consistency rate of treatment response allows it to more
  accurately replicate the progression of tumors in people.
• The humanized animal model was created on the basis of this in order to copy the
  physiological or pathological condition of humans.
• Making a range of cancer animal models is now possible thanks to the ongoing
  development and advancement of gene-editing technologies, which has signiﬁ-
  cantly aided the study of cancer.
• An improved model that is more like the human genome is being developed.
• Gene-editing technology will someday allow us to mix several building methods
  to produce a model that is better suited for cancer research.
• One future strategy for using zebra ﬁsh as a model organism of tumor growth and
  progression is to better mimic the tumor environment of the human body (Li et al.
  2021).


5.3.2    Various Animal Models Employed in Cancer Models

5.3.2.1 Canine Cancer Models
Customers’ dogs provide a special chance to research naturally occurring tumors in a
setting that is advantageous to both animals and people. The great genetic variety
throughout the canine population and the many physiologic similarities to humans
make these animals useful as comparative cancer models. Osteosarcoma, soft tissue
sarcoma (STS), lung cancer, oral melanoma, breast cancer, oral squamous cell
carcinoma, nasal tumors, and malignant non-Hodgkin’s lymphoma are among the
most prevalent tumors in dogs. Given the striking similarities, this is perhaps the
ﬁnest cancer model that dogs can provide.
5   Testing of Nanocarriers Loaded with Chemotherapeutic Agents in the Animal. . .   105


    The canine cancer model is distinctive because it displays tumors with various
traits that are similar to those found in humans on its own (for example, large dog
osteosarcoma). The quick study of DNA damage and epigenetic alterations that
build over time and result in carcinogenesis is made possible by the spontaneous
onset and history of cancer, especially given the high degree of genome similarity
observed between the canine and human genomes. Enable it to happen. These
beneﬁts have made it possible for researchers to use canine cancer models to ﬁnd
pertinent genetic changes and cancer-causing factors that are present in human
tumors. Canine participants also skip the clinical trial phase, hastening the process
of developing new drugs. Utilizing canine cancer models, numerous medicines have
undergone preclinical research. A medication called resiniferatoxin works as an
agonist for the pain brought on by bone cancer since dogs have a very strong
tendency to injure themselves when they are in agony. There are a number of
problems with using canines to simulate human cancer. Canine cancer models
often incorporate lymphoid and sarcoma tumor types rather than carcinomas. Studies
on the development of anticancer drugs in dogs are not necessarily applicable to
people because humans and dogs react to drugs differently (Wu et al. 2023).

5.3.2.2 Patient-Derived Xenograft (PDX) Model
Greek word xenos, which meaning foreign, is where the phrase “xenograft”
originates. It is taken out of one organism and inserted into another. Organs, tissues,
or living cells are used in these transplants, which are often carried out on mice with
immunocompetent immune systems. Xenografts are employed in the ﬁeld of cancer
research to tackle signiﬁcant studies that rely on using animal models that closely
mirror the tumor growth of human patients. In order to preserve the original or
primary tumor properties, xenograft models with primary carcinoma tissue are
constructed using very few transit numbers, for instance, fewer than 10 passes
discharged from human patients. Cell heterogeneity, clinical biomolecular markers,
malignant genotypes and phenotypes, tumor structure, and vasculature are a few of
these characteristics. In order to better understand the molecular cancer biology
pertinent to human cancer and how patients respond to cancer therapy, the PDX
models show enhanced preclinical investigations. Depends on the assumptions you
make. PDX models have proven useful for.
• Studies of cancer metastasis and drug inhibition.
• Individualized dosing and therapy.
• Preclinical studies and the identiﬁcation of new anticancer medication candidates
  are growing (Liu et al. 2023).

   Surgery or biopsy techniques are used to collect and preserve primary or meta-
static tumors as tissue structures. Using patient-derived tumor tissue xenograft
models to study anticancer drugs and treatment options can result from this progres-
sive development of tumor specimens in immune-deﬁcient mice.
   Primary or metastatic tumors are gathered and kept as tissue structures by surgical
or biopsy techniques. The investigation of anticancer medicines and therapies may
switch to using xenograft models of tumor tissue from patients after resection of this
106                                                                    L. Iftikhar et al.


tumor specimen in immunocompromised mice. The subcutaneous region (dorsal
region) is the most well-known location for tumor implantation in mice. Other
locations include the pancreas, brain, oral cavity, ovary, breast, etc. In order to
boost the rate of engraftment, numerous methods and initiatives have been used to
implant tumor in the renal capsular site (Liu et al. 2023).
   PDX models are used to identify possible therapeutic efﬁcacy biomarkers as well
as to organize potential clinical symptoms. Cetuximab does not alter Kirsten rat
sarcoma (KRAS)-mutant PDX models of colorectal cancer, according to numerous
testings. This is shown in the ovarian malignant tumor in particular, where exposure
to cisplatin causes the beginning of cisplatin resistance in a platinum-sensitive
animal, exactly as was shown in a clinical situation. Breast cancer PDXs models
serve as a useful model for doing translational research since they mimic various
facets of the biology of the tumor (Rivera et al. 2021). However, due to their unique
tumor microenvironment and inability to be genetically modiﬁed or to integrate the
immune system because they are created in immunodeﬁcient mice, patient-derived
xenografts have limitations. They are not appropriate for low-throughput drug
screening and immunomodulatory assays for cancer prevention, initiation, and
progression of cancer gene modeling.

5.3.2.3 Mouse Models
The study of carcinogenesis and the provision of experimental insights that other
systems are unable to supply have greatly beneﬁted from the use of human cancer
mouse models. Even while there are differences between mice and humans, current
models, for instance, can more accurately simulate sporadic human malignancies by
carefully controlling the timing and location of mutations, even within single cell.
Therefore, mice offer a model system that can be used in experiments and have a
variety of research tools established to understand the fundamental causes of cancer.
Because tumors form from scratch in the context of a healthy immune system and
co-evolve with the surrounding stroma, many of these problems can be addressed
using mice that have been genetically altered to have cell-speciﬁc human cancer-
associated defects. De novo mouse cancer models are being used to provide preclin-
ical direction for human therapeutic trials. In these methods, immune-compromised
mice at the orthotopic site are transplanted with human critical tumors, as well as
mice that have been “humanized” by receiving transplanted human hematopoietic
and other organ frameworks. By creating constitutive or conditional erasures within
the quality, “knockout” approaches are most frequently used in loss-of-function
investigations to remove the quality of interest. The primary few exons that encode
the start codon are typically sufﬁcient to stop translation or interpretation into a
useful protein item for traits that span large genomic areas.
    In order to generate systems where genetic processes can be precisely controlled
both geographically and temporally, complex conditional genetic engineering tech-
nology has been developed to go beyond conventional knockout constraints. Site-
speciﬁc recombinases, such as yeast FLP and bacterial Cre, catalyze accurate
recombination between speciﬁed 34 bploxP and FRT sites, respectively. Therefore,
in the presence of Cre or FLP protein synthesis, homologous recombination between
5   Testing of Nanocarriers Loaded with Chemotherapeutic Agents in the Animal. . .   107


loxP or FRT sites that ﬂank the gene of interest and are oriented in the same direction
is activated, recombining out the ﬂanked genetic sequence and deleting the gene of
interest. Then, it is possible to control the deletion of the target gene by controlling
the expression of the recombinase throughout time and space, overcoming interfer-
ence from lethality and developmental defects. To conditionally knockout the target
gene in a speciﬁc tissue or cell type, such as progenitor cells, or at speciﬁc times
during development, mice carrying the target gene ﬂanked by loxP or FRT sites are
crossed with mice carrying the Cre or the FLP recombinase.
    Using genetic techniques adapted from Drosophila systems, sporadic single-cell
knockout mouse models have been created. Recombination-enabling enzymes.
Under tissue-speciﬁc and temporal conditions, can be used to promote uncommon
G2 mitotic interchromosomal recombination events between FRT or loxP sites on
homologous mouse chromosomes, resulting in the production of a wild-type and a
mutant daughter cell within a population of heterozygous cells. In the cell cycle’s
G2, homologous chromosomes line up to prepare for mitosis. The Cre or FLP
recombinase can recombine homologues when they are aligned to make certain
chromosomal regions homozygous in the daughter cells. Gain-of-function assays are
often used to investigate oncogenes in mouse models. To learn how an oncogene
promotes cancer in vivo, transgenic or knockout animals that are constitutively
overexpress the oncogene can be employed.
    The Rosa26 region is widely used as an insertion site for knockin mice since it is
devoid of any essential genes and provides for the best transgene expression.
Transgenic animals may have many insertions of the transgene, whereas knocking
animals only have one copy of the transgene. Knockin approaches can also be used
to replace a normal gene copy with a mutant version of the gene in order to examine
speciﬁc mutational events in the context of the normal control of the gene (Proetzel
et al. 2014).

5.3.2.4 Transgenic Mouse Models (of Breast Cancer): The MMTV
          Paradigm
Taking advantage of the common preference of tumor-inducing infections known to
produce distinct types of cancer easily in mice was one way to achieve tissue-speciﬁc
regulation of oncogene expression. The mouse mammary tumor infection (MMTV),
a retrovirus known as the “milk agent” that is transmitted from mother to pup and is
responsible for developing mammary tumors in susceptible strains of innate mice, is
a crucial example of such an infection. Years of MMTV research led to the discovery
that the virus contains a regulatory DNA structure called the long terminal rehash
(LTR) that promotes steroid-hormone-inducible transcription. When the MMTV
provirus is inserted into the murine genome with a proto-oncogene, it imparts
overexpression on mammary epithelial cells that respond to hormonal stimulation
during pregnancy and lactation, leading to mammary tumors in female mice with
MMTV infection. Using the provirus coordinates as a “tag,” the analysis of MMTV
inclusion destinations led to the disclosure of new oncogenes. However, the tissue-
speciﬁcity of MMTV-driven tumorigenesis, the hormone-inducible nature of onco-
gene expression in these tumors, and the characterization of the transcriptional
108                                                                   L. Iftikhar et al.


enhancer responsible for this additionally displayed a tremendous opportunity for
the design of novel transgenic mouse models of cancer.
   After discovering the potential value of the MMTV-LTR for breast cancer
research, Phil Leder made an attempt to employ this viral enhancer to create the
ﬁrst transgenic mouse models of this illness. By this time, it was known that the
injection of a MMTV-H-Ras vector could trigger the production of hormone-
induced oncogenes in cultured cells. After that, Leder and Timothy Stewart, a
postdoctoral fellow in his lab, used pronuclear microinjection to produce transgenic
mice that expressed the oncogene Myc under the control of the MMTV-LTR. These
MMTV-Myc animals underwent careful inspection, and it was discovered that
female mice from two of the founder lines underwent Myc-driven mammary
tumor development following numerous nursing and pregnancy cycles that induced
the expression of the MMTV-LTR. This was deﬁning once more.
   The MMTV-Myc and MMTV-Ras mice were crossed in this work, leading to an
acceleration of carcinogenesis and the ﬁrst in vivo evidence of oncogene collabora-
tion. In 1988, Harold Varmus’ group released the MMTV-Int1 (now referred to as
MMTV-Wnt1) model, establishing this proviral insertion site as a legitimate proto-
oncogene capable of causing breast cancers. This extraordinary fusion of molecular
oncology, MMTV virology, and novel transgenic technologies. In addition, the
MMTV-LTR was used to show the effectiveness of the oncogene ErbB2, which is
ampliﬁed and overexpressed in 20% of human breast tumors (Slamon et al. 1987), to
transform the mouse mammary epithelium. For mechanical studies of mammary
gland transformation by this crucial oncogene and therapeutic target, transgenic
mouse models of ErbB2-driven breast cancer have proven invaluable. These models
have grown in complexity and their capacity to accurately model the human disease
over time. The MMTV-LTR is still an essential tool for the in vivo validation of
possible breast cancer onco-genes, and many of the initial MMTV-based breast
cancer mice models are still in use. To develop transgenic mice, endogenous
promoters that are speciﬁc to the mouse mammary epithelium have also been
characterized as a result of research from other. One example of a promoter in this
group is the whey acidic protein (Wap) gene. A mild phenotype with alveolar
hyperplasias was produced when oncogenic H- Ras was produced from this
hormone-dependent promoter, in contrast to the MMTV-driven Ras model, which
normally proceeds to adenocarcinoma. The shape and phenotype of these cancers
differ dramatically despite the nearly complete penetrance of breast tumors brought
on by Wap-promoted My expression during numerous cycles of pregnancy and
lactation.

5.3.2.5 Zebrafish Model
As a new model system for cancer research, tumors have been implanted in
zebraﬁsh. Both laboratory cell lines and actual cancer cells extracted from patients
were injected. Traditional patient-derived xenografts (PDXs) are created from cancer
cells or masses that have been surgically removed from patients following biopsy or
excision. These PDXs are then implanted into a model animal, like immunodeﬁcient
mice (Keller and Murtha 2004; Patton et al. 2005; Zon and Peterson 2005; Barros
5   Testing of Nanocarriers Loaded with Chemotherapeutic Agents in the Animal. . .   109


et al. 2008; Marques et al. 2009; Smith et al. 2010; Eguiara et al. 2011; Nguyen et al.
2012; Dai et al. 2014; Tang et al. 2014; Mandelbaum et al. 2018; Choo et al. 2019).
The zebraﬁsh model is a low-cost, high-throughput animal model that can be used to
study the mechanisms behind tumor formation, metastasis, and therapeutic response
as well as forecast the efﬁcacy of personalized cancer treatment (Chen et al. 2021).
    One of the best models at the moment is the zebraﬁsh cancer model, a vertebrate
model that has gained popularity recently. Zebraﬁsh genomes are conservative and
similar to those of humans, providing a strong basis for research into the origins of
various cancers. The zebraﬁsh model offers various distinct beneﬁts over the most
used mouse models for cancer research, including:

• Rapid reproduction, cheap cost, and tiny size.
• Clear embryos.
• Zebraﬁsh that are transgenic or immunodeﬁcient can stay transparent even after
  they reach adulthood. Real-time visualization and tracking of cancer cell growth,
  spread, and metastasis are useful.
• Because zebraﬁsh is fertilized in vitro, the transgenic animal model can be made
  fast and the gene operation is not too difﬁcult. With the aid of genetic engineering,
  gene editing, xenotransplantation, drug-induced toxic damage, and other
  methods, numerous zebraﬁsh cancer models have currently been created
  (Fig. 5.2) (Li et al. 2021; Veldman and Lin 2008). Nanoparticles-Based
  Oligonucleotides Delivery in Cancer: Role of Zebraﬁsh as Animal Model Oligo-
  nucleotide (ON) therapies are chemically produced DNA or RNA molecules that
  can be used as molecular target agents to suppress protein or gene expression. The
  capacity of ON compounds to cross the plasma membrane may depend on their
  chemical makeup. Zebraﬁsh (ZF) models could increase the range of possibilities
  for evaluating the efﬁcacy of the proposed therapeutic techniques in useful in vivo




Fig. 5.2 Animal Model for Genetic Manipulation (Credit: Proetzel et al. 2014)
110                                                                     L. Iftikhar et al.


   models, which is still a crucial need. The main characteristics of ZF models and
   the advantages and disadvantages of using them in the creation of ON-based
   therapy strategies are also covered.


5.4      Hu-PBL Model

Immunodeﬁcient mice were injected with human peripheral blood cells to create the
Hu-PBL model. It is currently the most simple and economical humanized mouse
model available. The hu-PBL model is currently being used in numerous areas of
cancer research, including investigations into malignancies of the thyroid, thyroid
gland, breast, nasopharynx, and other cancers. Because it can reproduce numerous T
cells, as opposed to the hu-HSC model, it is the ideal paradigm for the study of
mature effectors T cells. This model is prone to graft-versus-host disease (GVHD),
which shortens research time and diminishes mouse endurance due to the immune
system’s rejection of human T cells and mouse cells (Li et al. 2021).


5.5      Toxicities of Nanocarriers

Magnesium oxide nanoparticles (MgO NPs) are becoming more widely known for
their uses in cancer treatment methods such nano-cryotherapy and hyperthermia.
The current study showed how normal lung ﬁbroblast cells and several malignant
cell types responded to magnesium oxide nanoparticles (MgO NPs) in terms of
cytotoxicity (van Zandwijk and Frank 2019). Inﬂammation of lungs and cardiac
problems may be side effects of inhaled nanoparticles. Regardless of their level of
oxidation, copper- and zinc-based nanomaterials exhibit the highest levels of toxicity
among any nanoparticles examined till now. Current drugs based on nanotechnol-
ogy, such as Abraxane and Doxil, include side effects, such as diarrhea, nausea, and
weight loss. However, these problems can be caused by the chemotherapeutic drugs
they contain. Currently available drugs based on nanotechnology, such as Abraxane
and Doxil, include side effects, such as diarrhea, nausea, and weight loss. However,
these problems can be brought on by the chemotherapeutic drugs they contain.


5.6      Safety Profiling of Nanoparticles

Cancer is a devastating disease k, affecting millions of people worldwide. While
there have been signiﬁcant advancements in cancer treatment over the years, there is
still much to be done. One promising area of research is nanoparticles and their use
in cancer treatment. This has the potential to completely change how we handle this
illness. However, with great promise comes great responsibility. The safety of these
nanoparticles must be thoroughly studied and understood before they can be used in
clinical settings. This is where safety proﬁling comes into play. By understanding the
potential risks associated with nanoparticles, we can develop safer and more
5   Testing of Nanocarriers Loaded with Chemotherapeutic Agents in the Animal. . .   111


effective treatments for cancer. Nanoparticles are advantageous in cancer therapy
because they can speciﬁcally target cancer cells while sparing healthy cells. The
possibility of toxicity and the difﬁculty of transporting nanoparticles to particular
parts of the body are two possible disadvantages, though.


5.6.1     Importance of Safety Profiling

Safety proﬁling of nanoparticles is critical in cancer therapy because it ensures that
the treatment is effective and safe for patients. Without proper safety proﬁling, there
is a risk of harmful side effects and potential damage to healthy cells. For example, in
a recent study, researchers found that certain types of nanoparticles used in cancer
therapy had toxic effects on human liver cells. This highlights the need for rigorous
safety proﬁling to prevent harm to patients. Additionally, statistics show that adverse
reactions to nanoparticles are on the rise, making safety proﬁling even more crucial.


5.6.2     Current Methods of Safety Profiling

Currently, there are several methods used for safety proﬁling of nanoparticles in
cancer therapy. One common method is in vitro testing, which involves testing the
toxicity of nanoparticles on cell cultures. This method allows researchers to know
about the toxic effects of nanoparticles on healthy cells before they are tested on
humans. Another method is in vivo testing, which involves testing the toxicity of
nanoparticles on living organisms. This method provides fair representation of the
toxic effects of nanoparticles on humans. To see how nanoparticles and cells interact,
researchers also employ imaging methods including atomic force microscopy
(AFM) and transmission electron microscopy (TEM). These methods help
researchers better understand the behavior of nanoparticles in biological systems
and the possible hazardous impacts they may have.


5.6.3     Challenges and Limitations

One of the major challenges in safety proﬁling of nanoparticles in cancer therapy is
their potential toxicity. While nanoparticles have shown promise in delivering drugs
directly to cancer cells, they can also cause harm to healthy cells if not properly
targeted. This is why it’s important to develop methods that can accurately predict
the toxicity of nanoparticles before they are used in clinical trials. The use of
computer models, which can assist researchers in identifying potential hazardous
effects of nanoparticles without the requirement for animal testing, is one approach
to this problem.
    Another challenge is the lack of standardized methods for safety proﬁling.
Currently, different labs may use different techniques to evaluate the safety of
nanoparticles, making it difﬁcult to compare results across studies. To address this
112                                                                                  L. Iftikhar et al.


challenge, there is a need for more collaboration between researchers and regulatory
agencies to establish standardized protocols for safety proﬁling. This will ensure that
the results of safety studies are reliable and can be used to inform clinical decision-
making.


5.6.4     Future Directions

As we look toward the future of safety proﬁling for nanoparticles in cancer therapy,
there are several exciting possibilities on the horizon. The use of machine learning
techniques to analyze massive datasets and spot potential safety issues before they
become a problem is one of the most promising areas of research. By leveraging the
power of artiﬁcial intelligence, we can streamline the safety proﬁling process and
make it more efﬁcient than ever before. Another area of research that shows great
promise is the development of new imaging techniques that allow us to visualize
nanoparticles inside the body in real-time. This will enable us to better understand
how nanoparticles interact with different tissues and organs, and could lead to more
targeted and effective cancer therapies in the future.

Patients and Clinical Trials

Firdos Alam Khan




   Abstract

   The materials which possess biocompatibility, better tumor targeting, and lesser
   toxicity are the most desirable nanomaterials for clinical applications. Some of the
   best-studied nanomaterials are metallic nanomaterials, polymeric nanomaterials
   or other organic and inorganic nanomaterials which showed promising
   applications in cancer diagnosis and cancer therapy. As per the report which
   was published in the 2016, there are 25 different types of nanomaterials-based
   carriers that are approved by the US Food and Drug Administration (FDA) or
   European Medicines Agency (EMA) for the clinical applications. In addition,
   more than 45 other nanoparticle-based nanocarriers that were approved by the
   USFDA and EMA but were presently being assessed in different phases of
   clinical trials. The nano-based drug carriers are tested to examine how anti-
   cancer drugs can be effectively targeted cancer tissues. There are four different
   phases (I, II, III, and IV) of clinical trials of testing nano-based carriers. In these
   past 3 years, several nanomedicine-based products are approved by US-FDA and
   EMA for their application in cancer diagnosis and cancer therapy. It is estimated
   that more than 75 new trials are under progress in different phases of clinical
   trials.

   Keywords

   Nanocarriers · Nanocarriers-loaded chemotherapeutic agents · Cancer patients ·
   Clinical trials

6.1        Clinical Trials and Different Phases

There are four different phases (I, II, III, and IV) of clinical trials of testing nano-
based carriers (Fig. 6.1). The application of nanomaterials in biomedical applications
has attracted many researchers to develop special nanomaterials for clinical
applications (Khan 2020a). One of the main attributes of the nanomaterials to be
used as drug carriers is safety and better tumor targeting. The materials which
possess biocompatibility, better tumor targeting, and lesser toxicity are the most
desirable nanomaterials for clinical applications (Khan 2020b). Some of the best-
studied nanomaterials are liposomes, polymeric, micelles, albumin-bound
nanoparticles, and inorganic nanoparticles (Anselmo and Mitragotri 2019). There
are about 45 different non-approved nanoparticles used in 80 different trials in
various patients associated with radiation exposure, arthritis, pneumonia, amyloid-
osis, hepatitis, and ﬁbrosis (Anselmo and Mitragotri 2019). It has been reported that
out of 80 clinical trials, 28 trials have been successfully completed and 12 trials were
terminated early due to failure (Anselmo and Mitragotri 2019). There are various
application of different types of nanomaterials in clinical condition for example,
these trials were used for tumor targeting. The main objective of the clinical trials is
to examine whether prepared nanomaterials or nanocarriers possess desirable
capabilities for cancer treatments in the different types of patients and at the same
time also examine any side-effects or harmful effects associated with nanocarriers. It
is very necessary to choose those nanomaterials which possess potential anti-cancer
capabilities but also produce less harmful effects on human subjects. In order to start




Fig. 6.1 Illustration of different phases of clinical trials
6   Testing of Nanocarriers Loaded with Chemotherapeutic Agents:. . .                 117


any clinical trials, it is necessary to evaluate the anticancer properties of nanocarriers
in in vitro as well as in in vivo (animal) studies. Once in vitro and animal studies
showed very good results with effective anticancer properties and less cytotoxic
effects, then nanomaterials or nanocarriers may be taken for the clinical trials.
Another important aspects of clinical trials is that prepared nanomaterials or
nanocarriers should be produced as per the Good Laboratory Practice (GLP)
guidelines for the pre-clinical trials (animal studies) and for the clinical trials
nanomaterials or nanocarriers should be prepared as per the Good Manufacturing
Practice (GMP) guidelines. To produce GLP and GMP grade nanomaterials, the
facilities and staff who are involved in the production should be trained and
approved by the respective country government agencies such as FDA is the
organization which responsible GLP and GMP guidelines in the USA.


6.2       Application of Nanocarriers in Anticancer Drug Delivery

The application of different shapes, sizes, and types of nanomaterials (Fig. 6.2) have
been involved in the production of nanocarriers and based on the published papers
which are available in PubMed, NIH clinical trial sites, we have gathered the
information and have described the individual type of nanomaterials for their
application as the nanocarriers delivery the anti-cancer agents or drug or molecules
for both diagnostic and therapy purposes.




Fig. 6.2 Application of different types of nanomaterials in cancer treatment
118                                                                        F. A. Khan


6.2.1     Poly (Lactic-Co-Glycolic Acid) (PLGA) Nanocarriers

The application of Poly(lactic-co-glycolic acid) (PLGA)-nanoparticles in the devel-
opment of nanocarriers has attracted many researchers as they possessed many
advantages such better biocompatibility and better drug conjugation capabilities
(Fig. 6.3) and with low cytotoxicity (Creemers et al. 2021; Kumari et al. 2010). It
has been reported that nanoparticle which encapsulated antigens are taken up more
efﬁciently by dendritic cells (Foged et al. 2002). In addition, it has been observed
that PLGA-encapsulated peptides produced impact on major histocompatibility
complex classes I and II molecules that produced functional and tumor-antigen
speciﬁc (CD8+) and (CD4+) T-cell responses as found in the pre-clinical studies
(Waeckerle-Men and Groettrup 2005). The PLGA can be easily and rapidly
internalized in the myeloid cells after systemic injection. The administration of
PLGA nanocarriers contribute to disturb macrophage divergence for successful
anticancer action (Reichel et al. 2019; Feng et al. 2019). The PLGA has also been
used in the clinical conditions, where PLGA-encapsulated component which target
the immune response, is the called as cancer-testis antigen NY-ESO-1 have been
many advanced cancers lung, melanoma, bladder, prostate, ovarian, esophageal,
breast, and gastric (Gnjatic et al. 2006; Caballero and Chen 2009; Sharma et al.
2003; Sugita et al. 2004; Vaughan et al. 2004; Iura et al. 2017; Gure et al. 2005;
Tessari et al. 2018). The application of nanoparticles in drug encapsulation studied
using long and short peptides (85–111, 117–143 and 157–165), which combined
cover more than 80% of the European population for both class I and class II Human
Leukocyte Antigen (HLA) alleles (IEDB 2019).
    The application of NY-ESO-1-speciﬁc peptides as produced as per GMP
guidelines have shown promising results in the cancer patients (Baumgaertner
et al. 2016; Sabbatini et al. 2012; Chen et al. 2015; Odunsi et al. 2012). In one of
the ﬁrst human trials, immunomodulatory nanomedicines based on the PLGA
nanoparticles encapsulated with tumor-antigen NY-ESO-1 and invariant natural
killer T (iNKT) cell activator IMM60 showed profound anticancer activity when
they were administered intravenously (Creemers et al. 2021).




Fig. 6.3 Anti-cancer drug delivery by liposome conjugated nanoparticles
6   Testing of Nanocarriers Loaded with Chemotherapeutic Agents:. . .            119


6.2.2     Magnetic Nanoparticle-Based Nanocarriers

Magnetic nanoparticles such as super-para-magnetic iron oxide nanoparticles
(SPIONs) are one of the most extensively studied targeted nanomaterials for cancer
treatment. The SPIONs have been used in drug delivery (Schleich et al. 2014;
Wallyn et al. 2019), magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) (Hour et al. 2020; Du
et al. 2020), and cellular-speciﬁc targeting (Ni et al. 2020), and with prolonged
blood circulation (Ryu et al. 2014). It has been reported that SPIONs store in the
tumor site through higher permeability under an external magnetic ﬁeld (Zou et al.
2010). The application of SPIONs have been improved by the addition of folic acid
(Montazerabadi et al. 2019; Shaghaghi et al. 2019), transferrin (Wang et al. 2020) to
improve their targeting performance. SPIONs have been progressively studied for
their excellent superparamagnetic, magnetic heating properties, and for enhanced
magnetic resonance imaging (MRI). To deliver the drugs or anti-cancer agents to the
cancer tissues, SPIONs can be conjugated with anti-cancer drugs and this nano
delivery system has several beneﬁts such as better magnetic targeted
functionalization, cancer imaging, magnetic thermotherapy, and combined delivery
of anti-cancer agents. There are few magnetic nanomaterial-based nanocarriers
loaded with drugs were assessed in patients and did not produce convincing results,
leading to termination of clinical trials of Phase II and III (Zhi et al. 2020).


6.2.3     Plant-Based Nanocarriers

There are numerous plant-based anticancer nanoformulations have been approved
by the US-FDA, and there about 12 formulations are in different stages of clinical
trials (Dhupal and Chowdhury 2020). Plant-based molecules are conjugated with
anti-neoplastic drugs that can easily diffuse through the cell membrane escaping
immune defense mechanism is known as passive targeting. It has been reported that
plant-based-nanocomposites circulates through the cancer cell cytoplasm, diffused
into intracellular organelles inducing oxidative stress and loss of mitochondrial
membrane potential that led to apoptosis in cancer cells (Dhupal and Chowdhury
2020). The application of nanoparticles-based drug carrier having anti-neoplastic
agents plus phyto-molecules have transformed conventional cancer therapy greatly
advancing clinical practice. We have summarized the various plant-based molecules
which are conjugated with anticancer drug to be used for the clinical applications as
approved by US-FDA (www.ClinicalTrials.gov) (Table 6.1).
    We have listed clinical trials using in cancer patients with nano-formulations as
approved by US-FDA (www.ClinicalTrials.gov) (Table 6.2).

6.4       FDA Approved Nano-Based Materials for Cancer

There are few nanomedicine-based products which are approved and regulated by
the US-FDA (Patra et al. 2018). In addition, there are also number of nano-based
drug molecules which are under various phases of clinical trials for cancer diagnosis
and cancer therapy. There 18 different types of nano-based drug delivery materials
have been approved for chemotherapeutics applications; 15 were approved for
antimicrobial agents and 28 were approved for different medical applications such
as psychological diseases, autoimmune (Patra et al. 2018). The list of US-FDA
approved nano-based materials is given in Table 6.2.

6.5      Conclusion

There are many useful applications of nanomaterials especially in drug delivery as
the nanocarriers. One of the main advantages of the nano-based drug delivery system
is better biodegradability, and better biocompatibility with low cell toxicity. Some of
the best-studied nanomaterials are metallic nanomaterials, polymeric nanomaterials
or other organic and inorganic nanomaterials which showed promising applications
in cancer diagnosis, and cancer therapy. As per the report which was published in
2016, there are 25 different types of nanomaterials-based carriers that are approved
by the Food and Drug Administration (FDA) or European Medicines Agency
(EMA) for the clinical applications. In addition, more than 45 other nanoparticle-
based nanocarriers that were approved by the FDA and EMA but were presently
being assessed in different phases of clinical trials. The nano-based drug carriers are
tested to examine how anti-cancer drugs can be effectively targeted cancer tissues. It
6   Testing of Nanocarriers Loaded with Chemotherapeutic Agents:. . .                          133


is estimated that more than 75 new trials are under progress in different phases of
clinical trials. There regulatory mechanisms for nanomedicine-based products are
constantly improving where safety and toxicity issues are taken seriously for the
development of nanomedicine products.

FDA Approval, Commercialization                                                                7
of Nanocarriers, and Global Market

Firdos Alam Khan




   Abstract

   There is an increasing interest in the nanomaterial-based products especially for
   their applications in the biomedical ﬁelds. These nanomaterials possess small size
   together with astonishing physicochemical properties which makes most suitable
   for the therapeutic applications. Different types of nanomaterials such as metallic,
   carbon-based, ﬂuorescent, and polymer-based which have been used in the tissue
   engineering, drug delivery, and in other therapeutic applications. In spite of many
   useful applications, nanomaterials possess some harmful effects on human body,
   vegetation, and the environment. Nanomaterials can enter cells or tissues and
   organs because of their nano-size, large surface area, and better penetrability
   which may cause serious health issues (inhibition of cell proliferation, cell wall
   damage, modiﬁcation of function and morphology of organelles, oxidative stress,
   genotoxicity, and cell death) in living organisms including humans. In this
   chapter we have aimed to provide the negative or harmful effects of the
   nanomaterials as per published research papers. The topics related to
   nanomaterial’s toxicity, mode of action, and penetration of nanomaterials in the
   human body are discussed. We have also discussed the government regulations,
   ethical issues related to nanomaterials toxicity, and possible health hazards.

   Keywords

   Health and environmental hazards · Synthesis of nanocarriers · Nano-drug
   delivery · Respiratory diseases · Government regulations · Ethical issues

7.1      Commercialization of Nanomedicine Products

The commercialization is the process of transforming designs or ideas into products
for human consumption especially for biomedical applications. The commercializa-
tion of any product demands the big investment and also needed demand for product
in the market and hence it is very necessary to know the demand of the products for
particular disease conditions in the particular area of country or at the global level
(Craig et al. 2013; Sreeramana and Shubhrajyotsna 2016). The commercial value of
the product is based on the novelty of the product and its requirements such as
market size, and the current economic situation (Rogers 1995). The process of
commercialization of nanomedicine products is shown in Fig. 7.1. The product
development process has approximately 10 steps, which starts with generation of
idea, and idea can be used for ﬁnding solution to the problem. Once solution is
identiﬁed then research is started to generate data for proof of concept, once proof of
concepts showed positive results, then reach to patent ofﬁce of the organization to
start the ﬁling the disclosure of invention. The patent ofﬁce will assess the invention
and if they ﬁnd the application fulﬁlls the patenting requirements, they will ﬁle
application to US patent ofﬁce or patent ofﬁce of the respective country. The US
patent ofﬁce will examine the patent application, if everything is satisﬁed, they will
grant the patent. Once the patent is granted, the investors will start commercializa-
tion of the product by themselves provided they sufﬁcient funding or licensing the
patent to third party with the exchange of monitory beneﬁts. The product develop-
ment process start with development of Good Laboratory Practices (GLP) and Good
Manufacturing Practices (GMP) facilities and regulations and these facilities should
be approved by the USFDA or FDA of the respective country. Then preclinical and




Fig. 7.1 The illustration showing the process of nanomedicine product development
7   Nanocarriers-Based Products in the Market, FDA Approval, Commercialization. . .   139


clinical trials will start and once all trials are successful, the products will be
approved by USFDA or FDA of the respective country for the large production
and will be used for the human consumptions.


7.2       Process of Commercialization Nanomedicine Products

7.2.1     Identification of Problem

Any organization will start commercialization of the product when they have market
survey data that show that there is sufﬁcient and consistent demand for the product in
the market. For example, in cancer therapy what are problems are associated with
currently available treatments, and how these problems can be overcome by the
innovative approach.


7.2.2     Finding Solution

Once problem is identiﬁed, then efforts should be directed toward ﬁnding the
commercially viable solution, for example, the cancer can be better treated with
nanomaterials because of their small size, better surface area, better bioavailability,
and better tissue targeting capabilities. The organization may do technological
feasibility study of producing such products.


7.2.3     Research and Invention

The research work will start to bring the data that support idea where new product
possess many useful features what was initially expected to have. This is called proof
of concept stage.


7.2.4     Disclosure of Invention

Once proof of concept is ﬁnalized, the organization will ﬁle a disclosure of invention
to the organization’s patent ofﬁce.


7.2.5     Assessment of Invention

US Patent ofﬁce will examine the invention and will examine the features of the
products, reproducibility, and reliability and uniqueness of the products.
140                                                                           F. A. Khan


7.2.6    Issuing of Patent

If patent fulﬁlls all the requirements, patent ofﬁce will issue a patent which will
protect the inventors for 20 years.


7.2.7    Licensing of Patented Technology

This is commercial decision to make by the organization where technology can be
either transferred to the third part in return of money beneﬁts or keep themselves for
the product development process.


7.2.8    Preclinical and Clinical Trials

The nanomedicine product must be tested ﬁrst in the animals which is called ad
preclinical trial, and then tested in the humans in Phase I, Phase II, and Phase III for
checking the effectiveness and safety of the nanomaterials.


7.2.9    Approval of the Product

Once all the trials are successful as per the USFDA or FDA of respective country,
they will produce in large scale as per the market demand as per GMP requirements.


7.2.10 Marketing of Product

The nanomedicine product will be send to Hospitals for the human consumption
with necessary packaging, transportation and storage condition as approved by
USFDA or FDA of the respective country.


7.3      Nanomaterials as Commercial Products

The ﬁrst commercialization of nanomaterials in the medical ﬁeld has happened
where metallic oxide nanomaterials, zinc oxide and titanium dioxide showed
promising results, but the application of silver nanoparticles has been
commercialized used in treating microbial infections many years ago (Hobson
2009). In addition, the application of silver nanomaterials has been effective in the
treatment of chronic wound-care market where this product is used in wound
dressing to combat microbial infections (Shahverdi et al. 2007; Vlachou et al.
2007). The commercial applications of nano-based products showing remarkable
success as many big pharmaceutical companies are now catching up and started
investing in the nano-based product development businesses especially in the
7   Nanocarriers-Based Products in the Market, FDA Approval, Commercialization. . .   141


development of product using nano-formulation in the form of liposomes,
neo-somes, and nano-somes, respectively (Nohynek et al. 2007). The availability
of nano-based products for both diagnostic and therapeutic applications showing
continuous surge in the global markets as pharmaceutical and biotechnology
companies are investing in the research and development of nano-based research.
The application of nano-based product is not only applied to cancer diagnosis and
therapy area but also used in the other medical conditions such as ophthalmology,
gastroenterology, dermatology, dentistry, where nanomaterials have been applied.
   The renowned Wall Street analysts, Andy Kessler has said that ﬁeld of nanotech-
nology is already entering a signiﬁcantly advanced state of product development
(Hobson 2009). The ability of antibodies to link or conjugate with and other active
biomolecules to make silicon microchips for nano-bio-electronic detectors outside of
the body (Hobson 2009). The biobusiness of nano-based products for medical
applications is complex affairs with lot of risk factors such as nanomedicine products
may fail any point of clinical trials, where companies may face huge economical
loss. With compared to other well-established pharmaceuticals companies, the ﬁeld
of nanomedicine is still emerging and face many challenges as the FDA regulations
is not fully developed to safe guard for both patients and investors. We ﬁnd
considerable difference in the level of investment toward commercialization world-
wide, as European Union lagging behind the US and Japan in terms of public
funding and patents application. There is very low level about 3.5% of global
venture capital is invested in the European countries, creating concerns for regional
commercialization of nanomedicine products. A scarcity of funding or low invest-
ment is the principal cause for this difference of commercialization opportunities for
nanomedicine products. The business pattern of the nanomedicine products is
different than other business models and various constituents of business models
of nanomedicine product is illustrated in Fig. 7.1.
   The funds for nanomedicine product development may come either from with
small investors or from big companies who have infrastructure and resources to
develop the products. With compared to non-medical products, the nanomedicine
products are complex affairs with unpredictable risk factors associated with them.
The associated risk factors such as safety and efﬁcacy of the nanomedicine product
during the clinical trials may always pose serious risk, as nanomedicine may fail
during the clinical trials, where companies may face huge ﬁnancial loss. To avoid
such a huge ﬁnancial loss, the companies may seriously look into data obtained from
non-clinical or animal trials and phase I of clinical trials and if data is showing
profound results with regard to safety and efﬁcacy of the products, then they can
decide to carry the trial further or discontinue it. Another issue with nanomedicine
product is approval of FDA which is complex and time consuming, because FDA
will not approve any products, if products does not comply the guidelines of the
FDA with sound proof that product is safe and effective for human consumption.
142                                                                                 F. A. Khan


7.4       Global Market of Nanomedicine Products

The global market dynamics for the nanomedicine products has been increasing due
to successful development of new and novel nanomedicine products. The
nanomedicine market size (Fig. 7.2) was valued at $ 172.8 Billion in 2021 and is
projected to reach $ 523.2 Billion by 2030, growing at a CAGR of 13.14% from
2022 to 2030 (Healthcare Nanotechnology (Nanomedicine) Market Size and Fore-
cast 2023). There has been good demand for the nanomedicine products especially in
the North America and European markets. The emergence of the nanomedicine
market has impacted macroeconomic and microeconomic factors and due to positiv-
ity in the market, there has been surge of investments in the development and
production of the new and navel nanomedicine products. As the market dynamics
impact the supply and demand curves, companies and investors aim to examine the
best way to use various ﬁnancial tools to stem various policies for fast-moving the
growth and lowering the risks.


7.4.1     Market Classification of Nanomedicine Products

The global nanomedicine market is classiﬁed based on the different factors which are
shown below:

• Indication: The market is categorized into (1) cardiovascular diseases, (2) immu-
  nological diseases, (3) infectious diseases, (4) neurological diseases,
  (5) oncological diseases, (6) ophthalmological diseases, (7) orthopedic disorders,
  and (8) urological diseases (Fig. 7.3).

• Drug Delivery System: The market is categorized into (1) carbon nanotube,
  (2) dendrimers, (3) exosomes, (4) graphene, (5) injectable nanoparticle generator,
  (6) liposomes, (7) nano-bubbles, (8) nano-clusters.
• Product Type: The market is classiﬁed into (1) in vitro diagnostics, (2) in vivo

• Disease Application: the market is categorized into (1) anti-infectives, (2) anti-

• Geographical location: The global market is categorized into Americas, Europe,
  Middle-East and Africa and Asia-Paciﬁc.

    The global nanomedicine market is predictable to reach US$ 454.8 Billion by
2028 from US$ 177.1 Billion in 2021 (Anon (n.d.)). The tremendous increase in the
nanomedicine market shows the positivity about the nanomedicine products at
global level which in fact helping the companies to attract huge investments. It has
been found that nanomedicine products especially for the diagnostic purposes are
more reliable and give accurate results and most important require less time in
diagnosis of diseases. Due to this, the nano-based diagnostic methods need lesser
chemicals and reagents which is an attractive part for the investors. The
nanomaterials also have other advantages like having larger surface to volume
ratio compared to micro and macro sized materials which rises demand for nano-
drug delivery. The better targeting is another important feature of the nanomaterials
for effective delivery of the drug molecules in the targeted disease tissues with less
side effects and less chance to effect the non-disease tissues or cells.
144                                                                         F. A. Khan


   There has been increasing technological advancements in the nanomaterials for
biomedical applications, and in addition of having promising regulatory framework,
and surge research funding are some of the factors boosting growth of the market.
Furthermore, growing government initiatives in the North America and European
countries and with a high prevalence of chronic diseases such as diabetes, obesity,
cancer, orthopedic diseases, and kidney disorder, growing geriatric population, and
increasing preference for targeted nanomedicine is also likely to contribute to growth
of the nanomedicine market.
   There has been entry of new market players in the manufacturing of
nanomedicine products which will help to reduce the gap between supply and
demand. Currently, nanomedicine products that are available for the treatment of
various cancers such as Depocyt, Doxil, Oncospar, Neulasta, and Abraxane, respec-
tively. There has been increasing occurrence of cancer globally, hence, there is
demand for the anti-cancer drug therapy including nanomedicine product. As per
the National Cancer Institute, about 1.6 million new cases of cancer were diagnosed
in the USA alone in 2016 (Anon (n.d.)). North America accounted for the largest
market share in the global nanomedicine market. The market in Asia-Paciﬁc is likely
to see noteworthy growth due to growing geriatric population, growing healthcare
awareness among the populace, and increasing disposable income in the region.

7.5      Nanomedicine for Cancer Therapy

Nanomedicine products are expected to play a major role in the global pharmaceuti-
cal market and healthcare system. Since 1995, around 78 nanomedicine products
have been approved by the US Food and Drug Administration (USFDA) and the
European Medicines Agency (EMA) for clinical applications (Bulbake et al. 2017;
Halwani 2022; Prasad et al. 2018; Anselmo and Mitragotri 2019). In addition, more
than 140 nanomedicine products are under different stages of clinical trials. There
has been growing market trends for the nanomedicine products as the more and more
7   Nanocarriers-Based Products in the Market, FDA Approval, Commercialization. . .   145


number of nanomedicine products are being approved and marketed. Many reports
demonstrate that the number of medications based on nanotechnology are increasing
annually. New nanomedicine products enter clinical trials to examine their improve-
ment with regard to effectiveness compared with conventional preparations (Klein
et al. 2019; Caster et al. 2017). This is due to the fast growth in research and the high
market demand, which displays the prominence of nanomedicine products for the
drug delivery area. Nevertheless, the majority of nanomedicine approved products
have revealed a lower toxicity rather than better efﬁcacy (Caster et al. 2017). The
introduction of nanomedicine products in various countries for different the treat-
ment of disease have shown remarkable improvement.
    There are 78 different types of nanomedicine products have been approved by
USFDA and EMA and have entered the global market for both diagnosis and
treatment purposes. It has been reported that both the USFDA and the EMA have
shared in the approval of 20 nanomedicine products globally, whereas other
nanomedicine products have an approval from USFDA (43) and from EMA (12),
respectively. Over the past 10 years, due to better healthcare beneﬁts provided by the
medical insurance companies, the focus on the development of nanomedicine
products and the number of marketed nanomedicine products have signiﬁcantly
increased. Based on the types of nanomaterials used to produce nanomedicine
products, the nanomedicine products are classiﬁed as nano-crystals, lipid-based
nanomaterials, polymer-based nanomaterials, dendrimers-based nanomaterials,
protein-based nanomaterials, or inorganic nanomaterials.
    There has been growing demand for the inorganic nanomaterials for their appli-
cation in drug delivery systems. The different types of inorganic nanomaterials such
as metal, carbon nanotubes, calcium phosphate, iron oxide, silica, and quantum dot
nanomaterials are very attractive for use in drug delivery due to their dual function as
diagnostic area and also as therapeutic drug carriers (Anselmo and Mitragotri 2015).
It is possible to modify the inorganic nanomaterials chemically or biologically to
meet the perfect necessities for cellular drug delivery, and inorganic nanomaterials
possess better biocompatibility, high charge density, and precise targeted drug
delivery (Anselmo and Mitragotri 2015). It has been shown that inorganic
nanomaterials display excellent stability over broad ranges of temperature variations
and pH ﬂuctuations and they are not subject to microbial attack. In spite of these
different beneﬁts, few inorganic nanomaterials have reached the patients due to slow
dissolution and lack of biodegradation, which are the main shortcomings of inor-
ganic nanomaterials (Anselmo and Mitragotri 2015). On the other hand, organic
nanomaterial-based nanocarriers such as carbon nanomaterials that are usually
characterized by their better biocompatibility, low toxicity, and improved drug-
loading capacity. These organic-nanocarriers are mainly classiﬁed into three groups
(1) lipid-based vectors, (2) polymer-based vectors, and (3) dendrimers. It has been
found that these carriers provide special defense for therapeutic agents against
environmental degradation. The surfaces of liposomes and micelles can also be
functionalized to improve efﬁcacy of the anti-cancer agents.
    Cancer has 14 million new cases and more than 8.2 million cancer related deaths
were reported in 2012 (Kumar et al. 2020). Consequently, new and effective
146                                                                        F. A. Khan


anti-cancer molecules and drugs are immediately needed to control the high mortal-
ity. Anti-cancer nanomedicine products have made advancements in the past several
decades for the cancer diagnosis and treatments. Several nanomaterials with differ-
ent shapes and sizes have been developed for the effective delivery of therapeutic
and diagnostic agents to tumors to increase the accumulation of chemotherapeutic
agents within the tumor tissues (Peer et al. 2007; Schroeder et al. 2011; Farokhzad
and Langer 2009). To target tumor, different strategies are used which can be
classiﬁed as the passive targeting which is based on the enhanced permeability and
retention (EPR) effect and active targeting focused by tumor speciﬁc moieties and
stimuli-responsive tumor targeting (He et al. 2019). In the case of passive EPR
targeting, a phenomenon that leads to better accumulation of nanomaterials in the
tumor tissues as nanomaterials can easily pass through blood vessel of tumor
(Prabhakar et al. 2013). Passive EPR targeting and nanomaterial-size control were
the basic philosophies of ﬁrst generation cancer nanomedicine and examples is
Doxil, which is simple lipid vesicles protected with polyethylene glycol (PEG) to
avoid opsonization by the host cells, henceforth preventing an immune response and
prolonging circulation time. Doxil is a pegylated liposomal doxorubicin used to treat
different kinds of cancer such as metastatic ovarian cancer and AIDS-related
Kaposi’s sarcoma with less side effects. Doxorubicin was developed by
encapsulating an 80–90 nm size uni-lamellar liposome coated with PEG (Pillai
2014; Pillai and Ceballos-Coronel 2013). Doxorubicin is known to act on the cancer
cells by two different mechanisms, in the ﬁrst case, doxorubicin attacks cancer cells
DNA and caused the disruption of topoisomerase II-mediated DNA repairing
systems, and in the second case, doxorubicin caused the generation of free radicals,
resulting in damage to cellular membranes, DNA, and proteins, respectively
(Gordon et al. 2000). Nevertheless, doxorubicin has been known to cause severe
heart problems, such as heart failure during the treatment or after treatment (Pillai
2014).
    Another anti-cancer drug which is called as Abraxane which was approved by the
USFDA for the treatment of metastatic breast cancer in 2005, lung cancer in 2012,
and advanced pancreatic cancer in 2013, respectively. In this drug, human albumin is
conjugated to paclitaxel in order to deliver it in the form of nanomaterials with
130 nm in diameter size. It has been reported that better biodegradability, low
toxicity, and less immunogenicity make albumin a perfect carrier for drug delivery
by uptake by cells. The bioavailability of albumin-paclitaxel combination in the
tumor is enabled by the gp60 receptor-mediated pathway in the endothelial cell walls
of tumors, which binds to albumin with a great afﬁnity (Von Hoff et al. 2011; Desai
et al. 2004, 2006). The accumulation of Abraxane at the tumor site is also aided by
secreted protein, acidic and rich in cysteine, overexpressed in multiple types of
tumors, including breast, prostate, gastric, lung, and kidney (Pillai 2014).
    Another ground-breaking strategy for targeting cancer cells, which emphases on
nanomaterials that can be activated to release their contents (drug molecules) on
exposure to heat, light, or ultrasound method. ThermoDox is a stimuli-responsive
targeting nanomedicine which was industrialized by Celsion Corporation in partner-
ship with Duke University for the liver cancer treatment. ThermoDox is a
7   Nanocarriers-Based Products in the Market, FDA Approval, Commercialization. . .              147


temperature sensitive Doxorubicin-Pegylated Liposome that is able to release the
anti-cancer drug at the targeted site by increasing the temperature from 39 to 42 °
Celsius through the application of radio-frequency (Pillai 2014). Furthermore, the
accumulation of ThermoDox drug in tumor tissues upturns as the cancer blood
vessels become leaky due to the local application of hyperthermia (Pillai 2014).
   While more anti-cancer nanomedicine products are presently available on the
market than any other drugs, many drugs are marketed for the treatment of autoim-
mune conditions, metabolic, ophthalmic, neurological, hematological, inﬂammatory
diseases, respectively (Desai et al. 2006). The bulk of the developed nanomedicine
products have used liposomes and polymers as incorporated nanomaterials (Desai
et al. 2006). Additionally, there is a clear trend in the movement of nanomaterials
from simple to complex multi-component drug delivery systems (Desai et al. 2004).
These complex systems can include functions such as controlled release and active
targeting to allow nanomaterials to improve drug efﬁcacy, efﬁcacy, and drug safety.
Though most USFDA approved nanomedicine products are based on passive
targeting through the EPR effect, some next generation nanomedicine products use
active targeting methods in clinical trials (Bobo et al. 2016).
