Limitations of Nanocarriers Such as Cell
and Tissue Toxicity, Genotoxicity, Scale-Up                                                      8
of Nanomaterials

Dilawar Hassan, Ayesha Sani, and Dora I. Medina




   Abstract

   Due to drug resistance by the bacteria and systematic toxicity of classical
   medicines, a new range of medicines is being researched, termed as
   “Nanomedicine”. Nanomedicine is the use of nanomaterials for the diagnosis,
   prognosis and treatment of diseases. Nanomaterials are such materials, which
   have at least one dimension in Nanoscale range (one billionth of a metre).
   Nanomaterials are gaining worth due to large surface area and greater surface
   to volume ratio. Furthermore, the nanomaterials are being researched in the ﬁeld
   of Nanobiotechnology, due to enhanced optical, physiochemical and biomedical
   applications. The nanomaterials are being used for their potential anti-cancer,
   anti-bacterial, anti-fungal and drug loading capability. While, to make target
   speciﬁc, nanomaterials are surface modiﬁed with anti-bodies, proteins and
   siRNA for enhanced drug delivery and reduced systematic toxicity, still, there
   are various aspects need to be answered, including toxicity and other limitations
   faced by nanomedicine. The current chapter is to investigate various limitations
   faced by nanomaterials when being used for drug delivery purposes.

   Keywords
   Nanomedicine · Nanotoxicity · Drug delivery · Nanocarriers · Genotoxicity ·
   Scale-up · Tissue toxicity

8.1      Introduction

Diseases are leading cause of deaths, and the number is inkling every year.
According to a survey, 13.7 million deaths, only in 2019, around the globe were
related to pathogenic bacterial infections (Ikuta et al. 2022), while, ten million
deaths, reported in year 2020, were caused by some type of cancer, globally (Sung
et al. 2021). With evolution of science, in past, varieties of plants were employed to
ﬁght the diseases related to infections, due to the presence of a long range of
phytochemicals in them, but to their mode of action was very slow. Since, tradition-
ally used medications for treatment of various bacterial and fungal infections showed
efﬁciency in past, but with time, bacteria (Panáček et al. 2018) and fungus evolved
and created resistance against the used medicines. To overcome this resistance and
scientiﬁc development lead to production of synthetic medicines, which were much
more affective with a quick mode of action (Makabenta et al. 2021). Though these
results were portraying desired results, but on the contrary, the fungus and bacteria
had been getting stronger with time also (Morrison and Zembower 2020). Further-
more, classically used medicines for chemotherapeutic purposes have been known
for their cytotoxicity and potential against the cancers (Alyassin et al. 2020). But due
to this cytotoxic nature, the drugs have systematic toxicity also. The chemo-
therapeutic drugs are not target-speciﬁc and cause damage to normal and healthy
cell lines also, besides killing the cancerous cells. To overcome these issues of
bacterial and fungal resistance and systematic toxicity, new line of medicines is
being research for the last decade (Khalil et al. 2018). With that, pharmaceutical
research is also pursuing methods to enhance drug availability, and drug distribution
to obtain better results without using higher concentrations or stronger medicine
formulations. A new line of medicines is being researched due to their potential
applications, called nanomedicines, to answer the issues of classical methods.
    Nanomedicines make use of nanomaterials’ anti-fungal, anti-bacterial and anti-
cancer characteristics for medical applications. The ability of the nanomaterials to
cure several other ailments, such as neurodegenerative disorders, have also been
researched. Iron oxide (IO) (Hassan et al. 2018), nickel oxide (NiO) (Sani et al.
2021), chromium oxide (CrO) (Hassan et al. 2019), zinc oxide (ZnO) (Mendes et al.
2022), cobalt oxide (CoO) (Sani et al. 2023) and other metal oxides, both as bare
nanoparticles and as drug carriers, are nanoparticles utilized for medical reasons.
Figure 8.1 shows the various types of Nanoparticles/nanocarriers and their physical
properties. Polymeric nanoparticles, such as lipid nano-emulsions and Poly (lactic
acid-co-glycolic)-Polyethylene glycol (PLGA-PEG) (Simón-Vázquez et al. 2022),
are another class of nanoparticles being researched for their potential to deliver
drugs. When compared to previously used medications, nanomedicines have
demonstrated greater toxicity against illnesses, revolutionizing the science of phar-
macology (Vallorz et al. 2021). They have also demonstrated a method to prevent
achieving stronger pharmaceutical and discourage the use of huge doses (Weng et al.
2021). Nanomaterials have been used for the treatment of various types of cancers by
various treatments methods, one such method is induced hyperthermia for
8   Limitations of Nanocarriers Such as Cell and Tissue Toxicity,. . .                 151




Fig. 8.1 Various types of Nanoparticles/nanocarriers, their physical properties and surface
modiﬁcations

neutralizing cancerous cells (Kang et al. 2020). Another method makes use of
LASER for release of drugs at speciﬁc site (Xue et al. 2021).
   Though nanoscale provide materials with dramatic properties (Deng et al. 2021),
but with that comes a new set of issues that limit the full potential use of
nanomaterials as nanomedicines (Yang et al. 2019). Living body is a complex
system, carrying various obstacles for any foreign body to reach any speciﬁc
organ or part. Furthermore, due to small size and larger surface area, the
nanomaterials pose enhanced toxicity to the body they are being used against.
Genotoxicity (Ansari et al. 2019), muscle toxicity (Huang et al. 2019), cell toxicity
(Mohammed et al. 2019) and scale-up (Soni et al. 2020) are issues that limit the use
of nanomaterials as medicines in real life, though a lot of attention is being paid to
overcome them, but these issues still stand. Furthermore, to declare a potential
candidate for the next generation of medicines, focus must be paid to the evolution
of nanomaterials, that should be target-speciﬁc, environmentally friendly, with
greater bioavailability, enhanced retention time and lower toxicity.
   Here in this chapter, we will discuss about nanomaterials that are being employed
for medicinal purposes, their medicinal properties, but the chapter will place the
greatest emphasis on the toxicity of nanomaterials and other problems that are
restricting their usage. The chapter will concentrate on several toxicities (cell
toxicity, genotoxicity, scale-up and tissue toxicity) and what steps have been done,
particularly in recent years, to address these limitations.
152                                                                     D. Hassan et al.


8.2      Nanomaterials and Their Biomedical Applications

In nanoscale realm, the materials behave differently, since their bands become
clearer, therefore, they have varied optical, physiochemical and biomedical
properties, compared to their bulk counterparts. For a long time now, nanomaterials
have been researched as a potential candidate for future of medicine. Nanomaterials
have been studied for anti-bacterial, anti-fungal, anti-cancer, anti-leishmanial, anti-
inﬂammatory and many more biomedical applications. These biomedical properties
of nanomaterials heavily depend on the size and shape of nanoparticles (Dilawar
et al. 2019). Most commonly surface morphology and shape of the nanomaterials is
critical point in determining the potential anti-cancer effect of the nanomaterials
(Steckiewicz et al. 2019). Research published by Sani, A., and group described the
synthesis of Nickel Oxide nanoparticles. The group reported the diameter of
nanoparticles to have an average of ~17 nm. The synthesized material was used
for anti-bacterial potential against 12 pathogenic, waterborne, bacterial strains,
antioxidant potential, anti-leishmania potential, haemolytic potential, and ﬁnally
anti-cancer potential against HepG2 (hepatocellular carcinoma). The study
elaborated that biosynthesized NiO nanomaterials had excellent cytotoxicity against
bacterial, leishmanial and liver cancer cell lines, furthermore, the nanoparticles
caused minimal lysis of human blood samples during haemolytic study (Sani et al.
2021). Though nanomaterials are being widely used for biomedical studies, yet a lot
of research has been needed to discover actual mode of action of nanomaterials
towards a living cell. Various routes have been reported to cause cellular death, these
include (1) blockage and/or rapturing of cell wall/membrane, (2) enzyme inactiva-
tion, (3) causing damage and/or blocking nuclear membrane, (4) damaging
DNA/RNA and (5) destabilizing ribosomes and damaging proteins (Rahman et al.
2019). Figure 8.2 contains pictorial description about possible interaction of
nanoparticles with human blood components when introduced to human body via
intravenous route. Since the bare nanoparticles are target less and can enter organ,
therefore, a new approach is being investigated that loads a drug inside the nanopar-
ticle, in such cases termed as “nanocarrier”, and surface functionalized with an anti-
body or a compound that could take the nanocarrier to exact point to delivery, rather
than wondering around in the entire body. The nanocarrier can be a metallic, core-
shell structured, or polymeric nanoparticle. An article published by Zidan, A., et al.,
reported the synthesis of Poly(-D,L lactic-co-glycolic acid) PLGA nanocarriers
loaded with doxorubicin (DOX) for their anti-cancer application against Ehrlich
Ascites Carcinoma (EAC) in albino mice models. The group discovered that diame-
ter of loaded nanocarrier was 106.77 ± 22.15 nm. The nanocarriers showed impres-
sive results against the tested cancer cell lines, with enhanced cytotoxicity compared
to DOX only (Zidan et al. 2023). Furthermore, Esfandyari-Manesh, M., and group
synthesized Paclitaxel (PTX) encapsulated PLGA nanocarriers and surface modiﬁed
them with Human Serum Albumin (HSA), that provided stealth properties to
nanocarriers in sneaking out from reticuloendothelial system (RES). Group
measured the size of nanoparticles using DLS and calculated the size of PTX
encapsulated PLGA nanocarriers to be ~190 ± 10 nm while after conjugation of
8   Limitations of Nanocarriers Such as Cell and Tissue Toxicity,. . .               153




Fig. 8.2 Possible interaction between Nanocarriers/Nanoparticles and human blood, when
introduced via intravenously


HSA, there was an increase of ~25 ± 5 nm was observed in their diameter, whereas
zeta potential (ZE) value was found to be -6 mV. The drug release study showed that
drug was released in sustained manner at more acidic medium (pH 7.4) compared to
pH 5.5, up to 33 days (Esfandyari-Manesh et al. 2015). Although nanocarriers
provide with various advantages over classical use of drugs, still there remains a
large gap of issues that need to be answered. There are various issues associated with
nanocarriers may include but not limited to high cost of production, scale-up, drug
loading efﬁciencies, their toxicity and many more (Liu et al. 2020).


8.3       Limitations Faced by Drug Loaded Nanocarriers

Though drug loaded nanocarriers have revolutionized the ﬁeld of
nanobiotechnology and have provided with efﬁcient drug delivery approaches for
the treatment of various diseases, especially in the ﬁeld of cancer research, still there
are various obstacles that await the nanocarriers once they are introduced to a
diseased body (Reinholz et al. 2018). Majorly the ﬁrst hurdles, the nanocarriers
are meet with, are immune system (IS) and blood–brain barrier (BBB) (Galstyan
et al. 2019). Though these render the movement of nanocarriers inside the human or
animal body, the nanocarriers are also known to cause toxicities against the IS and
BBB. The structure and toxicities of nanocarriers against the IS and BBB and brieﬂy
described below.
154                                                                    D. Hassan et al.


8.3.1    Immune System (IS) and Nanocarriers

Immune system is defensive system of body (Islam et al. 2022). Its responsibility is
to conserve the integrity of body by defending it against invading chemical and
pathogenic alien bodies. Specially bacteria and viruses and other damages to human
body (Kraus and Gruber 2021). IS constitutes of soluble components and cellular
collections. There is a detailed and complex system that IS follows if it detects a
pathogen. For instance, if a bacterium enters the human body, IS analyses its
molecular structure, evaluates the tissue damage caused by it and then gets services
of various innate immune system (IIS) cells to get rid of them. Simultaneously,
dendritic cells (DCs) identify/sense pathogen caused damage and sample the invad-
ing pathogenic cell and transport it to lymph nodes. In the lymph node, DCs present
antigen to T cells which help in the elimination of the pathogenic cell (Lung et al.
2020).
   IS is further classiﬁed in two types: (1) innate immune system (IIS) (Costa da
Silva et al. 2021) and (2) adaptive immune system (Fontana et al. 2018). Innate
Immune System:

8.3.1.1 Innate Immune System
Innate immune system (IIS) is regarding as the ﬁrst line of defence of body against
an invading and/or attacking bacteria, virus and/or any pathogen (McKechnie and
Blish 2020). IIS has various types of cells, including lymphoid and myeloid, which
usually originate from bone marrow. Lymphoid includes innate lymphoid cells and
natural killers (NK), while myeloid includes DCs, neutrophils, macrophages and
monocytes. In addition to being ﬁrst defence line, IIS also maintains homeostasis of
tissues (Mattiola and Diefenbach 2023).

8.3.1.2 Adaptive Immune System
In contrast to IIS, which is very rapid and ﬁrst defensive line, adaptive immune
system (AIS) is very specialized and target speciﬁc immune system. AIS takes from
days to weeks to develop for every speciﬁc type of pathogen (Blanchard et al. 2020).
Unlike IIS, AIS is rooted on highly specialized class of white blood cells (WBCs)
known as T cells and B cells. Furthermore, AIS consists of CD4 T cells, B cells, CD8
T cells, NK cells and γδ T cells (Bomﬁm et al. 2017). T cells are further classiﬁed
into Th1; Th2; Treh (regulatory T cells) and Th22 cells (Sompayrac 2022).
   Since the primary function of IS is to eliminate the recognized foreign bodies,
therefore, a weak IS can affect the quality of life gravely. Furthermore, due to large
surface to volume ratio, nanocarriers may cause immunotoxicity also. Major
contributors to these toxicity issues of nanocarriers are associated to their shape,
size, larger surface area and specially their reactive oxygen species (ROS) produc-
tion property. ROS are known to cause oxidative stress on cells causing them to start
apoptosis (Ahmad et al. 2021). Furthermore, these nanocarriers also cause inﬂam-
mation, since they are regarded as a foreign body by the immune system. It is also
known that nanoparticles with size measurements greater than 100 nm are easily
eliminated by the immune cells (Aljabali et al. 2023). Figure 8.3 shows the
8   Limitations of Nanocarriers Such as Cell and Tissue Toxicity,. . .              155




Fig. 8.3 Possible interaction between innate and adaptive immune system with metal-based
nanoparticles/nanocarriers


schematics of nanocarriers/ nanoparticle’s interaction with immune system. Various
types of nanocarriers are used to deliver drugs to targeted cites including carbon,
polymer and metal and metal oxide based nanocarriers to be more common. Fur-
thermore, there is vast gap needed to be ﬁlled by research to know how nanoparticles
do behave when they come in contact with human blood. Immunotoxic impacts of
nanocarriers are broad spectrum based on the roue of administration and size; shape
of nanocarriers, ranging from lungs, systematic damage, to liver and acute inﬂam-
mation. If the nanocarriers are introduced intravenously, the nanocarriers can come
in undesired contact with various blood components, including red blood cells
(RBCs), white blood cells (WBCs), macrophages and blood proteins which can
156                                                                     D. Hassan et al.


alter the blood components, resulting is posing a threat to biocompatibility, safety
and biodegradability of the drug carrying nanoparticles (Hannon et al. 2019).


8.3.2    Blood–Brain Barrier (BBB)

Nanocarriers are not only suitable to deliver medicine to mutated tumour in lower
parts of body (i.e., lungs, liver, kidneys, etc.) but due to small size and altered and
enhanced photo and physiochemical properties, they have been studied for deliver-
ing drugs to upper part of body (Brain) also. Nanocarriers are being studied for drug
delivery to treat various neurodegenerative diseases, since they have been noticed to
cross the blood–brain barrier (BBB). BBB is a protective barrier that safeguards the
brain and central nervous system (CNS) from being coming contact with larger or
alien bodies or blood components. Due to highly selective and semipermeable nature
of BBB, various drugs that are meant to reach brain to treat various diseases are
precluded (Zhang et al. 2021). The main function of BBB is to maintain transport
regulation, tissue function and protection of brain and CNS, further the duties also
include to maintain the ﬂow of essential metabolites that include water, glucose and
amino acids with maintaining the brain homeostasis (Obermeier et al. 2013). The
BBB is structured of capillary basement membrane (BM), endothelial cells of CNS,
pericytes and astrocytes (Ballabh et al. 2004). Figure 8.4a shows the schematics of
BBB structure and Fig. 8.4b shows the schematics of transportation of various
components through BBB. Although there have been various developments and
studies on the passage of nanocarriers moving across the BBB, on various animals,
but still their toxic impacts on brain, CNS and BBB are still long from understood. A
study published by Rabanel, J.-M., et al. reports the use of PEGylated PLA (Poly
lactic acid) for to study their bioavailability across the BBB and their entry into
neuronal cells. The group found that 90 and 100 nm sized polymeric nanocarriers
were easily moving across the BBB of zebra ﬁsh, and found that after 24 h, ~80% of
zebra ﬁsh eggs hatched when exposed to 133 μg/mL concentration of the
nanocarriers (Rabanel et al. 2020). But still, a vast study is needed to assess the
toxicity of the nanocarriers and nanoparticles across the BBB. To move freely across
the BBB, nanoparticles/nanocarriers may disrupt the tight connections between
endothelial cells, which may result in a temporary or permanent dysfunction of
endothelial cell junctions, and there may be uncontrolled ﬂow of other unwanted
material also, that can disrupt the normal functionality of brain and CNS, resulting in
causing toxicity (Ceña and Játiva 2018). Even though the above-mentioned study
about the PEGylated PLA nanocarriers resulted in the hatching of ~80% of zebra ﬁsh
eggs, yet, these were not loaded with any drug, the question arises that what if the
drug crossing through the BBB, may be more toxic, and how much would be the safe
concentration of drug and nanoparticles that can cross through the BBB without
causing a permanent damage and with zero or minimal toxicity? Furthermore, a
study conducted by Sharma, H. S., et al., evaluated the permeability of Silver
(Ag) and Copper (Cu) nanoparticles, through BBB, in rat models. The group
mentioned that all studies were carried out by following the guidelines provided
8   Limitations of Nanocarriers Such as Cell and Tissue Toxicity,. . .               157




Fig. 8.4 (a) schematic structure of Blood–Brain Barrier (BBB) and (b) shows schematics of
component transportation across healthy BBB


by National Institute of Health (NIH) of the United States of America (USA). The
group revealed that the nanoparticles penetrated the BBB of rat models and caused
alterations in myelin and glial cells other than causing nerve cell damages in various
regions of brain (Ikuta et al. 2022). All these issues combined together limit the use
of nanoparticles for drug carrying purposes, unless these issues are answered.


8.4       Toxicity of Nanocarriers

Although the major hindrances in studying or approving the nanocarriers for medical
use are IS and BBB, but there are reports on various other toxicities of nanocarriers
also, in lab study stages. The metal, metal oxide and polymeric nanocarriers have
now been studied for their biomedical applications, for quite a time now. Various
studies report the bare and drug loaded nanoparticles have potential in treating
various fatal diseases, and reduce the systematic toxicity of drugs, but still, when
cytotoxicity of nanocarriers remains a major question. Although various studies,
when performed haemolytic studies, declared the use of nanoparticles to be safe for
use in lower concentration, but the change and alteration in behaviour of
nanoparticles/nanocarriers, when inside a living body, is yet to be investigated.
158                                                                        D. Hassan et al.


For instance, a study by Khalil, A. T., and group reported the genesis of Nickel
Oxide (NiO) nanoparticles using distilled water-based extract of Sageretia thea
leaves extract and studied them for various in vitro biomedical applications, i.e.,
anti-fungal, anti-cancer, anti-bacterial, anti-oxidant and anti-leishmanial. The results
revealed very high potential of synthesized NiO nanoparticles against all tested
strains and cell lines and even showed very attractive antioxidant properties, but
when studied for haemolytic assay on human drawn blood, the nanoparticles showed
~22% of lysis at 200 μg/mL concentration of NiO nanoparticles (Khalil et al. 2018).
There are various toxicities that can limit the use of nanoparticles and nanocarriers in
their evolution from lab trials to human trials, and those toxicities can be against
either cell, tissue and gene, other than previously explained toxicities against IS and
BBB. Here we will discuss the toxicity of nanocarriers against cells and tissues and
genotoxicity, one by one.


8.4.1    Cellular Toxicity of Nanocarriers

Cell is a basic unit of life (Zhu et al. 2019). Human body is made of cells, and they
are food and energy factories of human body, the cells are responsible of delivering
food and oxygen to all organs and parts of body for their proper functioning (Kumar
et al. 2020). There are various types of cells, majorly two types, RBCs and WBCs.
The function of RBCs is to carry oxygen and energy to organs (Scindia et al. 2019),
while the function of WBCs is majorly defending body against infections and
bacteria, fungal or viral attacks (Teoh 2023). If the WBCs somehow fail to contain
the pathogenic bacterial or fungal cells or viral attack, the red blood cells get infected
or mutated thus resulting in a sick or ill body (Bhattacharyya 2021). Therefore, to
treat these diseases, various drugs are introduced that are toxic to attacking bacterial
or fungal cells and/or virally mutated cells. Usually, anti-bacterial and anti-fungal
drugs are not much toxic to human cells, but their misuse may cause various fatal
side effects, like mutation in liver, etc. It is a well-known fact that now a days
bacteria are becoming resistant to many available anti-bacterial drugs, since they
have natural phenomenon to create resistance against any foreign entity, therefore,
now either strong dose (i.e., up to 2 g per dose) or new generation of anti-microbial
drugs are being prescribed. Various countries have put strong rules against the
prescription of anti-bacterial drugs, but in many countries, especially third world
countries, anti-bacterial drugs can be availed without production of prescription. On
the other hand, anti-viral (i.e., anti-cancer) drugs are toxic to both mutated and
normal healthy cell. Their major function is to kill a cell, regardless of diseased or
healthy.
   Therefore, a new range of medicines, Nanomedicine, are being researched to be
evolution is the ﬁeld of pharmacy and pharmacology. Nanomedicine are medicines
that utilize nanomaterials for prognosis, diagnosis and treatment of various diseases.
Nanomaterials can be used bare for introduction of toxicity to bacterial, fungal and
cancerous cell or they can be used as a carrier for taking a drug to fungal, cancerous
and/or bacterial cell. If the nanomaterial is opted to carry drug, it needs to be surface
8   Limitations of Nanocarriers Such as Cell and Tissue Toxicity,. . .                       159




Fig. 8.5 Proposed routes followed by a nanocarrier or a nanoparticle to enter a cell and interact
with the cell to cause cell death


functionalized with it or drug could be loaded inside the nanomaterial. In other terms
such nanomaterials which carry a drug are termed as nanocarriers. Nanoparticles and
nanocarriers used for their biomedical potential can be either, metal and metal oxide
or polymeric. There are various possible ways by which a nanomaterial or
nanocarriers can cause cell death. In a bacterial cell, a nanoparticle may follow
one or more paths to kill it. The possible paths can be (1) generation of ROS,
(2) Damage the electron transport chain, (3) Peptidoglycan disruption, (4) Cell
membrane and/or cell wall disruption, (5) enzyme inactivation, (6) Protein
denaturing, (7) DNA/RNA damaging and/or (8) Nuclear membrane damage, as
shown in Fig. 8.5. A recent study published by Sani A., et al., reported green
chemical synthesis of NiO nanoparticles by reducing the salt of Ni with the help
of plant extract. The group then used the bio-modulated NiO nanoparticles against
Hepatocellculr carcinoma (HepG2) cell lines and studied the toxic impact of bio-
genic nanoparticles. Group revealed that the possible route of cell death was due to
ROS, while the group explains that there can be any possible route followed by
nanoparticle ought to cause apoptosis (Sani et al. 2021). In another study, Khan, I.,
and group reported the synthesis of PLGA nanocarriers functionalized with Berber-
ine and Doxirubicin (DOX) for co-delivery and study their toxicity against T47D
and MDA-MB-231 breast cancer cell lines. Group deﬁned the method of
160                                                                       D. Hassan et al.


nanoparticles causing toxicity in cancer cell lines to be ROS generation and found
out that nanoparticles were causing high rate of ROS generation at 25 μM concen-
tration against MDA-MB-231 cell lines while against the T47D, the ROS generation
was comparatively lower (Khan et al. 2019). Furthermore, Vaz-Ramos, J., and group
generated Iron Oxide (IO) cored Silica (SiO2) nanoparticles functionalized with
Epirubicin (EPI) to study their toxicity against the HepG2 cell lines and rate of
drug release over a period of 48 h. The group reported that EPI loaded IO.SiO2
nanocarriers showed enhanced drug release even after 48 h of study while % release
eached 38%, whereas, the nanocarriers apoptosis of almost ~90% cells when
introduced to HepG2 cell lines with concentration of 100 μg/mL (Vaz-Ramos
et al. 2020).
    Although all results mentioned above show good toxicity against the cancer cell
lines but in case of Sani A., et al., and Khan I., et al., reported the nanoparticles and
Nanocarriers to be safe against the human blood drawn RBCs at very low concen-
tration, while, as the nanoparticle concentration increased, the toxicity showed
elevated results also. Whereas, the Vaz-Ramos, J., and group did not publish any
report on toxicity of their synthesized nanoparticles against the normal and healthy
blood cells. Furthermore, all studies published to date report the in vitro toxicity of
nanoparticles and nanocarriers against healthy human blood, hence leaving a big
question on feasibility of nanocarriers and nanoparticles to be used for the treatment
on humans.


8.4.2    Gene Toxicity

Another possible rout nanocarriers and nanoparticles opt to cause death of a living
cell (weather cancerous or healthy) can be by targeting the gene and causing
genotoxicity. It is already a well-established fact that nanoparticles are known to
generate Reactive Oxygen and Nitrogen Species (RONS) and during the treatment it
is mandatory to keep a track on RONS concentrations being generated (Chen et al.
2023). If there is any imbalance in riddance and generation of RONS, and the RONS
start accruing, they can cause damage to DNA, intracellular lipids and/or proteins,
resulting in genotoxicity (Moldogazieva et al. 2018). And if this imbalance happens,
it can trigger a chain reaction, resulting in immunogenicity and acute inﬂammation
(Yang and Merlin 2023). Other than that it is also reported that various metal
nanoparticles can catalyse the generation of reactive hydroxyl radicals and oxygen
driven metabolites that engender lipid peroxidation (Paciorek et al. 2020), which can
result in malfunction of various cellular organelles including plasma membrane,
mitochondria and endoplasmic reticulum (ER) (Husain and Mahmood 2019).
    Cells, in routine, produce superoxide dismutase (SOD), reduced glutathione
(GSH) and α-lipoic acid to defuse RONS that can also cause oxidative stress and
stop cell from countering RONS, in return causing genotoxicity (Yang and Merlin
2023). It has been found in studies also that ZnO nanoparticles with concentration of
50 mg/kg concentration largely upset the SOD genesis and causing acute intestinal
damages in studied rats (Abbasalipourkabir et al. 2015). In addition, it has also been
8   Limitations of Nanocarriers Such as Cell and Tissue Toxicity,. . .                       161




Fig. 8.6 Possible mechanisms of Nanocarriers/nanoparticle for their genotoxicity potential


found that Titania (TiO2) nanoparticles gravely disrupt the ration between oxidized
glutathione (GSSG) and GSH causing elevated concentration of ROS in hepatic cells
resulting in lipid peroxidation, and rise in micronucleus frequency—that is one of the
potential signs for genotoxicity (Shakeel et al. 2016). Whereas, it has also been
found in case of copper (Cu) driven CuO nanoparticles that they tend to disturb the
transmembrane potential of mitochondria and reduce the GSH levels in blood,
resulting in altering the normal function of macrophages (Abudayyak et al. 2020).
According to literature there can be three major types of mechanisms that a nano-
particle/nanocarrier can follow to cause genotoxicity in a cell (Landsiedel et al.
2022). Figure 8.6 shows possible mechanisms of Nanocarriers/nanoparticle for their
genotoxicity potential.
162                                                                     D. Hassan et al.


• Direct interaction between nanocarriers/nanoparticles and DNA (primary
  mechanisms).
• The interaction between nanocarriers/nanoparticles with molecules that take part
  in cell division or DNA replication (primary indirect mechanisms).
• Nanocarriers/nanoparticles caused genesis of ROS that damage or disrupt the
  DNA (secondary mechanisms).

   The toxicity of nanocarriers largely depend upon the rate of uptake of
nanocarriers inside the cell, whereas the rate of uptake depends on the size of
nanocarriers (Landsiedel et al. 2009). As reported by El-Shorbagy, H., and group,
used Zinc Oxide (ZnO) nanoparticles bought from Sigma Aldrich and used them for
studying their effect on P53, Bcl2 and Bax genes for apoptotic induction in Ehrlich
solid carcinoma (ESC) bearing mice models, by feeding them 50, 300 and 500 mg of
nanoparticles per kg of their weight for a week continuously after every 24 h. The
results revealed incline in the P53 and Bax genes while decline in Bcl2 genes with
signiﬁcant shrink in the tumour size of ZnO nanoparticles introduced mice. The
tumour size was 46% smaller in size compared to control. Furthermore, histopath-
ological analysis revealed massive inﬁltration of mononuclear inﬂammatory cells in
the portal triad and vacuolar degeneration of hepatocytes (El-Shorbagy et al. 2019).
   Another commentary study published by Wang, Y., and group reports the
genotoxicity evaluation of Gold (Au) nanoparticles. The group reported the size of
Au nanoparticles to be 14 nm while they used various concentration (0.1 to 100 μg/
mL) against Human peripheral blood lymphocytes (PBLs), and 6.2 to 50 μg/mL
concentration of 12 nm sized Au nanoparticles against BALB mice 3 T3 ﬁbroblast
cells. Furthermore, the group also investigated the genotoxicity of 10, 30 or 60 nm
sized Au nanoparticles against HepG2 cell lines at concentration of 10 ppm via
following comet assay. The results revealed noticeable damage in DNA and oxida-
tive DNA damage. While in vivo study involved Rats and ﬁsh. The study conducted
on rats revealed DNA damage, while the rats were fed with Au nanoparticles for
28 days. For the ﬁsh, the results raised DNA damage even when ﬁsh were exposed to
80 μg/L concentration of nanoparticles for 72 h (Wang et al. 2020).
   Another study carried by Schulz et al., involved the introduction of Au
nanoparticles to rats lugs via intratracheal installation for 72 h, though the study
explained that there was no signiﬁcant damage in DNA of the rats exposed to Au
nanoparticles compared to control rats, but the question yet remains is the time of
exposure. There should be various routes of study to explain the impact of different
concentration of varied size and shaped nanoparticles over varied units of time
(Schulz et al. 2012). Therefore, nanocarriers and nanoparticles, even after being
very beneﬁcial towards treatments of various fatal diseases, are still being seen as a
potential hazard since their safety and toxicity is not fully known, which limit their
progress.
8   Limitations of Nanocarriers Such as Cell and Tissue Toxicity,. . .               163


8.4.3     Accumulation in Organs

Apart from toxicity, another issue that limits the progression of nanocarriers/
nanoparticles being risen to the status of next generation of medicine is their
accumulation inside organs. There have been reports of nanoparticles getting
accumulated in liver, lungs, lymph nodes, brain, kidneys and others. In liver the
nanoparticles tend to accumulate due to macrophage (Gustafson et al. 2015). Every
drug or chemical that is entering a living body have to pass through the liver, in the
same way, nanocarriers also ﬁnd their way inside the liver (Boey and Ho 2020).
More than 30% of all administered nanocarriers or nanomedicine into any animal
body tend to get accumulated in the liver, resulting in enhanced toxicity to liver and
reduce targeting application for treatment of any disease (Zhang et al. 2016). Jiang,
X., and group published research about the synthesis of Indocyanine Green (ICG)
and GSH loaded Au nanoparticles and studied their accumulation inside BALB/c
mice models. The researchers found that 70% of all administered ICG-GSH-AU
nanoparticles tend to accumulate in liver while their concentration decrease with
time (Jiang et al. 2019).
   Other than liver, nanocarriers have also been reported to be accumulated inside
other organs as well. Though the longer retention time gives additional beneﬁts to
nanocarriers, i.e., increased bioavailability, but in return can also cause enhanced
toxicity. Brain is also one of the most important organs of a human body. There have
been various research studies to produce nanomedicines that can cross the highly
selective BBB to treat various neurodegenerative diseases. Many of the studies have
reported promising results, but their tendency to accumulate inside brain raises a
major question about their toxicity. In previous topics we have already discussed the
damage that nanocarriers may cause to BBB. It is impossible to quantitatively
discern between endothelium accumulation and transit into the parenchyma from
the published data on nanoparticle accumulation in the brain in vivo (Betzer et al.
2017). The factors of size and shape may impact the accumulation and retention rate
of nanoparticles inside brain cells (Nowak et al. 2020). Kolhar and group reported
the accumulation of anti-TfR anti-body functionalized poly-styrene
(PS) nanoparticles with two different shapes, i.e., rods and spherical, and found
the rod shaped nanoparticles tend to accumulate in higher concentration into brain
compared to spherical shaped PS nanoparticles (Kolhar et al. 2013). while in another
study by Arnida, M. et al., the accumulation of sphere shaped PEGylated Au
nanoparticles was higher in brain compared to rod shaped counterparts (Arnida
et al. 2011).
   Other than these organs there is a study carried by Bartucci, R., et al., that reports
the accumulation of PS nanoparticles functionalized carboxylate into liver lungs and
kidneys. The size of nanoparticles was measured to be 40 nm while their accumula-
tion was studies on C57BL/6 J mice models. The group found accumulation of
nanoparticles inside all studied organs and reported that the rate of nanoparticles
accumulation inclined with the increase in time. Furthermore, the group reported
40% of nanoparticles in ﬁrst hour of exposure to lungs tissues while about 10% of
the nanoparticles accumulated in liver in the same time. While after 2 days of
164                                                                         D. Hassan et al.




Fig. 8.7 Possible organs, where nanoparticles/nanocarriers can accumulate


exposure more than 80% of liver, lungs and kidneys macrophages contain PS
nanoparticles in them. Whereas the concentration of PS nanoparticles accumulation
inside kidney macrophages was less in comparison to liver and lungs (Bartucci et al.
2020).
   Apart from nanocarriers accumulation in liver, lungs, kidneys and brain, the
nanocarriers can get accumulated inside lymphatics, bines and eyes (Busatto et al.
2019). Figure 8.7 shows various organs, where nanoparticles/nanocarriers can
possibly accumulate.


8.5       Scale-Up of Nanocarriers

Nanotechnology is interfacing technology with many other technologies, for
instance, nano-chemistry (Li and Jin 2020), nano-electronics (Meteab et al. 2023),
nanobiotechnology (Hassan et al. 2018), nanomedicine (Sani et al. 2023) and many
more. Currently vast majority of researchers are turning to nanotechnology due to
dramatic changes in the properties of materials when they reach nano realm (Cheng
et al. 2021). A large number of those researchers is working the biomedical
applications of nanomaterials (Abd Elkodous et al. 2019), focusing on the genesis
of nanomaterials that can carry drug to reach various parts of body to treat diseases
with decreased systematic toxicity (Shariﬁ-Rad et al. 2021). But before going to use
8   Limitations of Nanocarriers Such as Cell and Tissue Toxicity,. . .               165


them for acquired properties, materials must be brought to nanoscale. There are two
major approaches that can be employed to reach nano realm: (1) Bottom-Up and
(2) Top-Down (Camargos and Rezende 2021). In the Bottom-Up approach atoms or
molecules are joined together to reach the nano size, whereas in case of Top-Down
approach, bulk sized materials are broken down or chemically reduced to reach nano
scale range. In the Top-Down approach there are various methods to get desired
material of nanoscale range that may include chemical degradation of bulk materials,
biological degradation (Ijaz et al. 2021), physical degradation (Salem and Fouda
2021), or biochemical degradation (Hassan et al. 2019) of bulk materials.
Controlling materials at atomic or molecular scale is difﬁcult (Khajetoorians et al.
2019) and needs high economical and energy resources (Jia et al. 2019) while to
break down material from bulk is easier. Most widely used method to break a bulk
material to obtain nanomaterial is co-precipitation method (Selvaraju et al. 2019)
(chemical method), while it has its drawn backs also. Major drawbacks include the
generation of bio- and environmentally toxic by-products (Sani et al. 2021). Now a
days, a new approach is being research to obtain desired materials i.e., biochemical,
or green chemical method (Khalil et al. 2018). The green chemical method employs
the use of plant extracts for the reduction of metal salts of desired material.
    The generation of materials at lab scale for small scale research is easy but scaling
up of that same material at industrial scale is quite difﬁcult (Wu et al. 2020). There
are various issues that can be faced while trying to scale-up the synthesis of
nanomaterials, that may include, but not limited to, economic issues, manpower,
safety issues, toxicity issues to workers and most majorly environmental impacts.
Other than that controlling the size and shape of nanocarriers/nanoparticles is quite
difﬁcult and challenging since minor pressure and temperature changes can cause
variations. Furthermore, it is highly unlikely to reproduce the same sized and shaped
nanomaterials under same conditions (Paliwal et al. 2014). Other than that, the %
yield of production is also a major hindrance in scaling up the production of
nanocarriers. Furthermore, the raw materials required for desired materials also
varies in acquisition price.
    Even though the production of nanocarriers could be started, the question about
the toxicological impacts on the works remain a major question. Until today, the
safety and bio- and environmental degradability of nanocarriers/nanoparticles
remains unclear (Klaper 2020), therefore, getting approvals for hiking up the
production of nanomaterials at large scale discouraged. Furthermore, the degradation
of nanomaterials inside environment is also needing to be answered (Klaper 2020). It
is still unknown that how nanomaterials behave when they escape into the environ-
ment and how deep they penetrate our environment and what is their shelf life before
they get degraded completely. Furthermore, their behaviour towards the micro-
organism living in the environment and other living things like animals and plants
are also the aspects that need a lot of attention. Therefore, all these factors combined
hinder the development and scaling up of nanomaterials/nanocarriers for their use in
the ﬁeld of medical industry.
166                                                                              D. Hassan et al.


8.6       Conclusion

Nanomaterials when loaded with a drug or molecule are called as nanocarriers. Even
though the nanocarriers have many advantages over the classically used medicines,
but there are various factors that breaking the speed of their development and
growth. The factors like the toxicity of nanocarriers when a living system is exposed
to them need high priority attentions, since their impacts and behaviour when they
enter a living system are unknown. Their can be various factors that can impact the
action and targeting properties of nanocarriers when they enter a living body. The
ﬁrst hindrance nanocarriers will face is immune system. Immune system has the job
of capturing and eliminating the alien entities entering living body, since
nanocarriers are not part of living body, the immune system treats them as a foreign
body and tries to eliminate them, or else, captures and encapsulates them, which
limit the mode of action and bioavailability if the nanocarriers. Second barrier
nanoparticles can face inside an animal or human body is BBB, the BBB is major
protector of brain and nervous system, if breached there can be various grave issues
to the exposed human or animal body. Furthermore, nanocarriers tend to produce
various toxicities to cells, genes, tissues, muscles and organs followed by the issue of
nanocarriers accumulation inside organs and muscles largely question the develop-
ment of nanocarriers and act as an obstacle towards their growths. Even if all these
issues are ignored, the scale-up of nanocarriers remains a major issue also, since
societal impacts of nanocarriers/nanomaterials are unknown. Therefore, before
going to test nanocarriers on human or enhancing their production, all these issues
must be answered.

Health and Environmental Hazards
Associated with the Synthesis                                                                  9
of Nanomaterials-Respiratory Diseases,
Government Regulations, Ethical Issues

Khalid Umar Fakhri and Firdos Alam Khan



   Abstract

   Recently, there has been growing interest in nanomaterials, especially for their
   applications in the biomedical ﬁeld. These nanomaterials possess a small size
   together with astonishing physicochemical properties, which makes them most
   suitable for therapeutic applications. Different types of nanomaterials, such as
   metallic, carbon-based, ﬂuorescent, and polymer-based, have been used in tissue
   engineering, drug delivery, and other therapeutic applications. Despite many
   useful applications, nanomaterials possess some harmful effects on the human
   body, vegetation, and also on the environment. Nanomaterials can enter cells or
   tissues and organs because of their nano-size, large surface area, and better
   penetrability, which may cause serious health issues (inhibition of cell prolifera-
   tion, cell wall damage, modiﬁcation of function and morphology of organelles,
   oxidative stress, genotoxicity, and cell death) in living organisms including
   humans. In this chapter, we have aimed to provide the harmful effects of
   nanomaterials as per published research papers. The topics related to
   nanomaterial’s toxicity, mode of action and penetration of nanomaterials in the
   human body are discussed. We have also discussed the government regulations,
   ethical issues related to nanomaterials toxicity and possible health hazards.

   Keywords

   Health and environmental hazards · Synthesis of nanocarriers · Nano drug
   delivery · Respiratory diseases · Government regulations · Ethical issues


9.1      Introduction

Nanoparticles are tiny structures composed of atoms, with dimensions falling
between the sizes of ions (10^-10 m) and larger materials. Their uniqueness stems
from their small atom count, leading to a substantial proportion of atoms being
present on or near the particle’s surfaces. This prominent surface presence makes
notable alterations to the particle’s atomic, electronic, and magnetic properties and
physical and chemical characteristics, setting it apart from bulk materials. Addition-
ally, the surfaces of nanoparticles themselves can exhibit distinct attributes (Banﬁeld
and Zhang 2001). Nanoparticles have been used in many areas, such as agriculture,
cosmetics, medicine, drug delivery, water puriﬁcation, the oil industry, etc. Pres-
ently, nanotechnology serves as a valuable instrument for delving into the most
intricate aspects of medical science through various avenues such as imaging,
sensing, precise administration of drugs, genetic material delivery systems, and the
creation of synthetic implants. Modern pharmaceutical advancements include nano-
particle forms comprising polymers, metals, or ceramics, capable of addressing
ailments like cancer and combating human pathogens like bacteria.
    Building upon nanotechnology, the development of remarkably effective
indicators and accurate, quantiﬁable detection tools holds the potential to revolu-
tionize patient care. These innovations can signiﬁcantly impact medical management
by enabling early diagnosis and meticulous monitoring of treatments. As a result,
patients’ quality of life can be enhanced, and mortality rates in conditions such as
cancer and Alzheimer’s can be reduced (Nasimi and Haidari 2013). While
nanoparticles bring numerous beneﬁts to the medical domain, their appealing
characteristics can also inﬂuence their potential for toxicity within biological
systems, encompassing aspects like absorption, distribution, metabolism, and clear-
ance. These toxicological considerations underscore the need to consider size, shape,
surface chemistry, and chemical composition when producing nanoparticles. These
concerns also extend to their impact on cellular interactions, the endocytic pathway,
and the absorption process. Consequently, nanoparticles can trigger cytotoxic
mechanisms that disrupt cellular equilibrium (Mauricio et al. 2018). In this chapter,
we have aimed to provide the harmful effects of nanomaterials as per published
research papers. The topics related to nanomaterial’s toxicity, mode of action, and
penetration of nanomaterials in the human body are discussed. We have also
discussed the government regulations, ethical issues related to nanomaterials toxicity
and possible health hazards.
9   Health and Environmental Hazards Associated with the Synthesis. . .           175


9.2       Types of Nanoparticles

Nanoparticles are categorized according to shape, size, and chemical attributes.
These classiﬁcations are determined by their physical and chemical properties.
Based on their dimensions, nanomaterials can be classiﬁed into zero-dimensional
(0-D), one-dimensional (1-D), two-dimensional (2-D), and three-dimensional (3-D).
In the 0-D category, nanomaterials possess all three of their dimensions within the
nanoscale range, such as quantum dots, fullerenes, and nanoparticles. The 1-D class
comprises nanomaterials with one dimension extending beyond the nanoscale.
Examples encompass nanotubes, nanoﬁbers, nanorods, nanowires, and nanohorns.
Nanomaterials in the 2-D grouping have two dimensions exceeding the nanoscale.
Instances include nanosheets, nanoﬁlms, and nanolayers. Three-dimensional
nanomaterials (3-D) or bulk nanomaterials do not possess conﬁnement to the
nanoscale in any dimension. This category encompasses bulk powders, dispersions
of nanoparticles, arrays of nanowires and nanotubes, and similar structures (Joudeh
and Linke 2022).
    Fullerenes and carbon nanotubes (CNTs) are prominent categories within carbon-
based nanoparticles. Fullerenes consist of nanomaterials characterized by spherical
hollow structures, representing various allotropes of carbon. They have garnered
considerable attention in the commercial sphere due to their exceptional attributes,
such as electrical conductivity, robustness, unique structure, electron afﬁnity, and
adaptability. On the other hand, CNTs are elongated, tube-like formations measuring
around 1–2 nm in diameter. Their diameter dictates their metallic or semiconducting
nature. Structurally, they resemble the rolling of a graphite sheet upon itself (Khan
et al. 2019). Due to its outstanding antioxidant potential, Fullerene holds signiﬁcant
promise as a fundamental component in numerous dermatological and skincare
formulations (Mousavi et al. 2017). There is a strong focus on investigating the
mechanisms behind the selective targeting of tumors using biofunctionalized CNTs.
This heightened interest is driven by their remarkable capacity to convert near-
infrared (NIR) laser radiation into heat. This inherent characteristic presents fresh
opportunities for creating innovative immunoconjugates tailored for cancer photo-
therapy. These novel constructs exhibit notable potential and effectiveness in pre-
cisely eradicating cancerous cells through selective thermal ablation (Iancu and
Mocan 2011).
    Metal nanoparticles are composed solely of metal precursors and exhibit distinct
optoelectrical characteristics owing to the well-established phenomenon of localized
surface plasmon resonance (LSPR). Notably, alkali and noble metals such as Copper
(Cu), Silver (Ag), and Gold (Au) nanoparticles exhibit a broad absorption band
within the visible region of the electromagnetic solar spectrum. In contemporary
materials research, precise control over the synthesis of metal nanoparticles in terms
of their facets, sizes, and shapes holds signiﬁcant importance. Leveraging their
advanced optical attributes, metal nanoparticles ﬁnd utility across diverse research
domains. Gold nanoparticles, for instance, are commonly employed to coat surfaces
for scanning electron microscopy (SEM) sampling. This coating effectively
176                                                            K. U. Fakhri and F. A. Khan


enhances the ﬂow of electrons, consequently contributing to acquiring high-quality
SEM images (Khan et al. 2019).
    Polymers are large molecules composed of numerous repeating units, forming a
chain-like molecular structure with a wide range of compositions, structures, and
characteristics. This diversity in compositions, structures, and attributes makes
polymers a valuable component in nanoparticle systems, enabling the creation of
nanoparticles tailored to speciﬁc biomedical purposes. The primary application of
polymeric nanoparticles lies in drug delivery, while they also ﬁnd utility in tasks like
bioimaging and biosensing assays (Morena-Vega et al. 2012). Semiconductor
nanomaterials exhibit captivating physical and chemical attributes along with prac-
tical functionalities that distinguish them from conventional bulk substances and
molecular materials. Noteworthy features of these materials include concentrated
emission spectra with limited width, uninterrupted absorption ranges, robust chemi-
cal and photobleaching stability, processability, and surface adaptability (Sahu
2019).
    Lipid-based nanoparticles, such as liposomes, solid lipid nanoparticles (SLN),
and nanostructured lipid carriers, have garnered signiﬁcant interest in the realms of
drug development and cancer therapy. These nanoparticles can convey hydrophobic
and hydrophilic compounds while exhibiting minimal to negligible toxicity. Fur-
thermore, they enhance drug efﬁcacy by prolonging drug activity through extended
half-lives and controlled release mechanisms. The primary constituents of these
nanoparticles consist of phospholipids, which arrange themselves in a bilayer con-
ﬁguration owing to their amphipathic nature. When exposed to water, they assemble
into vesicles, enhancing the solubility and stability of anticancer medications when
incorporated within their framework (García-Pinel et al. 2019). Despite many
applications of nanoparticles, it is important to explore the toxicities induced by
them because of continuous applications.


9.3      Harmful Effects of Nanomaterials

The speedy growth of nanomaterials production and synthesis has raised safety
concerns for human health and the surrounding environment. The unique properties
of nanomaterials make them the most promising candidates for various biomedical
applications but at the same time, they pose a potential threat to the biological
functions of the human body and produce hazardous effects (Soares and Soares
2021). Humans are exposed to nanomaterials intentionally (Table 9.1) through the
usage of nanomedicine and also get exposed to them unintentionally through
industrial production and synthesis of nanomaterials (Lombi et al. 2019; Klaper
2020). The biological application of nanomaterials may cause damage to human
cells and tissues as they can easily penetrate inside human cells due to their small
sizes. The unintentional impact of nanomaterials, such as their application in envi-
ronmental remediation processes (Guerra et al. 2018; Qian et al. 2020) and in the
agricultural (Servin et al. 2015; Usman et al. 2020), is noteworthy. Examples of
unintentional release of nanomaterials are when products such as paints, cosmetics,

2013).


9.4       Impact of Nanomaterials-Induced Toxicity

Humans are constantly exposed to various sources of nanoparticles, and the current
method of evaluation of toxicity induced by nanoparticles is not always fully
representative of real-life scenarios (Forest 2021). The interaction of nanoparticles
with human cells and tissues is difﬁcult to assess in living individuals, which is
responsible for combined toxicity (Forest 2021). Certainly, the biological effects
generated by nanoparticles are usually evaluated focusing on individual
nanoparticles (Carlsten 2018; Rosário et al. 2020), whereas their interaction with
co-contaminants can intensely impact the cells and tissues with stimulatory and
inhibitory ways (Forest 2021; Abbas et al. 2020). The toxicity of individual
nanoparticles can be easily measured, whereas it is difﬁcult to measure toxicity
induced by multiple or complex nanoparticles (Silins and Hogberg 2011; Zheng
et al. 2012).
   When dissolved in the solvent, certain nanomaterials release ions in the medium
that can reach cells to produce a harmful effect after intracellular (Soares and Soares
2021). It has been reported that metal ions do not diffuse freely across the plasma
membrane. Consequently, different membrane transport proteins (pumps and
channels) are involved in their inﬂux inside the cells (Argueello et al. 2012). There
are certain metallic nanomaterial, where ions can play a major role in their inducing
ecotoxicity especially in the case of Zinc Oxide nanomaterials induced cytotoxicity
in (1) bacteria (Heinlaan et al. 2008; Li et al. 2011; Wang et al. 2016), (2) yeasts
(Kasemets et al. 2009; Bayat et al. 2014), (3) crustaceans (Heinlaan et al. 2008;
178                                                                    K. U. Fakhri and F. A. Khan




Fig. 9.1 Illustration of induction of toxicity of zinc oxide nanomaterials on different species


Wiench et al. 2009; Vimercati et al. 2020), (4) microalgae (Franklin et al. 2007;
Miller et al. 2010; Lee and An 2013; Aravantinou et al. 2015; Schiavo et al. 2016) or
(5) mammalian cell lines (Brunner et al. 2006; Zhang et al. 2012) or in vivo model
(Wang et al. 2017). The transcriptomics, metabolomics, and lipidomics have con-
ﬁrmed that metal ions mediated the main toxicological pathways of Zinc oxide
nanomaterials in lung epithelial cells (Dekkers et al. 2018). In addition, Cupper
oxide nanomaterials toxicity were also reported in bacteria (Bondarenko et al. 2012),
yeasts (Kasemets et al. 2009, 2013; Bayat et al. 2014; Bao et al. 2015), green algae
(Aruoja et al. 2009; von Moos et al. 2015), crustaceans (Heinlaan et al. 2008; Jo et al.
2012) or human cell lines (Cohen et al. 2013; He et al. 2020). Metallic ions leaching
effects was also detected in P. subcapitata and S. cerevisiae (Moriyama et al. 2019)
(Fig. 9.1).
   The harmful effects of quantum dots (QDs) are linked to releasing metal ions like
cadmium, lead, and arsenic from their cores due to oxidation by environmental
factors. These QDs can enter mitochondria, leading to structural alterations and
organelle malfunctions. The introduction of Cadmium based QDs into cells prompts
the creation of free Cd2+ ions, resulting in oxidative stress (Singh et al. 2012;
9   Health and Environmental Hazards Associated with the Synthesis. . .             179


Ambrosone et al. 2012). Furthermore, the interaction of nanoparticles with cells can
damage the cytoskeleton. For instance, titanium dioxide (TiO2) nanoparticles can
cause tubulin, a protein, to undergo structural alterations and hinder its assembly into
microtubules. This interference disrupts processes like cellular transport, division,
and movement. In human umbilical vein endothelial cells (HUVECs), cytoskeletal
damage impedes the development of adhesive complexes that connect the cytoskel-
eton with the extracellular matrix. As a result, the formation of the vascular network
is disrupted. Moreover, the toxicity of nanoparticles can disrupt processes such as
cell specialization and the creation of proteins while also triggering the activation of
inﬂammation-related genes and the production of inﬂammatory substances. It’s
crucial to highlight that typical defense mechanisms don’t impact nanoparticles.
Notably, larger PEGylated nanoparticles are taken up more effectively by
macrophages than smaller ones, causing nanoparticles to accumulate within the
body (Walkey et al. 2012). Research has revealed that superparamagnetic iron
oxide nanoparticles can hinder or even completely suppress the differentiation of
stem cells into bone-forming cells (osteogenic differentiation). These nanoparticles
also prompt the generation of signaling molecules and tumor antigens. Additionally,
the interaction between nanoparticles and cells heightens the expression of genes
responsible for lysosome formation, disrupting their function and impeding protein
synthesis. An investigation into the harmful effects of nanoparticles with varying
compositions on lung epithelial cells and human tumor cell lines demonstrated that
nanoparticles prompt the production of inﬂammatory mediators, such as IL8
(Sukhanova et al. 2018). According to Park and Park (2009), who conducted studies
in controlled cell environments and living organisms, exposure to silicon
nanoparticles leads to an increased expression of proinﬂammatory cytokines like
interleukin 1 beta (IL-1β) and tumor necrosis factor-alpha (TNFα).
    The cytotoxic effects triggered by nanomaterials arise from their interaction with
cellular components at the nanomaterial surface. As the particle diameter diminishes,
the surface area undergoes exponential expansion. Consequently, even with identical
compositions, nanomaterials can exhibit signiﬁcantly varying levels of cytotoxicity
due to particle size and surface reactivity. Moreover, particle size signiﬁcantly
inﬂuences how nanomaterials are delivered within cells and distributed in living
organisms. In evaluating nanomaterial cytotoxicity, it is crucial to consider both
chemical attributes and size-dependent effects, along with the extent of such size-
dependent impacts (Lefebvre et al. 2015). To elicit cytotoxicity and provoke an
inﬂammatory response in animal models, nanoparticles must traverse the epithelial
barrier. Here, nanoparticle size emerges as a pivotal determinant. In scenarios
involving the inhalation of nanoparticles, these particles penetrate deeply into the
lung parenchyma. Diverse nanoparticle sizes exhibit distinct distribution patterns in
the respiratory tract, a phenomenon inﬂuenced by factors like Stoke’s and Reynold’s
numbers. Initial distribution occurs predominantly in the gas phase, but upon
inhalation, nanoparticles transition into the liquid phase present in respiratory ﬂuids
(Gliga et al. 2014).
    In a recent study by Braakhuis et al. (2016), the cytotoxicity induced by inhaled
silver nanoparticles of varying sizes was evaluated. Silver nanoparticles measuring
180                                                           K. U. Fakhri and F. A. Khan


18, 34, 60, and 160 nm were generated and administered to rats at different
concentrations. Following exposure, the rats were sacriﬁced to measure the silver
nanoparticle content in their lungs. The results showed that the 18 and 34 nm silver
nanoparticles triggered dose-dependent expression of lactate dehydrogenase (LDH),
an indicator of cell damage, after 24 h. Conversely, the 60 and 160 nm nanoparticles
showed no dose-dependent cell damage. Despite an overall higher presence of the
larger 60 and 160 nm nanoparticles in the lungs, the alveoli contained more of the
18 and 34 nm nanoparticles. The authors suggested that the increased surface area of
the nano-scaled particles was the primary factor contributing to the toxicological
effects of the silver nanoparticles.
   Oxidative stress generally occurs when an imbalance between the generation of
reactive species (RS) and the level of antioxidant defenses, either enzymatic or
non-enzymatic (Halliwell and Gutteridge 2015) in the cells. There are different
types of nanomaterials, such as monoxide nanomaterials that possess a pro-oxidant
capability that is responsible for the production of reactive oxygen species (ROS)
(Nel et al. 2006). Moreover, reactive oxygen species ROS production can occur
when metallic ions induce cell death in cells. Oxidative stress is related to the
impairment of biological molecules such as lipids, carbohydrates, proteins and
DNA (Halliwell and Gutteridge 2015) due to cytotoxicity induced by nanomaterials
(Nel et al. 2006; Xia et al. 2006). Metal ions released from nanoparticles (NPs) have
been demonstrated to participate in redox cycling and chemical catalysis through
reactions like the Fenton reaction (H2O2 + Fe2+ → Fe3+ + HO- + OH) or Fenton-
like reactions (Ag + H2O2 + H+ = Ag+ + OH + H2O). The separated metal ions,
such as Ag+, also lead to the deactivation of cellular enzymes, disruption of
membrane structures, interference with electron transport processes, lowered redox
potential levels, impaired mitochondrial membrane potentials (MMP), and height-
ened accumulation of ROS within cells (Yu et al. 2020).
    Lipids, particularly unsaturated fatty acids, hold signiﬁcance as crucial cellular
components, playing vital roles in both cell membrane structure and function.
Nanoparticles exhibit a strong attraction to the cell membrane, where they can trigger
the generation of ROS and induce lipid peroxidation in the outer membrane. This
alteration in fatty acid composition within the cell membrane can potentially lead to
heightened cell permeability, causing unregulated movement of NPs from the
external environment into the cytoplasm. This inﬁltration may pave the way for
ongoing cellular harm. Upon entering the cell, internalized NPs initiate subsequent
rounds of ROS bursts. The excessive ROS accumulation subsequently results in the
rupture of organelle membranes, leading to the release of organelle contents (Foster
et al. 2011), deactivation of cell receptors (Hirano et al. 2008), the liberation of
lactate dehydrogenase (LDH), and further irreversible cellular damage (Gu et al.
2014).
    Consequently, ROS production by various monoxide nanomaterials may cause
cell oxidative disturbances, cell membrane damage, reduced mitochondrial function,
chromatin condensation, DNA damage, and cell death via apoptotic pathway were
reported in bacteria (Kumar et al. 2011; Rodea et al. 2012), yeasts (Zhang et al. 2016;
Babele et al. 2018; Sousa et al. 2018a), freshwater and marine microalgae (Rodea
9   Health and Environmental Hazards Associated with the Synthesis. . .                        181




Fig. 9.2 Illustration of exposure of nanomaterials to human cells leading to apoptotic-induced cell
death


et al. 2012; von Moos et al. 2015; Xia et al. 2015; Suman et al. 2015; Oukarroum
et al. 2017; Sousa et al. 2018b), carp (Cyprinus carpio) larva (Naeemi et al. 2020),
and human cell lines (Karlsson et al. 2008; Park et al. 2008; Lu et al. 2015; Rajiv
et al. 2016; Subramaniam et al. 2020) (Fig. 9.2).
    ROS target the hydrophobic segments of amino acids, disrupting peptide bonds
and interfering with the proper functioning of these proteins. Another outcome of
oxidative damage is protein carbonylation. Proteins affected by carbonylation form
chemically irreversible aggregates that cannot be broken down by proteasomes,
ultimately causing a permanent loss of function in these proteins. Gurunathan
et al. (2019) demonstrated that platinum nanoparticles (PtNPs) could intensify
ROS production and elevate levels of carbonylated proteins. This, in turn, hindered
the proliferation of osteosarcoma cells and contributed to the process of apoptosis. In
a separate instance, nanoparticles derived from combustion and friction (CFDNPs)
were found to accumulate in the brains of young adults with Alzheimer’s disease.
This accumulation likely spurred the generation of ROS, resulting in protein
misfolding, aggregation, and the formation of ﬁbrils. Additionally, research by
Pelgrift and Friedman (2013) indicated that magnesium nanoparticles (Mg NPs)
might impede gene transcription or directly harm proteins.
