9.5      Methods of Evaluating Nanomaterial-Induced Toxicities

Two different methods are used to evaluate the toxicities induced by the
nanomaterials: in vitro and in vivo. In both cases, the impact of nanomaterials is
evaluated by exposing the cells or tissues or individuals (animals or humans) to
various concentrations of the nanomaterials and examining their effects using
cellular, molecular and genetic techniques. Control samples (without nanomaterials)
are included in the studies to compare the effects of both methods. The main
parameters used to evaluate toxicity induced by the nanomaterials are
bio-distribution and cellular uptake of nanomaterials and the assessment of the
induction of toxicity on the cell damage, DNA condensation, mitochondrial damage,
cell membrane disruption, and cell death (Hillegass et al. 2010; Kumar et al. 2017;
Domb et al. 2021).


9.5.1    In Vitro Method of Testing

The application of in vitro model is based on the cell lines or primary cell lines
developed from animal and human tissue biopsies. These cell lines are preferred due
to their homogeneity and stability during the in vitro testing and help get reproduc-
ible results. These cell lines are generated from different organs/tissues of the
animals or humans, either from normal or diseased individuals. These cell lines
could be cancerous cells or normal cells, which can be artiﬁcially immortalized, with
a high rate of growth and proliferation, making them easy to cultivate. These cells are
available in large quantities at an economical cost (Drasler et al. 2017; Joris et al.
2013). Furthermore, different cell types can be used for different applications, for
example, cells from the immune system such as macrophages, epithelial cells,
ﬁbroblasts, cells and cells from lung, cells are different from cells of the gastrointes-
tinal tract, and neurons. Moreover, lung and gastrointestinal cells are frequently used
for testing the inhalation and ingestion of nanomaterials (Drasler et al. 2017). These
cell lines are commercially available from the American Cell Culture Type (ATCC),
USA with reasonable price. There are limitations for the 2D culture cell lines as they
don’t represent the actual tissues or organs, and to overcome this probleb,
researchers have developed 3D cell lines which represent the actual tissues and
organs and however these 3D cell lines are expensive and donor variations is another
issues associated with this (Rach et al. 2014; Lacroix et al. 2018; Kooter et al. 2019;
Bessa et al. 2021).
    To examine in vitro toxicity of the nanomaterials, different methods are used
where cell viability, cell death or rate of cell proliferation are measured after the
treatments of the nanomaterials. The most widely used in vitro methods of
nanomaterials testing are quantitative colorimetric or ﬂuorimetric assays such as
MTT (3-(4,5-dimethylthiazol-2-yl)-2,5-diphenyltetrazolium bromide) assay, XTT
(tetrazolium dye) assay, WST-1 assay, Alamar blue staining, neutral red uptake
staining. The in vitro tests also include LDH, trypan blue assays whereas cell
division or proliferation can be assessed through clonogenic assays or cell counting.
9   Health and Environmental Hazards Associated with the Synthesis. . .            183


Furthermore, programmed cell death or apoptosis assays such as TUNEL assay,
caspase activation or apoptosis/necrosis assays can be used to toxicity induced by
nanomaterials (Hillegass et al. 2010; Kumar et al. 2017; Park et al. 2009; Takhar and
Mahant 2011; Gupta and Xie 2018; Savage et al. 2019; Tirumala et al. 2021).
    The oxidative stress is recognized as the chief mechanism of nanomaterial-
induced toxicity (Kumar et al. 2017). It has been reported that treatment of
nanomaterials caused the production of ROS-reactive oxygen species and
RNS-reactive nitrogen species in the treated cells (Kumar et al. 2017). It has been
reported that cells normally generate ROS to maintain normal metabolism or
homeostasis, then overproduction of ROS can interfere cell signal transduction
pathways. The ROS overproduction also induced programmed cell death or apopto-
sis and DNA damage (Naz et al. 2020; Kumar et al. 2017; Tirumala et al. 2021).
    Genotoxicity is the possible injury made by nanomaterials to the DNA of the cells
(Drasler et al. 2017). There are two types of genotoxicity primary genotoxicity and
secondary genotoxicity. In primary genotoxicity, the genetic daismage caused by
nanomaterials themselves, whereas in secondary genotoxicity, nanomaterials do not
interact directly with the target cell then produce an inﬂammatory response in
neighboring cells, which resulted the over-production of the ROS which ﬁnally
caused the DNA damage. It must be remembered that primary genotoxicity may
also mediated by ROS overproduction to damage the DNA (Drasler et al. 2017;
Achawi et al. 2021). To evaluate DNA damage, gene mutations, and chromosomal
damage different DNA markers are used (Kohl et al. 2020) and these include in vitro
mammalian cell gene mutation assay and the in vitro mammalian cell micronucleus
assay respectively (Drasler et al. 2017; El Yamani et al. 2017). The micronucleus
assay is also used to assess the chromosomal loss in the nanomaterials treated cells.
In addition, the comet assay is widely used to detect the genotoxic potential of
nanomaterials, which help to detect allow single- and double-stranded DNA breaks
in individual cells (Bajpayee et al. 2019).
    There are many advantages of using in vitro models in testing nanomaterial-
induced toxicities. The application of cell lines from variety of tissues and organs
help to study the impact of the nanomaterials on individual type of cells. The in vitro
method also help to screen the effects of a very small quantity of nanomaterials and
studies can be conducted with different concentration and with time intervals such as
6 h, 12 h, 24 h, 48 h, 72 h or 96-h post-treatment of nanomaterials (Takhar and
Mahant 2011; Fröhlich and Salar-Behzadi 2014). Another advantage of using the
in vitro method is that the experiment can be performed under controlled testing
conditions; they help reduce the experiment-to-experiment variability (Russell and
Burch 1959). The in vitro method is based on the “3R” rule, which was introduced in
the year 1959 aiming to Reduce, Reﬁne, and Replace animal experiments (Fröhlich
2018), which is now commonly fortiﬁed by international legislation (Fröhlich 2018).
In 2010, the European Commission requested the partial and even full replacement
of animal studies in drug testing (European Commission 2010) as the in vitro method
is easy, faster, and economical (Kumar et al. 2011).
184                                                             K. U. Fakhri and F. A. Khan


9.5.2    In Vivo Method of Testing

The leading beneﬁt of in vivo models is that they can mimic human physiology and
allow the systemic evaluation of the effects activated by nanomaterials. The impact
of nanomaterials can also well examine the body’s antioxidant defense and body
tissue repair mechanism. Certainly, in vivo testing is the most technically relevant
model as it can capture the effects of nanomaterials on the body tissues or organisms.
Another key advantage of in vivo models is that they allow the measurement of
nanomaterials’ different human physiological impacts through absorption, distribu-
tion, metabolism, and excretion in the clinical. In vivo, testing has been initiated for a
range of nanomaterials mainly exposed to humans by the oral and dermal routes. For
the respiratory inhalation of nanomaterials, the application of intra-tracheal instilla-
tion is employed in rodents (rats and mice), as these rodents are commonly used
species for toxicity testing. In addition, as the in vivo testing is a very expensive
affairs, it’s technically not possible to evaluate all nanomaterials through in vivo
method. It has been estimated that $1.18 billion is needed for comprehensive in vivo
long-term testing in the animals’ studies (Choi et al. 2009). Another negative aspect
of animal testing is species differences as different animals produce varied physio-
logical responses. For example, in the animals, the autophagy inhibitor
3-methyladenine decreased acute lung injury triggered by poly-amidoamine
dendrimers, but it lacks clinical usefulness due to the low stability of the
nanomaterials (Jud et al. 2013). Moreover, there is a difference in the immune
response to the nanomaterials in animals and human which is another issue with
in vivo testing (Wheeler et al. 1990).
    But there are few speciﬁc experiments which are not possible to conduct in vitro
testing such as retention of inhaled particles and long-term studies cannot be
performed in vitro. The use of in vivo testing where nanomaterials with size
≤100 nm can be inhaled and can be retained in the body for longer period of time,
for example, 75% of 100 nm carbon nanomaterials were retained for more than 48 h
in hamster lungs (Geiser and Kreyling 2010). Explanations for the prolonged
retention include deep penetration of nanomaterials into the mucus lung lining
layer. In both cases, interaction with airway lung cells and probability of transmigra-
tion is increased. Astonishingly, not all nanomaterials reserved in the lung cells but
they migrate to lymph nodes or other body tissues (Kreyling et al. 2009). One study
in dogs showed that inhalation of nanomaterials appeared in the lung epithelium,
where macrophage-mediated clearance occurred and about 90% of the inhaled
nanomaterials were recovered in the lung (Semmler-Behnke et al. 2007). The
capability of macrophages to swallow nanomaterials is lower for nanomaterials
than for micro materials (Geiser et al. 1990). It has been reported that after 24 h of
nanomaterial inhalation, only 1.7% of macrophages contained nanomaterials com-
pared to 12–15% with micro materials (Geiser et al. 1994).
9   Health and Environmental Hazards Associated with the Synthesis. . .            185


9.6       Reduction of Nanoparticles Toxicity

Comprehending the mechanism behind nanoparticle toxicity is essential to guide the
development of nanoparticles that have a reduced impact on the environment.
Redesign strategies should be chosen considering both the primary mode of toxicity
and the preservation of the nanomaterial’s intended functionality. To diminish
interactions with cell surfaces, nanoparticles can be designed with a negative surface
charge, employ ligands like polyethylene glycol to mitigate protein binding, or adopt
shapes that discourage cell surface attachment. To curb the dissolution of
nanoparticles into harmful ions, toxic components can be substituted with less
harmful elements possessing similar traits. The nanoparticle’s surface can be capped
with a protective shell; its morphology can be tailored to minimize surface area and
dissolution, or chelating agents can be introduced to the nanomaterial’s surface. To
lower the generation of ROS, the material’s band gap can be adjusted by utilizing
different elements or doping. An outer shell layer can be added to prevent direct
contact with the nanoparticle’s core, and antioxidant molecules can be afﬁxed to the
nanoparticle surface. During the nanoparticle redesign, validating that the chosen
strategy effectively reduces toxicity to organisms in relevant environmental settings
is imperative. Additionally, it’s crucial to ensure that the modiﬁed nanomaterial still
retains the crucial physicochemical properties that initially warranted its
incorporation into a product or device (Buchman et al. 2019).
    Nanotechnology-enabled formulations for pesticides have emerged as a
promising avenue to enhance pesticide efﬁciency while mitigating their environ-
mental impact. In a recent study, researchers focused on optimizing the utilization of
prochloraz, an active pesticide ingredient, by encapsulating it within mesoporous
silica nanoparticles (MSNs). The MSNs were functionalized with chitosan to
enhance controlled release capabilities, covalently linked to the nanoparticle surface
via a silane coupling agent. This resulted in the synthesis of MSNs-
chitosan@prochloraz nanoparticles. The investigation revealed that these
nanoparticles exhibited a notable loading efﬁciency of prochloraz (25.4% w/w)
and effectively improved the stability of the pesticide under light exposure. The
nanoparticles demonstrated remarkable dual-responsive attributes, reacting sensi-
tively to esterase activity and pH changes. This responsiveness was associated with
controlled release behavior. The biological assessment revealed that the interplay of
fruit-produced acid and enzymes efﬁciently triggered the opening of the “gate”
enforced by chitosan, facilitating an on-demand release of the pesticide in response
to esterase activity and pH ﬂuctuations. Comparative analysis with prochloraz
emulsiﬁable concentrate demonstrated that applyingMSNs-chitosan@prochloraz
nanoparticles before harvesting led to extended efﬁcacy and enhanced antifungal
activity against citrus diseases. Importantly, these nanoparticle-based formulations
exhibited signiﬁcantly reduced toxicity to zebraﬁsh, with toxicity levels over
six times lower compared to prochloraz technical, suggesting MSNs-
chitosan@prochloraz nanoparticles as a sustainable and eco-friendly preharvest
treatment in agricultural applications (Liang et al. 2018).
186                                                           K. U. Fakhri and F. A. Khan


    Prior research has demonstrated the adverse effects of copper oxide nanoparticles
(CuO) on the growth of duckweed (Landoltia punctata), a prominent aquatic plant
consumed by waterfowl and commonly found in agricultural runoff channels in
temperate climates. Nonetheless, previous investigations of CuO nanoparticle toxic-
ity toward duckweed have predominantly been carried out in systems devoid of
dissolved organic matter. In this present study, the inhibition of duckweed growth
was found to be closely linked to the concentration of aqueous Cu2+. The most
pronounced growth inhibition stemmed from aqueous CuCl2, and for nanoparticle
systems, the inhibitory effect increased as the size of CuO particles decreased.
    Evaluations of CuO NP dissolution in ½ Hoagland’s solution revealed that
dissolution rates escalated with diminishing particle size. Furthermore, Suwannee
River Humic and Fulvic acids (HA; FA) ampliﬁed the dissolution process. However,
the study’s ﬁndings indicate that both Humic acid and, to a lesser degree, Fulvic acid
actually mitigate the toxicity of CuO NPs and free ionized Cu toward duckweed.
This amelioration is attributed to the inhibition of copper availability facilitated by
forming Cu-dissolved organic matter complexes. These outcomes align with
predictions from speciation modeling software, suggesting alterations in copper
speciation driven by dissolved organic matter, thereby inﬂuencing toxicity within
natural environments (Rippner et al. 2018). The dissolution of ZnO nanoparticles
and the subsequent release of Zn2+ ions trigger a cascade of sublethal and lethal
cellular responses, which can be mitigated through iron doping. Introducing iron
alters the particle matrix and decelerates the particle dissolution rate. To assess
whether iron doping of ZnO particles also leads to reduced toxic effects in vivo,
toxicological investigations were conducted using rodent and zebraﬁsh models.
Initially, a fresh batch of ZnO nanoparticles doped with 1–10 wt % of Fe was
synthesized. Iron doping, akin to the effects observed with the metal chelator
DTPA, counteracted the inhibitory effects of Zn2+ on zebraﬁsh hatching. In a murine
model utilizing oropharyngeal aspiration, iron-doped particles were associated with
decreased polymorphonuclear cell counts and lowered IL-6 mRNA production.
Furthermore, the doped particles elicited reduced expression of heme oxygenase
1 in the murine lung. In rat studies involving intratracheal instillation, iron doping
correlated with diminished polymorphonuclear cell counts, LDH levels, and albumin
levels. Collectively, the aforementioned data underline that iron doping presents a
plausible and safe design approach to attenuate ZnO toxicity in both animals and the
environment (Xia et al. 2011).
    TiO2@Ag nanoparticles (TiAgNps) exhibited notable antileishmanial effects.
However, while a lethal dose of TiAgNps was found to be toxic for L. tropica
promastigotes, it also posed harm to macrophage cells. Additionally, concentrations
of TiAgNps that were non-toxic had limited effectiveness in inhibiting L. tropica
promastigotes and amastigotes. Interestingly, when combined with other
antileishmanial compounds, such as meglumine antimoniate (MA) at non-toxic
concentrations, the efﬁcacy of both agents appeared to increase while their toxicities
decreased. The combinations of TiAgNps and MA at concentrations that did not
induce toxicity exhibited remarkable effectiveness against both promastigotes and
amastigotes. In contrast, the application of MA alone did not display any inhibitory
9   Health and Environmental Hazards Associated with the Synthesis. . .              187


effects. The combined treatments reduced L. tropica promastigote proliferation,
contrasting with the outcomes seen with sole MA application. The efﬁcacy of the
TiAgNps-MA combinations signiﬁcantly surpassed that of using MA alone at
non-toxic concentrations. These results suggest a strong potential for the develop-
ment of novel antileishmanial drugs targeting leishmaniasis (Abamor et al. 2017).


9.7       Nanomaterials and Regulations for Preventing Health
          Hazards

Similar to many emerging technologies, including nascent medicine and medical
devices, there is a growing discussion surrounding potential negative outcomes from
using nanoscale particles. As nanotechnology becomes more prevalent, it becomes
crucial to thoroughly comprehend the risks associated with exposure to
nanoparticles, the various pathways through which they enter the body, and the
molecular processes underlying any potential toxicity. These minuscule particles can
inﬁltrate the body through the skin, respiratory system, or digestive tract,
accumulating in multiple organs and potentially triggering unfavorable biological
responses by altering the physicochemical characteristics of living matter at the
nanoscale. Moreover, the toxicity of nanoparticles is contingent on factors like
their persistence within the body and whether they are eliminated from different
entry points, as well as the host’s ability to effectively counteract and eliminate these
particles. Recent investigations have pinpointed nanoparticles as culprits for toxicity
in various organs. Consequently, it is prudent to assess the balance between risks and
beneﬁts when considering the incorporation of nanoparticles into technological or
medical advancements. For this purpose, many global organizations have outlined
the guidelines (Medina et al. 2009).
    The goal of these global organizations such as the Royal Society and Royal
Academy of Engineering (2004), NIOSH (2005), Jacobstein (2006), CEST (2008),
Tomellini and Giordani (2008), Luigi (2009), Nanocyl (2009), NNI (2011), Forloni
(2012), VCI (2012), BASF (2013), and BIAC (2013). (Schulte et al. 2014) to
provide the rules and guidelines for the successful production of the nanomaterials
for various applications. Preferably, the idea of responsible development of
nanomaterials is to prevent the harmful effects of the nanomaterials to humans and
the environment. The front-line people who are directly exposed to the
nanomaterials are researchers, and industry workers who are working in both
government laboratories and private companies. These people are the ﬁrst ones
who are exposed to the potential hazards of nanomaterials during the research and
development, manufacturing, production, recycling, and clearance of nanomaterials.
It has been found that workers often have the highest exposure in the development of
nanomaterials when nanomaterials are produced. If exposure to nanomaterials has
harmful effects on the workers, then in that case, the production process is not being
conscientiously developed. Because of these issues, occupational safety and health is
the basis of responsible nanomaterials production (Maynard and Kuempel 2005;
Schulte and Salamanca-Buentello 2007; Seaton et al. 2010).
188                                                                    K. U. Fakhri and F. A. Khan


   There is an ethical requirement for the workers who are working in the production
of nanomaterials, and these workers have the right to have a safe and clean work
environment (Gewirth 1986). Consequently, the safety of workers has been
recognized as a basic human right as per the 2008 Seoul Declaration on Safety
and Health at Work (ILO 2008). Importantly, these rights bring proportionate
responsibilities for employers and government authorities to protect workers from
harm effects of nanomaterials. These guidelines were codiﬁed in legal methods such
as the Occupational Safety and Health Act, the Mine Safety and Health Act, and the
Toxic Substances Control Act in the United States. In addition, similar legislation
was also prepared globally, such as WHO (1994), BAuA (2007), Nanosafe (2008),
ISO (2009), Japan NIOSH (2009), Saner and Pelley (2009), (Murashov et al. 2011;
Nakanishi 2011a, b). Principal criteria for the responsible development of nanotech-
nology must be improved to protect workers and consumers from exposure to
nanomaterials (Kreider and Halperin 2011).

Future Trends and Innovation in Nano Drug
Delivery for Cancer Therapy, Application                                                   10
of siRNA (Nanoparticle-Based RNA)
Therapy, Ultrasound Linked Nano-Cancer
Therapeutics, and Application
of Exosomes-Based Cancer Therapy

Kanwal Abbasi, Kauser Siddiqui, Saeeda Bano, Samina Iqbal,
and Shagufta A. Shaikh


   Abstract
   The chapter discusses the roles of nanoparticles and hybrid nanoparticles for drug
   delivery in chemotherapy, targeted therapy, and immunotherapy and describes
   the targeting mechanism of nanoparticle-based drug delivery as well as its
   function on reversing drug resistance, compared to conventional drugs,
   nanoparticle-based drug delivery has speciﬁc advantages, such as improved
   stability and biocompatibility, enhanced permeability, retention effect, and pre-
   cise targeting. The application and development of nano-system, which
   incorporates the combined properties of different nanoparticles, has led this
   type of drug-carrier system to the next level. The chapter also focuses on
   combinations of siRNA with chemotherapeutic drug delivery systems for the
   treatment of cancer and gives an overview of several nanocarrier formulations in
   both research and clinical applications. The use of various combinations of
   ultrasounds and nano-systems for cancer treatment has been reviewed, the current
   chapter introduces the biogenesis and uptake of exosomes and compares different
   approaches for isolation and drug loading, focusing on the application and current
   challenges of exosomes as drug delivery vehicles in cancer therapy.

   Keywords

   Nano drug delivery · Cancer therapy · Application of siRNA · Nanoparticle-based
   RNA therapy · Ultrasound linked nano-cancer therapeutics · Application of
   exosomes-based cancer therapy

10.1     Introduction

Nanotechnology has been widely studied and used in cancer therapy because
nanoparticles (NPs) can play an important role as a drug delivery system. Compared
with conventional drugs, nanoparticle-based drug delivery has special advantages
such as better stability and biocompatibility, better permeability and retention effect,
and precise targeting. The application and development of hybrid nanoparticles,
10   Future Trends and Innovation in Nano Drug Delivery for Cancer Therapy,. . .   199


which combine the combined properties of different nanoparticles, have taken this
type of drug delivery systems to the next level (Bahrami et al. 2017). Nanoparticle-
based drug delivery systems have been shown to play a role in overcoming cancer-
related drug resistance. Nanoparticles offer several advantages in cancer therapy and
have been shown to be effective in solving the problem of delivering hydrophobic
agents, protecting bio-sensitive cargo, and promoting controlled drug release.6-
8 Nanoparticles are also reliable platforms for tumor surveillance and
microenvironment-responsive technologies.
   RNAi is an important therapeutic strategy to prevent gene overexpression in
many diseases, including cancer. RNAi processes allow siRNA to recognize and
degrade a homologous mRNA sequence in the cell. However, the limited potential of
siRNA to target cells, poor cellular uptake, off-target effects, and their degradation
by serum nucleases in the systemic circulation are factors limiting their therapeutic
use. Nanocarriers for the delivery of siRNA cargo and their combined use with
anticancer drugs, as well as their applications in cancer therapy, are undergoing a
period of clinical translation (Dolly et al. 2023). The current status of siRNA
therapies in clinical trials is explored, providing a systematic and comprehensive
overview of siRNA-based therapies for effective anticancer therapy. In the current
chapter we discussed the future trends and innovation in nano drug delivery for
cancer therapy, application of siRNA (nanoparticle-based RNA) therapy.


10.2      NPs in Cancer Therapy

The NPs used in medical treatment usually have speciﬁc sizes, shapes, and surface
characteristics as these three aspects have a major inﬂuence on the efﬁciency of the
nano-drug delivery and thus control therapeutic efﬁcacy.
    The composition of nanoparticles can be varied, because the precursors can be
biological lipids, dextran, lactic acid, phospholipids, chitosan or chemicals such as
silica, carbon, metals, and various polymers. Interactions between biological
components of nanoparticles are different from interactions with non-biological
components. Biodegradable nanoparticles are necessary in drug delivery to transport
the drug and release it at the destination. NPs with a diameter of 10–100 nm are
generally considered suitable for cancer therapy due to their ability to efﬁciently
deliver drugs and improve permeability and retention (EPR) effect (Venturoli and
Rippe 2005). Smaller particles can easily leak from normal blood vessels (less than
1–2 nm) to damage normal cells and can be easily ﬁltered by the kidneys (less than
10 nm in diameter), while particles less than 100 nm in diameter can be eliminated of
the blood by phagocytes (Decuzzi et al. 2009).
    Nanoparticles are essentially non-toxic, chemically stable and biocompatible
materials, and this nature allows them to be chosen as an effective drug delivery
vehicle. Nanotechnology and nanoparticles have attracted great interest in cancer
therapy because they can provide better and targeted drug delivery systems to
overcome the shortcomings of conventional chemotherapy. Limitations of chemo-
therapy include inefﬁcient delivery of drugs to the target due to physical and
200                                                                              K. Abbasi et al.




Fig. 10.1 Different types of nanoparticles (NPs) for cancer therapy. NPs applied to drug delivery
systems include organic NPs, inorganic NPs, and hybrid NPs. The organic NPs contain liposome-
based NPs, polymer-based NPs, and dendrimers. Among polymer-based NPs, polymeric NPs and
polymeric micelles are common. The inorganic NPs consist of gold NPs (Au NPs), carbon
nanotubes, silica NPs, magnetic NPs, and quantum dots. Hybrid NPs combine the advantages of
different NPs, including lipid-polymer hybrid NPs, organic-inorganic hybrid NPs, and cell
membrane-coated NPs (Yao et al. 2020)

biochemical barriers, and drug resistance at the tumor level due to cellular and
non-cellular mechanisms that limit drug action, leading to recurrence and high
mortality. The different properties of NP determine their therapeutic effect in cancer
treatment. Different types of NP for cancer treatment are shown in Fig. 10.1 (Yao
et al. 2020).


10.3      NPs Used for Drug Delivery in Cancer Therapy

Several innovative drug delivery strategies are currently being used to treat cancer,
and new cancer therapies have been developed using a variety of nanomaterials,
including organic and inorganic particles, as well as synthetically produced lipids,
proteins, and polymers. Drug delivery encapsulated in NPs offers several advantages
over direct delivery of reﬁned chemotherapy drugs. These include improved drug
delivery, protection of encapsulated drugs from degradation in the bloodstream,
targeted drug delivery, effective therapy with reduced systemic toxicity, improved
10   Future Trends and Innovation in Nano Drug Delivery for Cancer Therapy,. . .   201


drug solubility, and drug pharmacodynamic and pharmacokinetic properties
(Perrault et al. 2009; Wong et al. 2011; Yang et al. 2014).


10.3.1 Inorganic NPs for Drug Delivery in Cancer Therapy

Inorganic NCs have been shown to be very useful in cancer therapy due to their large
surface area and volume and easy conjugation with various anticancer drugs
(Table 10.1). In addition, biocompatibility, low toxicity, and the ability to control
drug release facilitated the use of inorganic NPs, such as mesoporous silica NCs
(MSNPs), gold NPs (AuNPs), magnetic NPs (MNPs), carbon nanotubes NPs (CNT-
NP), graphene oxide, and quantum dots (QDs) as drug carriers (Kaushik et al. 2022).
   The investigated inorganic NPs include gold NPs (AuNPs), carbon nanotubes
(CNTs), quantum dots, magnetic NPs (MNPs), and silica NPs (SNPs). AuNPs are
the most widely studied inorganic NPs, and gold core-based monolayer-protected
mixed clusters are considered promising candidates for drug delivery system. The
gold core is inert and nontoxic, and surface-functionalized AuNPs have been shown
to enhance drug accumulation in tumors and overcome drug resistance. AuNPs are
involved in multimodal cancer therapy, including gene therapy, photothermal ther-
apy, and immunotherapy (Ali et al. 2021). Carbon nanotubes are tubular materials
with unique biological, physical, and chemical properties that have been used to
deliver anticancer agents such as doxorubicin, paclitaxel, and methotrexate RNA to a
variety of cancers. CNTs generate heat when exposed to near-infrared radiation,
which can be used for thermal ablation in cancer therapy (Debnath and Srivastava
2021). Mesoporous silica nanoparticle carriers are a type of SNPs that have a large
internal pore volume, which allows them to encapsulate the maximum amount of
anticancer drugs, and the supramolecular components act as a cap that allows the
drugs to be trapped and released (Cheng et al. 2019; Lei et al. 2019). Porous silica
NPs have shown great potential in immunotherapy due to their immunoadjuvant
properties, including promoting antigen cross-presentation, lymphocyte polariza-
tion, and interferon-g (IFN-g) secretion (Fontana et al. 2017). Magnetic NPs
(MNPs) used for drug delivery usually contain metal or metal oxide NPs. To
improve stability and biocompatibility, MNPs are usually coated with organic
materials, including polymers and fatty acids. They have been shown to have high
efﬁcacy in chemotherapy, gene therapy, and magnetic hyperthermia using MNPs to
achieve thermal ablation of tumors, providing an alternative anticancer treatment
(Basoglu et al. 2018; Mandriota et al. 2019).

10.3.1.1 Liposomes
A liposome, the ﬁrst nanosized drug approved for clinical use, consists of an outer
lipid layer and a core surrounding either a hydrophobic or hydrophilic drug.
Liposomes can perform many functions by altering the structure of the lipid bilayer,
including mimicking the biophysical properties of living cells (e.g., motility and
deformation). which can help achieve more effective therapeutic drug delivery
(Zylberberg and Matosevic 2016). Liposomes are a good platform for the in vivo

delivery of many antitumor drugs such as doxorubicin and paclitaxel, other chemo-
therapeutic agents, and nucleic acids. paclitaxel liposomes have better antitumor
efﬁcacy and better bioavailability compared to free paclitaxel. Liposomal doxorubi-
cin has been shown to reduce cardiotoxicity in breast cancer with comparable
efﬁcacy. Liposome-based nanosystems have also provided an alternative to drug
combination that can improve therapeutic efﬁcacy (Gao et al. 2023) (Table 10.2).

10.3.1.2 Polymeric Nanoparticles
Polymer-based NPs are another type of NPs that have speciﬁc structural
arrangements for drug delivery formed by different monomers. Polylactic-co-
glycolic acid (PLGA), a common polymeric NP, involves the copolymerization of
glycolic acid and lactic acid. Due to its better biocompatibility and biodegradability
and EPR effect, PLGA is widely used as a drug delivery carrier, and the multiple
functional groups on its surface improve the ability to load and transport therapeutic
agents (Feltrin et al. 2022). Polyethylene glycol (PEG), also known as polyethylene
oxide and polyoxyethylene, has a linear polymer structure. PEG nanoparticles can
increase the systemic circulation of the drug in the body and reduce the immunoge-
nicity, which leads to their widespread use in cancer treatment. Due to their
non-immunogenic and biodegradable properties, PEG nanoparticles are considered
“stealth” polymers and several, including DOXil, Asclera, and Movantik, have been
approved by the US FDA (Nordstrom et al. 2021). Polyethyleneimine (PEI) is a
cationic polymer composed of repeating linear and branched ethylamine units. Due
to the strong positive charge on the surface of PEI, it is possible to condense nucleic
acid through electrostatic interactions, which enables high transport efﬁciency of
drugs in cancer treatment (Chou et al. 2018; Hailing et al. 2020). The multifunctional
construction methods based on PEI are mainly mediated by covalent coupling amine
groups. 70 For example, the amine groups of PEI can be covalently coupled with
carboxyl, such as 4-(bromomethyl) phenylboronic acid (PBA) (Zhu et al. 2018).
Cyclodextrins (CDs) are degradation products of amylase and consist of
glycopyranoses that form cyclic oligosaccharides. CDs are widely used as delivery
vectors in cancer therapy due to their hydrophilic properties and ability to form host-
guest structures with drugs (Liu et al. 2021a, b). Chitosan (CS) is a natural cationic
polymer composed of β-(1,4)-linked N-acetylglucosamine units produced by
deacetylation of chitin in shellﬁsh shells. Because CS nanoparticles have excellent
biocompatibility and biodegradability, they can be used as a drug coating and drug
delivery vector in cancer therapy (Wang et al. 2022). Dextran is a hydrophilic natural
polymer formed by the condensation of glucose. The dextran chain consists of
α-1,6-glucosidic bonds between glucose monomers, which have hydrophilic,
bio-compatible and biodegradable properties, dextran can be widely used as a
scaffold surface, for coating and delivery of vector for cancer treatment (Chenglong
et al. 2020). Hyaluronic acid (HA) is a natural polymer composed of d-glucuronic
acid and N-acetyl-d-glucosamine. HA is a biodegradable hydrophilic polymer mate-
rial used as a biological scaffold in cancer therapy. HA has tumor-targeting,
non-immunogenic, and biosafety properties, HA has been widely used in drug
delivery systems for drug regulation and sustained release (Huang and Huang
204                                                                             K. Abbasi et al.

2018). Thanks to the glucuronic acid and glucosamine contained in HA, multifunc-
tional design strategies are also used in cancer treatment.

10.3.1.3 Peptides and Protein Nanoparticles
Peptide nanoparticles consist of nanoparticle-sized natural or synthetic amino acids.
Peptide nanoparticles are widely used in cancer therapy, gene delivery, and targeted
drug delivery due to peptide biodegradability and active groups, multifunctional
peptide nanoparticles include physical adsorption and chemical covalent bonding
with various drugs. Protein NPs can be incorporated into biodegradable polymers in
a monolithic particle structure, such as microspheres, to enable controlled and
sustained drug release, and can be produced using proteinaceous materials such as
silk protein ﬁbroin, bovine, human serum albumin, and gliadin. Peptides contain
benzene or macrocyclic structures that allow peptide nanoparticles to interact with
various metals (Rong et al. 2018). The ﬂuorescent peptide nanoparticles with cyclic
peptides modiﬁed them with Arginylglycylaspartic acid (RGD) to encapsulate
epirubicin (EPI) to form a multifunctional RGD-f-PNPs/EPI complex through π–π
stacking and electrostatic interactions. Due to the RGD surface modiﬁcation, the
prepared nanoparticles selectively targeted EC cells and promoted EPI internaliza-
tion. Albumin is a commonly used protein nanoparticle in cancer therapy due to its
high drug loading efﬁciency, with bovine serum albumin (BSA) and human serum
albumin (HSA) commonly used in drug delivery systems (Fan et al. 2018).


10.3.2 Micelles NPs

Micelles are spherical nano-sized structures formed by the self-assembly of amphi-
philic block copolymers with both hydrophilic and hydrophobic moieties in aqueous
solution to form a hydrophobic core and a hydrophilic shell known as polymeric
micelles. Micelles can form naturally at speciﬁc concentrations (critical micelle
concentration (CMC)) and temperatures. In polymeric micelles, the hydrophobic
core acts as a reservoir for hydrophobic drug incorporation, while the hydrophilic
shell stabilizes the core and keeps the polymer and drug intact, making (Ghezzi et al.
2021). The micelles suitable for intravenous administration Genexol-PM [PEG-poly
(D,Llactiide)-paclitaxel] was the ﬁrst cremophor-free polymeric gel containing pac-
litaxel with no adverse effects (Kim et al. 2004).


10.3.3 Self-Assembled Drug NPs

Both organic and inorganic NPs have their own advantages and disadvantages,
because combining the two into one hybrid drug delivery system provides a multi-
functional carrier with better biological properties that can improve therapeutic
efﬁcacy and reduce drug resistance. The spontaneous arrangement of molecules in
ordered geometric structures is called molecular self-assembly, and it gives inor-
ganic and organic structures with unique properties through non-covalent
206                                                                            K. Abbasi et al.


interactions (Yadav et al. 2020). The spontaneity, versatility, cheapness, and sim-
plicity of self-assembly means that it is widely used in the design of nanoscale
biomaterials and has a range of biomedical applications, including drug delivery,
tissue engineering, and regenerative medicine. Self-assembled NPs such as micelles,
polymeric NPs, liposomes and CNTs have been shown to enhance drug delivery,
prolong circulation, regulate drug release kinetics, mediate molecular targeting,
enhance tumor accumulation and bioavailability, enable proper encapsulation, and
protect drug from the external environment (Wang et al. 2020). These self-
assembled or hybrid NPs help to overcome physiological barriers during in vivo
drug delivery.


10.4      Targeted Drug Delivery

Targeting of cancer cells speciﬁcally is a vital characteristic of nano-carriers for drug
delivery, as it enhances the therapeutic efﬁcacy while protecting normal cells from
cytotoxicity. The tumor targeting and the nano-carrier system is design to ﬁrst
understand tumor biology and the interaction between nano-carriers and tumor
cells (Attia et al. 2019). The targeting mechanisms can be broadly divided into
two categories, passive targeting and active targeting (Fig. 10.2) (Holder et al. 2023).


10.4.1 Passive Targeting

Intravenous injection is the most used method of administering anticancer
medications based on nanomaterials. This method avoids the stage of intestinal




Fig. 10.2 Schematic representation of receptor-mediated active targeting and passive targeting
through the EPR effect (Holder et al. 2023)
10   Future Trends and Innovation in Nano Drug Delivery for Cancer Therapy,. . .   207


epithelial absorption that is necessary following oral delivery (Zhao and Castranova
2011). Drugs are successfully transported to the target site by passive targeting in
order to perform a therapeutic function. High cancer cell proliferation causes
neovascularization, and big holes in the vascular wall make tumor arteries more
permeable than healthy vessels. Macromolecules, such as NPs, might leak from
blood arteries supplying the tumor and accumulate within tumor tissue as a result of
the fast and deﬁcient angiogenesis. Depending on the nature, location, and environ-
ment of the tumor, the distances between the endothelial cells in the tumor vascula-
ture might range from 200 to 2000 nm (Rajendra and Stylianopoulos 2010).
Additionally, the nanoparticles are not quickly removed and build up in the tumor
interstitium because of the poor lymphatic function. Passive targeting is based on a
phenomenon known as enhanced permeability and retention (EPR) effect. This
passive process of drug buildup at the tumor sites necessitates prolonged drug
circulation for effective delivery. Nanocarrier accumulation is largely inﬂuenced
by physicochemical characteristics such size, shape (morphology), surface charge,
and surface chemistry (Bertrand et al. 2014). The ﬁrst FDA (the Food and Drug
Administration, national agency of the United States Department of Health and
Human Services) approved nano-drug contains PEGylated liposome-entrapped
doxorubicin (DOX), which is intended to treat ovarian cancer and Kaposi sarcoma
tumors linked to HIV. The drug’s cellular and systemic toxicity was signiﬁcantly
decreased by encasing it inside the lipid substance, which also enhanced the drug’s
pharmacokinetics and allowed for more controlled biodistribution and release
(Navya et al. 2019).

10.4.1.1 Limitations in Passive Targeting
The variety of EPR effects reported inside and between different cancers limits the
therapeutic efﬁcacy of passive targeted methods. It may lead to non-uniform extrav-
asation of nanoparticles into the target location because of varying endothelial gaps
brought on by aggressive tumorous cell proliferation. Furthermore, extravasation has
been shown to depend not only on permeability but also on the blood ﬂow rate
around the tumor site. Vascularization exhibits spatial and temporal heterogeneity
within and between tumor cells adding another level of challenges to passive
targeting (Chauhan and Jain 2013). The nanoparticles permeability is typically
higher in the hypoxic core of the tumor area rather than the periphery. The key to
enhancing penetration into the tumor matrix is the nanomaterial’s size and size-
dependent characteristics. The NPs’ diameter; research has shown that smaller NPs
have better penetration but do not spill into normal channels, whereas
micromolecules are selectively eliminated by immune defense mechanisms. Another
barrier to the efﬁcient deposition of drug-loaded nanocarriers in solid tumors is
interstitial ﬂuid pressure (IFP) (Waite and Roth 2012).
208                                                                       K. Abbasi et al.


10.4.2 Active Targeting

Active targeting directly engages ligands and receptors to target cancer cells speciﬁ-
cally. In order to discriminate between targeted cells and healthy cells, the ligands on
the surface of NPs are chosen to target the molecules that are overexpressed on the
surfaces of cancer cells. Active targeting is essential for the medication and gene
delivery to the desired location, which increases therapeutic effectiveness and limits
adverse effects on healthy tissues. Compared to passively targeted NCs, active
targeting can increase the amount of medication given to the target tumor cell.
Chemical afﬁnities for active targeting are based on many unique molecular
interactions, such as interactions between receptors and ligands, contacts based on
charges, and facilitated interactions based on motif with substrate molecules (Byrne
et al. 2008). A ligand can be made up of a variety of biomolecules, including as
antibodies, proteins, nucleic acids, peptides, carbohydrates, and tiny chemical
compounds like vitamins. Target substrates include surface molecules expressed
by diseased cells, proteins, carbohydrates, or lipids found in organs, chemicals found
in the diseased cells' microenvironment (or secreted there by tumor cells), and even
the local physicochemical environment (Weissleder et al. 2005; Verma and Stellacci
2010; Mout et al. 2012). Internalized NPs can successfully release therapeutic
medications because of receptor-mediated endocytosis, which is brought on by the
contact between ligands on NPs and the receptors on the surface of cancer cells. The
avidity of the nanoparticles for their appropriate targets increases overall when
numerous ligand molecules are added to the nanosystems. Additionally, the
cooperativity effects that result from the binding of one ligand molecule typically
make the binding of succeeding molecules easier, improving the overall efﬁciency of
binding and the actions that follow. The internalization of nanoparticles by target
cells is increased by an active targeting strategy, which also increases the effective-
ness of drug delivery. The ligands speciﬁcally bind to receptors on targeted cells,
which include the transferrin receptor, folate receptor, glycoproteins, and the epider-
mal growth factor receptor (EGFR).


10.4.3 Targeting to Cancer Cells

10.4.3.1 Epidermal Growth Factor Receptor (EGFRs)-Based Active
           Targeting
The ErbB family of tyrosine kinase receptors includes the epidermal growth factor
receptor. It has already been used as a target for cancer treatment. EGFR is
overexpressed in a number of malignancies, including lung, pancreatic, colorectal,
and breast cancers. It is also involved in a number of processes that contribute to
tumor growth and progression. Lysosomes arise as a result of the nanoparticles being
absorbed into the cells by an EGFR-mediated endocytosis process, releasing the
enclosed medicines for cancer treatment. Targeting the human epidermal receptor-
2 (HER-2) is a frequent treatment for breast and stomach cancer that is HER-2
positive. Numerous monoclonal antibodies (Abs) and their fragments, along with
10   Future Trends and Innovation in Nano Drug Delivery for Cancer Therapy,. . .   209


other peptide medicines, have recently been included as targeting units on
nanoparticles against HER2 overexpressing tumors. Nanoparticles now have good
therapeutic efﬁcacy for the treatment of breast cancer thanks to the development of
trastuzumab (TZ), a humanized anti-HER2 Ab. By inhibiting downstream HER2
signaling, it can prevent cell cycle and decrease angiogenesis (Elias et al. 2021).
EGFR-overexpressed cancer cells can be targeted using NPs that have been designed
to incorporate modiﬁed ligands that bind to EGFR. These NPs have improved
bioavailability, prolonged blood circulation, enhanced tumor accumulation, and
reduced off-target side effects, which signiﬁcantly increases therapeutic efﬁcacy.

10.4.3.2 Transferrin (Tf) Receptor-Mediated Active Targeting
Transferrin (tf) is an example of a serum glycoprotein, which is involved in iron
(Fe) delivery and metabolism. Transferrin receptors (TfR), which have a high
afﬁnity for transferrin (Tf), are frequently overexpressed on the surface of a variety
of tumors, including pancreatic, breast, prostate, colon, and lung cancer (Wu et al.
2018a, b). In a recent article (Qi et al. 2022) developed a novel transferrin protein
corona (Tpc)-modiﬁed CuGd nanoplatform (Tpc-CuGd) for tumor-targeting
photothermal and chemodynamic synergistic therapy. These Tf-modiﬁed
nanoparticles have been developed for the active targeted delivery of therapeutic
agents to tumor sites, and they can preferentially deliver therapeutics into
TfR-overexpressing tumors (Arumov et al. 2021).

10.4.3.3 Estrogen Receptor-Mediated Active Targeting
Estrogen is a steroid hormone that is essential for regulating cardiovascular
remodeling, bone homeostasis, brain development, and reproductive system health.
E2 is essential for the development of breast, endometrial, and ovarian malignancies
among the three types of estradiol (estrone (E1), estradiol (E2), and estriol
(E3) (Scabia et al. 2022; Hamilton et al. 2017). The main mechanism by which
estrogens function is the binding and subsequent activation of two estrogen receptors
(ERα and ERβ), which differ structurally. Intracellular ERs can transport estrogen-
modiﬁed nanoparticles toward the nucleus for nuclear targeting after intracellular
uptake via receptor-mediated endocytosis (Thomas and Gustafsson 2011). On
numerous tumor cell surfaces, these receptors have been discovered to be
overexpressed. In a recent application, anti- ERα Ab-modiﬁed AuNPs were
internalized by MCF-7 cells. It was discovered that the ER receptor-mediated
endocytosis pathway for increased tumor treatment allowed these modiﬁed
nanoparticles to be efﬁciently absorbed by tumor cells (Kapara et al. 2020).

10.4.3.4 Cluster of Differentiation (CD) Receptor-Mediated
Surface receptors from the CD receptor family, such as CD14, CD22, CD36, CD44,
and CD133, which are primarily found on cancer stem cells (CSCs), can be exploited
as promising delivery targets to prevent tumor spread. A transmembrane adhesion
glycoprotein called CD44 has been frequently used to target receptors for targeted
tumor treatment among them (Tian et al. 2022). Widespread usage of the biocom-
patible ligand hyaluronic acid (HA) in CD44 receptor-mediated active targeted
210                                                                       K. Abbasi et al.


delivery systems. Due to its availability as a natural polymer as opposed to polymers
that require a multi-step chemical synthesis, it can be easily obtained (Through
CD44 receptor-mediated active targeting, HA-functionalized nanoplatforms can
efﬁciently deliver therapeutic drugs to tumor cells with a superior cytotoxic proﬁle
and tumor killing (Poudel et al. 2020). Compared to groups without the HA modiﬁ-
cation, HA-enveloped nanoparticles substantially inhibited tumor growth. HA is
typically modiﬁed on the surface of nanoparticles to preferentially bind to CD44
receptors that are overexpressed in tumor cells, leading to tumor endocytosis.
Additionally, hyaluronidase, which is widely distributed in cancer cells, has the
ability to break down HA into smaller fragments. The adaptable properties of HA as
a targeted and enzyme-responsive ligand make it an attractive option for use in
particular drug delivery systems (Lei et al. 2021).

10.4.3.5 Folate Receptor (FR)-Mediated Active Targeting
Three subtypes of FRs, a group of glycoproteins, have been identiﬁed: FRα, FRβ,
and FRγ. It should be emphasized that, in contrast to FRγ, which has only been
identiﬁed in hematopoietic cells, FRα and FRβ can bind tightly to the tumor cell
membrane via a glycosylphosphatidylinositol anchor (Holm and Hansen 2020). The
most often created FR subtype, FRα, is overexpressed in a variety of tumor cells,
particularly those that are cancerous of the breast, lung, kidney, cervical, and ovarian
types (Scaranti et al. 2020). Through receptor-mediated endocytosis, FR can provide
folate to tumor cells. For improved uptake of therapeutic drugs by tumor cells, a
number of FA-based nanoplatforms have been developed. A well-known example of
an organic/inorganic hybrid nanoplatform modiﬁed by FA-chitosan conjugates to
load upconverting NaYF4nanoparticles and daunorubicin for tumor therapy
(Fernández et al. 2017). The FA modiﬁcation dramatically increased the cellular
absorption of the nanoparticles. Another example is the preparation of a palladium
nanoplatform for FA-mediated targeted treatment using an FA-conjugated chitosan
loaded with rutin. Through a process known as FR-mediated endocytosis, FRs can
recognize and bind to extracellular FA-modiﬁed nanoparticles before delivering
them into the tumor cells (Sathiyaseelan et al. 2022). The FA (folic acid) component
serves as a tumor-targeting ligand in these nanoparticles. The cell membrane can
invade and pinch off to produce endosomes, which then travel to lysosomes or other
organelles, upon binding to the FR on tumor cells. The drug-encapsulated
nanoparticles have the ability to separate from the FR and successfully release the
medication at the tumor environment for the purpose of treating the tumor. The
endocytosis efﬁciency of the nanoparticles in breast cancer cells was dramatically
increased by the addition of FA to the developed nanoplatform. A cell viability study
shown that the developed nanoplatform signiﬁcantly reduced cell proliferation (Barz
et al. 2010).

10.4.3.6 Glycoproteins-Mediated Active Targeting Systems
The many types of glycoproteins, including lectins, which are non-immunological
proteins that particularly detect and bind to particular carbohydrates, are typically
expressed by cancer cells. The direct lectin targeting pathway targets the
10   Future Trends and Innovation in Nano Drug Delivery for Cancer Therapy,. . .   211


carbohydrates on the surface of cancer cells with lectins attached to NPs, whereas the
reverse lectin targeting mechanism targets the lectins on cancer cells with
carbohydrates moieties integrated into NPs. Another strategy for active targeting is
the conjugation of two cancer-speciﬁc ligands onto a single NP, which can increase
target speciﬁcity.

10.4.3.7 Other Receptor-Mediated Active Targeting Systems
Targeted anticancer nanoparticles have also been created using the other receptors,
such as chemokine, biotin, and luteinizing hormone-releasing hormone (LHRH)
receptors. A G-protein-coupled receptor family known as chemokine receptor type
4 (CXCR4), for instance, plays a crucial role in tumor dissemination by attracting
tumor cells along chemokine gradients (Zheng et al. 2019; Xiao et al. 2021). The
development of circular peptide T22-functionalized mesoporous silica for the efﬁ-
cient delivery of chemotherapeutic agents to tumor cells (De La Torre et al. 2015),
the preparation of epirubicin-encapsulated polymeric nanoparticles that signiﬁcantly
increased therapeutic efﬁcacy in hepatocellular carcinoma by conjugating the
LFC131 peptide to increase the afﬁnity, recently cellulose nanoparticles with the
LFC131 peptide for targeted tumor treatment was designed (Di-Wen et al. 2016;
Chittasupho et al. 2017). A brand-new nanoplatform to successfully stimulate p53
expression in hepatocellular cancer models by targeting CXCR-4. In established
hepatocellular carcinoma models, the CXCR4-targeted p53 mRNA nanoplatform
and anti-PD-1 therapy efﬁciently triggered cellular reprogramming and immunolog-
ical components of the tumor microenvironment (Xiao et al. 2022).


10.4.4 Targeting to Endothelium

Some NPs have an impact on angiogenesis, another strategy for treating cancer,
rather than directly targeting cancer cells. Vascularization depends on the interaction
between vascular endothelial growth factor (VEGF) and VEGF receptors
(VEGFRs), with liposomes simultaneously targeting the two main VEGF receptors,
VEGFR-2 and VEGFR-3, to improve therapeutic efﬁcacy (Apte et al. 2019; Orleth
et al. 2016). Integrins are extracellular matrix protein-binding cell surface receptors
that are crucial for the invasion and motility of malignancy cells (Desgrosellier and
Cheresh 2010). The calcium-dependent route that promotes endothelial cell migra-
tion depends on the αβv3 integrin, which is signiﬁcantly expressed in tumor
neovascular endothelial cells but not in resting endothelial cells or normal cells.
the effective medication delivery into tumor cells using cationic NPs in conjunction
with ligands that target the αβv3 integrin (Nisato et al. 2003). The glycoprotein
known as Vascular Cell Adhesion Molecule-1 (VCAM-1) interacts with vascular
endothelial cells to play a role in angiogenesis. It is also found on the surface of
tumor endothelium. VCAM-1 overexpression has been seen in a number of
malignancies, suggesting that it may play a part in the active targeting of NPs for
drug delivery (Dienst et al. 2005). The extracellular matrix remodeling and tumor
neovascularization processes are carried out by the matrix metalloproteinase (MMP),
212                                                                    K. Abbasi et al.


a component of the tumor microenvironment. Breast cancer, pancreatic cancer, and
melanoma are among the tumors for which MMP-sensitive NPs have been suggested
to have possible antitumor effects (Cun et al. 2019).


10.5     Stimuli-Responsive Targeting Strategies

An acidic environment, elevated levels of GSH and ROS, and enhanced production
of certain enzymes (MMP-2/cathepsin B) are only a few of the TME’s (tumor
microenvironment) peculiar properties. As a result, tumor treatment and targeted
medication delivery are made possible by nanoparticles that incorporate TME
responsive components (Banstola et al. 2021). These endogenous cues can cause
changes in molecular function, dispersion behavior, morphology, and degradation
kinetics. This makes it easier for pharmaceutical active components to enter cells or
to escape endosome/lysosomal breakdown and be released. Exogenous stimuli-
responsive nanoparticles can also respond to endogenous stimuli like pH, ATP,
H2O2, enzyme, redox-potential, and hypoxia in addition to exogenous stimuli like
temperature, magnetic ﬁeld, light, ultrasound, and lasers. although the stimuli might
be emerged in TME or inside cancer cells (Fig. 10.3) (Tian et al. 2022).


10.5.1 Endogenous Stimuli

Also known as an intrinsic stimulus, endogenous stimuli include changes in the
internal pH levels, enzyme activity, redox activity, hypoxic conditions of the body.




Fig. 10.3 Schematic representation of exogenous and endogenous stimuli-responsive
nanoplatforms for tumor therapy (Tian et al. 2022)
10   Future Trends and Innovation in Nano Drug Delivery for Cancer Therapy,. . .   213


10.5.1.1 pH-Responsive Targeting Strategies
pH-sensitive nanoparticles have been extensively investigated to design versatile
nanoplatforms for targeted drug delivery. The TME typically has a lower extracellu-
lar pH (pHex) with a mean value of ~6.5 compared to healthy tissue. In general,
compared to healthy cells, tumor cells rapidly consume glucose for glycolysis with
rapid lactate production to obtain the energy needed to sustain their proliferation
regardless of oxygen content; consequently, the higher metabolic rate of tumor cells
was recognized as the main cause of acidic TME (Stubbs et al. 2000). The tumor cell,
lysosomes, and endosomes also have a lower pH (endosomal pH(pHen)) compared
to pHex (Lee et al. 2007; Yang et al. 2016). Therefore, pH-responsive nanoplatforms
have been developed as an effective tumor treatment tool, signiﬁcantly increasing
the accumulation of charged therapeutic agents in the tumor and facilitating cargo
release in the acidic tumor microenvironment, encapsulation of chemotherapeutics
inside pH-responsive nanoparticles is an effective approach to prolong the blood
circulation time of encapsulated agents and their retention inside the nanoparticles in
the physiological environment (Xu et al. 2022). In addition, pH-responsive
nanoparticles are also able to improve the pharmacokinetics and biodistribution of
the encapsulated payload. This is necessary to delay the metabolism and subsequent
release of drugs. The main feature of pH-triggered NPs is their transition from
hydrophobic to hydrophilic in response to acidic pH levels, which can change
their size or behavior. Reported pH-sensitive NPs, a system based on reversibly
ionizable carboxyl groups that undergo protonation and deprotonation due to pH
changes, leading to swelling, bursting, and drug release (Iversen et al. 2011).

10.5.1.2 Redox-Responsive Targeting Strategies
Redox species in tumor cells form a complex antioxidant defense system for
modulating redox homeostasis, which plays an important role in the cell’s life
cycle (Diaz-Vivancos et al. 2015). Common reactive oxygen species (ROS) include
hydroxyl radicals (OH), singlet oxygen (1O2), and hydrogen peroxide (H2O2). It
should be noted that H2O2 is a stable and non-toxic ROS, while others have a short
half-life and can be efﬁciently converted into potent toxic agents (Wu et al. 2019).
On the one hand, H2O2 can be used as a substrate for O2 production by a speciﬁc
enzyme to alleviate the hypoxia of the tumor microenvironment in some
O2-demanding therapeutic strategies (Yang et al. 2020). H2O2 can also be converted
into other highly active ROS, increased ROS can result in oxidative stress such as
lipid peroxidation (LPO) and protein and DNA damage (Dong et al. 2019). Gluta-
thione (GSH), as an antioxidant, is normally distributed in tumor cells in
concentrations up to 2–10 mM, plays a signiﬁcant role in the consumption of ROS
and the modulation of redox homeostasis (Fu et al. 2021). A high concentration of
GSH can make tumor cells resistant to various treatments. Therefore, it is advanta-
geous to develop redox-sensitive nanoparticles to deliver therapeutic agents to
trigger treatments such as chemodynamic therapy (CDT). Recently and with an
improved therapeutic proﬁle, ROS generation combined with GSH depletion can
effectively disrupt redox homeostasis to increase oxidative stress, leading to tumor
cell apoptosis (Hu and Liu 2020). To produce redox-responsive NPs, GSH cleaves
214                                                                     K. Abbasi et al.


the disulﬁde bond of NPs in cancer cells, leading to redox-controlled drug release. In
recent years, various redox-responsive drug delivery systems have been described,
including liposomes, micelles, nanogels, and prodrug-based systems. Disulﬁde
bonds are a major component in the design of redox-responsive NPs, diselenide
bonds can be used to prepare mesoporous silica-based NPs, controlled drug release
can be achieved using albumin or myoglobin by conjugating them to mesoporous
silica-based NPs through diselenide bonds, providing NPs, which is highly sensitive
to GSH and H2O2 (Gisbert-garzar 2021; Yan et al. 2021). In addition, they devel-
oped a new intrinsic redox-responsive metal–organic framework support using iron,
aluminum, or zirconium as metal nodes and 4,4-dithiobisbenzoic acid (4,4-DTBA)
as an organic ligand (Lei et al. 2021).

10.5.1.3 Enzyme-Responsive Targeting Strategies
Enzymes, being a sizeable component of the nanobiotechnology toolbox, have
tremendous biorecognition abilities in addition to outstanding catalytic houses.
Generally, bizarre enzyme expression found in cancer provides many possibilities
for designing focused nanoparticles modiﬁed with enzyme-responsive linkages.
Currently, many smart nanoparticles were organized for intracellular as well as
extracellular tumor-precise drug delivery based totally upon enzyme expression on
the goal web site. MMP-responsive nanoplatforms. Matrix metalloproteinases
(MMPs), which can be overexpressed in numerous sorts of tumors, are intently
associated with cancer pathophysiology. MMP-2 and MMP-nine mainly had been
explored for getting ready enzyme-responsive nanoplatforms (Jhaveri et al. 2014;
Eatemadi et al. 2017). The gelatin-functionalized DOX-loaded mesoporous silica
nanoparticles have been implemented for the shipping of healing sellers toward
MMP-9 overgenerated in most cancers. As expected, the organized nanoformulation
conﬁrmed genuinely superior DOX launch below simulated tumor microenviron-
ment situations and in tumor mobile models (Xu et al. 2013). The synthesized
camptothecin-encapsulated mesoporous silica nanoparticles surface functionalized
with targeting cRGD and MMP-2 responsive ﬂuorescence imaging companies,
performing as a diagnostic platform as well as for tumor location. These advanced
nanoplatforms have been capable of correctly going through enzymatic hydrolysis in
overexpressed MMP-2 environments to improve tumor remedy via the discharge of
their cargoes (Zeng et al. 2021). The superior MMP stimuli-responsive nanoplatform
encapsulated with the chemotherapeutic agent curcumin using a block copolymer
with surface-adsorbed peptides that would improve endocytosis. The prepared
nanoparticles showed a sustained curcumin launch behavior underneath physiologi-
cal conditions, while release will be elevated below conditions that mimic the tumor
microenvironment. these designed MMP-responsive nanoparticles present great
tumor speciﬁcity and therapeutic efﬁcacy in most cancers models with few side
effects (Guo et al. 2020).

10.5.1.4 Hypoxia-Responsive Targeting Strategies
Poor vascularization in cancer leads to low oxygen (hypoxia), which plays an
important role in cancer, including distant metastasis. Therefore, to treat hypoxic
10   Future Trends and Innovation in Nano Drug Delivery for Cancer Therapy,. . .    215


tumors, mainly oxygen-enhancing and hypoxia-activating drugs, etc. Many methods
are used, including so far, many types of nanoparticles have been prepared for the
delivery of hypoxic drugs, such as silica nanoparticles, liposomes, layered
nanoparticles, polymer micelles, nanovesicles, polymersomes, and albumin
nanoparticles (Liu et al. 2018; Im et al. 2019). Cancer hypoxia is often considered
an adverse indicator in cancer therapy, but certain biological agents may make it a
unique target for cancer therapy. In fact, due to the Warburg effect, tumor cells prefer
aerobic glycolysis rather than the oxidative phosphorylation pathway for energy.
Therefore, many enzymes involved in electron donation or reduction reactions, such
as azoreductase, nitroreductase, methionine synthase reductase, inducible nitrate
synthase, and DT-diaphorase (DTD), are overproduced in Oxygen tumor cells.
Therefore, great effort has been devoted to the development of hypoxia-sensitive
nanoplatforms that can be activated by enzymes for better cancer therapy. Hypoxia-
sensitive chemical linkages, including nitro groups, azo groups, quinones, and
N-oxide compounds, have also been used in the design of hypoxia-sensitive
nanoparticles. By gaining or losing electrons, they can change their conformation
and physical properties such as hydrophobicity and electron afﬁnity. As expected,
this hypoxia-sensitive nanoparticle exhibits interesting drug activity. They have
great potential in cancer therapy, including hypoxia-responsive product release,
prolonged circulation time, and improved tumor penetration and accumulation
(Wang et al. 2019a, b).

10.5.1.5 ATP-Responsive Targeting Strategies
ATP, which has been called the energy currency of the cell, ATP is fundamental to
various cellular signal cascades. ATP concentrations can reach up to 10 mM in
tumor cells, while it is only approximately 5 mM in the extracellular ﬂuid. Therefore,
a concentration gradient of ATP levels between extracellular and intracellular levels
has been used to develop ATP-responsive nanoplatforms for tumor treatment. The
nanoparticles/nanocarrier was prepared using various barrel-shaped chaperonin
groups assembled via coordination with Mg2+ into tubular structures that protected
loaded therapeutic agents from biological metabolism and degradation (Biswas et al.
2013). Upon internalization by tumor cells, hydrolysis of ATP to form ADP can
trigger protein conformational changes and collapse of the nanoparticles, resulting in
the selective release of the contents. The prepared nanoparticles using a gradient of
ATP concentrations showed rapid cargo release in an ATP-responsive manner. An
enhanced anti-proliferative effect, possibly due to enhanced cell apoptosis in
mitochondria-mediated pathways and cell cycle arrest at the G2 phase (Zhang
et al. 2017a, b). In another example, designed ATP-binding aptamer DNA
functionalized with polymeric nanocarriers encapsulated with chemotherapeutics
for targeted delivery to ATP overgenerated environments (Mo et al. 2014). ATP
can be considered an efﬁcient stimulus to promote release of preloaded drugs from
nanoparticles for speciﬁc cancer treatments and for diagnostic purposes.
216                                                                       K. Abbasi et al.


10.5.1.6 Tumor-Metabolite Responsive Targeting Strategies
Metabolic reprogramming in tumor cells alters metabolic processes to meet unmet
nutrient and energy needs. In this case, tumor-associated metabolites are often
valuable for timely prediction of tumor progression and progression. Malnutrition
affects cancer cells, this lactate (an additional metabolite) lowers the pH of the tumor
microenvironment. Oxalate functionalized NPs can activate lactate dehydrogenase
(LDH), which forms OxNP-protein complexes. This study validated the idea of
changing the sensitivity of nanoparticles by conjugating them with important
proteins to target speciﬁc metabolites in cancer cells (Fruehauf et al. 2019).


10.5.2 Exogenous Stimuli

When trigger stimuli are caused by external factors, such as a change in temperature,
ultrasound, or light responsiveness, they are known as exogenous stimuli.

10.5.2.1 Temperature Stimuli-Responsive Targeting Strategies
Heat-sensitive nanoparticles use temperature changes as a trigger to release their
charge because they can change their hydrophilic and hydrophobic balance, solubil-
ity or structural properties in response to temperature and cold. These nanoparticles
have a low critical solution temperature (LCST), below which the mixture can
become miscible (Pasparakis and Tsitsilianis 2020) The best thermal materials
with low solubility include poly(2-oxazoline)s (POxs), poly-N-isopropylacrylamide
(PNIPAAm), poly (methyl vinyl ether) (PMVE), and poly(vinylcaprolactam)
(PNVCL) (Tian et al. 2022). It easily enters the phase transition from solid to liquid
depending on external conditions. Among these materials, PNIPAAm is mainly used
to prepare nanoparticles due to its low temperature of about 30 °C, disulﬁram to
cancer cells as a long-term cancer treatment, and the drug-loaded hydrogel has been
found to allow cellular uptake.
    Graphene oxide (GO) has a high concentration of functional groups on the
surface, allows the transfer of bioactive molecules and is highly reactive in aqueous
conditions; However, their application is limited by incorrect dosing and lack of
control. To overcome these limitations, GO has been modiﬁed using biodegradable
and hydrophilic polymers such as poly(N vinylcaprolactam) (PNVCL) and poly
(glycolic acid), which confer pH and temperature sensitivity to allow the loading and
release of oxaliplatin in MCF-7 cells (Kazempour et al. 2019). Similarly, smart
temperature and pH sensitive nanoparticles were fabricated from poly
(N-isopropylacrylamide-acrylamide) random copolymer. The presence of a hydro-
philic group in acrylamide raises the LCST to 37 °C (closer to body temperature),
eventually making this sample suitable for drug delivery (Farjadian et al. 2020). This
structure is further modiﬁed with hydrazine and binds to DOX via a Schiff base bond
(an acid-ﬁssile bond), making the nanoparticles heat and pH sensitive. Considering
that hyperthermia raises the temperature of human tissue to 113 °C and increases the
10   Future Trends and Innovation in Nano Drug Delivery for Cancer Therapy,. . .   217


sensitivity of cancer cells to chemotherapy, these two effects are combined because
thermochemotherapy can improve cancer treatment.

10.5.2.2 Magnetic Stimuli-Responsive Targeting Strategies
Magnetic nanoparticles have good physical, chemical, and biological effects and
have been recognized as an ideal platform for many cancer treatments and diagnoses
(Jiao et al. 2022). Compared with other magnetic stimulus sensitive nanoplatforms,
the encapsulated superparamagnetic Fe2O3 nanoparticles (SPION) have good mag-
netic moments and satisfactory biocompatibility. In addition, magnetic molybdenum
disulﬁde (mMoS2) can be modiﬁed by liposomes with phospholipid bilayer mem-
brane structure to form a magnetic nanoplatform that is not easy to collect in
physiological solutions and has good biocompatibility, so it shows great promise
in large-scale application. nanomedicine. Sensitive nanoparticles involve
encapsulating/immobilizing therapeutic agents into magnetically responsive
nanoparticles, injecting magnetically responsive nanoparticles into the body, and
using an external magnetic ﬁeld to locate and activate the magnetically responsive
nanoparticles of interest (Li et al. 2022a, b). Recent article reported, GSH-responsive
metal-organic framework (MOF) that can be loaded with an indoleamine
2,3-dioxygenase (IDO) inhibitor and free s-nitrosothiol donor group to improve
antitumor immunotherapy (Du et al. 2022). In this nanoplatform, high relaxation
Time (T1) provides magnetic resonance (MR) imaging capability to control the
in vivo biodistribution of nanomedicines. Multifunctional nanodiagnostics based on
DOX-encapsulated tannic acid-Fe network (TAF) coupled with ﬁbronectin function
for combinatorial cancer therapy guided by MR imaging. Developing nanoparticles
sensitive to magnetic stimulus by imaging will help determine when a tumor is
growing (Xu et al. 2022).

10.5.2.3 Light Stimuli-Responsive Targeting Strategies
Various nanoparticles can respond to light and have the ability to adjust the irradia-
tion wavelength, power, and domain, and photosensitive nanoparticles have recently
been developed for controlled drug release. Nanoparticles can be sensitive to any
type of light, including ultraviolet, visible or near infrared, which can affect
biological diseases such as cancer or tumors. By controlling various irradiation,
photosensitive tumor treatment can be done precisely to reduce the negative effects
of the body (Yan and Li 2016). Polymer complexes, polymer micelles, liposomes,
nanogels, and nanorods have all been developed as photosensitive NCs using
materials such as gold nanocomposites, carbon nanotubes, graphene, and organic
molecules. Nanoparticles can change shape in response to light by changing the
shape of the molecules. For instance, photo-switching nanoparticles that absorb
UV-visible light have been shown to be able to transport drugs for cancer treatment,
including DOX, docetaxel, and paclitaxel (Tong et al. 2013). The short wavelength
of UV-vis light limits the use of nanoparticles. Therefore, near-infrared photosensi-
tive nanoparticles would be suitable for controlled drug delivery (Timko et al. 2014).
218                                                                      K. Abbasi et al.


10.5.2.4 Ultrasound Stimuli-Responsive Targeting Strategies
Ultrasound has emerged as an external stimulus that can stimulate the destruction of
nanoparticles and release their respective charge. The solution is released by the heat
generated by the cavitation. Ultrasound sensitive nanocarriers/particles can be safely
and cost-effectively used for cancer diagnosis and are widely used in hospitals.
Gases and perﬂuoropentans combine nanoparticles to form gases (e.g., CO2)
presents cancer in a special place in the biological atmosphere (Wang et al. 2015).
In addition, the ultrasound device can be used to facilitate siRNA or DNA delivery in
the tumor (Carson et al. 2012). However, the large size of ultrasound-sensitive
nanoparticles limits their penetration into tissues due to their poor penetration. In
general, drug-loaded ultrasound-sensitive nanoparticles can be used for cancer
therapy and theranostics. Recently, cisplatin-encapsulated solid lipid nanoparticles
have been prepared under ultrasound guidance.
   In ultrasound therapy, engineered nanoparticles exhibit satisfactory drug release,
cellular uptake, and other pharmacokinetic properties as well as excellent antitumor
effect in glioma models. Another way to prepare DOX-loaded mesoporous silica
nanoparticles sensitive to ultrasound stimuli characterized by ultrasound release of
cargo for glioma treatment (Wu et al. 2018a, b). In a mouse model of glioma, they
found that tumor growth and development was signiﬁcantly inhibited, as well as
improved survival. Using HIFU, nanoparticles can be fragmented, allowing drug
delivery in dogs with idiopathic chondrosarcoma, osteosarcoma, hepatocellular
carcinoma, and prostate cancer (Horise et al. 2019). Biocompatible piezoelectric
nanoparticles were also encapsulated with DSPEG and modiﬁed with anti-HER2 Ab
to target cancer therapy. As expected, nanoplatforms sensitive to engineered ultra-
sound stimuli could release effective drugs in a controlled manner, interfere with cell
division, and inhibit tumors.


10.6     Stimuli-Responsive Gene Delivery Nanocarriers for Cancer
         Therapy

Stimulus-sensitive gene carrier nanocarriers (GDNCs) have great potential in gene
therapy due to their effective protection, prolonged blood circulation, selective
delivery targets, and controlled nucleic acid release. Demonstrate nanocarriers that
respond to endogenous stimuli such as pH, reactive oxygen species, glutathione, and
enzymes, as well as exogenous stimuli such as light, heat, ultrasound, and magnetic
ﬁelds, and demonstrate the biomedical potential of stimulus-response gene delivery.
for the treatment of cancer. Over the last few years, more than 4600 gene therapy
trials have been completed worldwide, of which 29% have entered phase II/III/IV
clinical trials for cancer treatment (Zhang et al. 2023).
    Major nucleic acid drugs used to treat cancer include small interfering RNA
(siRNA), antisense oligonucleotides, microRNA, messenger RNA, short hairpin
RNA, and gene therapy (such as the CRISPR/Cas9 system) (Wu et al. 2022;
Lopez-Cantu et al. 2022; Zhang et al. 2021). Each nucleic acid type can act on its
speciﬁc target cell at the genetic level. Due to non-biodegradation, lack of cellular
10   Future Trends and Innovation in Nano Drug Delivery for Cancer Therapy,. . .       219




Fig. 10.4 Schematic diagram of stimuli-responsive nanocarriers for gene delivery and cancer
therapy (Zhang et al. 2023)

uptake, rapid clearance, and easy degradation by enzymes, bare nucleic acid drugs
cannot achieve the desired results with direct administration.
   Low toxicity, low immunogenicity, high packaging capacity, nanoparticle
formulations not limited by the type of gene load, and non-viral vectors (such as
cationic polymers, cationic liposomes, inorganic or organic nanoparticles) have
become research topics. In recent years, stimuli-sensitive GDNCs, which can effec-
tively deliver nucleic acid to the target and respond to speciﬁc stimuli, thereby
increasing the effectiveness of gene therapy, have attracted many attentions.
(Mohammadinejad et al. 2020; Liu et al. 2021a, b). These GDNCs often contain
nanocarriers that can be activated by endogenous stimuli (pH, reactive oxygen
species (ROS), glutathione (GSH), enzymes, etc.) and exogenous stimuli (light,
heat, ultrasound (US), magnetic ﬁeld (MF), etc.) for gene delivery in cancer treat-
ment (Fig. 10.4).
