10.7     Application of siRNA (Nanoparticle-Based RNA) Cancer
         Therapy

10.7.1 Introduction

Small interfering RNA (siRNA) is a double-stranded non-coding RNA of 20–25
nucleotides in base pair length. siRNA inhibits the production of speciﬁc genes by
binding after being transmitted to the cytoplasm by transcription. siRNAs bind to
speciﬁc regions of genes with greater speciﬁcity. siRNAs silence disease-speciﬁc
gene expression through the RNAi process. bare siRNAs cannot be delivered due to
uncertainty about their stability (Elbashir et al. 2001).
    The RNA integrates itself into the RNA-induced silencing complex (RISC),
directing Argonaute-2 to extract sequence-speciﬁc target mRNAs, thereby blocking
translation of the target mRNA and silencing the target gene. siRNA gene silencing
is more efﬁcient and selective for more targets than antibody oligonucleotides.
Although some ﬁndings have raised concerns about the ability of siRNAs to
stimulate the immune system and subsequent interferon responses through interac-
tion with Toll-like 3, siRNA therapy has many advantages such as ease of synthesis,
low cost of production (compared to protein or antibodies), practical goals, high
speciﬁcity and characteristics of good medicine. Designing and producing siRNA
drugs is not easy. The challenge is to get a good delivery (Draz et al. 2014).
    Many problems prevent siRNAs from binding to the right place, such as the ease
of degradation by blood nucleases and rapid removal by the immune system, the
difﬁculty of targeting the drug, the lack of cellular uptake, and the lack of escape
from endosomes. Delivery efﬁciency is considered the most important issue of
RNAi technology and clinical application. Nanoparticles have better speciﬁcity
than other carriers and can affect the efﬁcient delivery of siRNA in the bloodstream.
In 2014, Atu027, a lipid-based RNAi therapy targeting protein kinase N3 (PKN3)
mRNA in the vascular endothelium for the treatment of pancreatic ductal adenocar-
cinoma (PDAC), achieved promising results in the dose escalation phase. More than
90% of Atu027 adverse events are grade 1 or 2, indicating a high level of safety
(Schultheis et al. 2020).
    In addition, another lipid-based siRNA nanoparticle (called DCR-MYC) has been
developed to suppress MYC, an oncoprotein that is dysregulated in most
malignancies. In a phase I dose escalation trial, ﬁve doses of DCRMYC therapy
were given to patients with multiple types of cancer, including neuroendocrine
tumors (NET), metastatic breast cancer (MBC), colorectal cancer (CRC), or other
tumors. After treatment, tumor shrinkage was observed in many patients (Tolcher
et al. 2015). All these results suggest that the nanoparticle-based RNAi approach is a
promising method for cancer therapy. In Table 10.3 details of the RNAi-based
cancer therapies in clinical trials (Dolly et al. 2023).
    RNAi starts with mature siRNA in the cytoplasmic perinuclear region. Exoge-
nous double-stranded RNA (dsRNA) is usually split into 21–24 nucleotides by an
endoribonuclease or an RNase motif helicase, Dicer, and is referred to as mature
siRNA. The guide helix then binds to RISC to restore its gene silencing activity,

while the non-guiding helix of the old siRNA is degraded by Argonaute 2 (Ago2).
Next, the guide strand complexed with RISC searches for target mRNA based on the
Watson-Crick base in the assembly. Once localized, the Ago2 protein in RISC
cleaves the target mRNA for degradation, at which point the target’s ability to
express the protein is inhibited. Unlike antisense oligonucleotides, RISC can support
multiple replications of target mRNAs, allowing siRNAs to exert their silencing
effects more effectively (Fig. 10.5) (Hattab et al. 2021).


10.7.2 Difficulties in siRNA Delivery

10.7.2.1 Physiological Barriers
When siRNA is administered and injected into the bloodstream, many structural and
anatomical barriers block the pathway of siRNA to its domain of action. The main
stage of coordination is elimination by the reticuloendothelial system (RES) in the
kidneys and other organs (e.g., bones, liver, lungs, and spleen). Particles larger than
100 nm are captured by it. Therefore, the recommended RNA molecule diameter
should be between 20 and 100 nm for renal passage and elimination of RES (Wang
et al. 2010). In addition, therapeutics of this size exhibit enhanced passive uptake of
tumor cells from blood vessels with a pore size of 100–800 nm. Penetration of
siRNAs from target tissue after crossing the RES is inhibited by local barriers in the
224                                                                                 K. Abbasi et al.




Fig. 10.6 Physiological barriers to in vivo delivery of siRNA-nanoparticles. Extracellular barriers
include hepatic and renal clearance, which are dependent on the particle sizes. Opsonization and
macrophage engulfment via the mononuclear phagocytic system (MPS) further reduce the bioavail-
ability of siRNA nanoparticles in the plasma. Extracellular matrix (ECM) trapping further decreases
the localization of siRNA complexes into the tumor tissues. Intracellular barriers involve effective
endosomal escape and siRNA release into the cytosol to initiate RNAi-associated gene knockdown
activity (Hattab et al. 2021)


extracellular matrix and endothelial cell layer. The pore size of endothelial tissue is
generally 4.5–25 nm, so the largest pore size can pass through particles of 4–5
nm. Only naked siRNA molecules or those associated with small molecule ligands
are easily passed through endothelial cells (Gupta et al. 2019). In addition, naked or
complex siRNAs must cross the dense extracellular space to reach the target cell
surface (Fig. 10.6) (Hattab et al. 2021).

10.7.2.2 Cell Membrane
Negatively charged phospholipid bilayers containing functional proteins form the
cell membrane. Because naked siRNA is polyanionic and water-soluble, it cannot
enter cells passively through hydrophobic cell membranes. On the other hand,
transmembrane transport via endocytosis overcomes this limitation (Shukla et al.
2016). Although siRNA must cross the endosomal membrane and enter the cyto-
plasm, if not, it is degraded by ribonucleases or lost by exocytosis. Pinocytosis and
phagocytosis are two types of endocytosis. The former is characterized by
endosomes facing the surface of the cell membrane, thereby suppressing the endo-
some and pulling it into the cytoplasm. The membrane of the latter spreads outward,
absorbs the particle, and attaches to the lysosome. To improve cell recruitment,
10   Future Trends and Innovation in Nano Drug Delivery for Cancer Therapy,. . .   225


endocytosis is promoted by receptor-mediated endocytosis using cell peptides and/or
ligands. Anionic proteoglycans on the cell surface allow them to interact with the
cation-carrying siRNA complex/transporter and enter the cell via endocytosis.

10.7.2.3 Stability
The in vivo stability of siRNA is an important issue. Transfected siRNAs are readily
hydrolyzed in the extracellular environment due to diverse enzyme activities in
blood and target organs. It is known that the half-life of naked siRNA in blood
ranges from a few minutes to an hour. The backbone of the siRNA molecule consists
of ribose sugar. Studies have shown that modiﬁcation of ribosomal loops and
nucleobases improves siRNA stability and reduces immune response and resistance.
RES contains phagocytes that degrade siRNA. Uptake of polyethylene glycol (PEG)
in the nanocomplex reduces interaction with phagocytes, thereby reducing phagocy-
tosis (Tokatlian and Segura 2010). Serum nuclease targets the hydroxyl group at the
2′ position of the pentose ring. These nucleases destroy RNA by disrupting the
phosphodiester structure of nucleic acids. Therefore, the delivery of negative siRNA
into the blood and maintenance of the therapeutic effect is difﬁcult (Czech et al.
2011). The siRNA must remain in the blood to achieve the desired target in a
particular tissue, allowing for proper endocytosis and desired RNAi activity. If
unmodiﬁed siRNA has high intracellular stability, nuclease stability may not be a
limiting factor when siRNA enters the cell. One study found that while nuclease
stability is important to prevent degradation of siRNAs in the extracellular environ-
ment (such as blood vessels after systemic administration), sexual dominance of
cells does not control the persistence of siRNAs internalized into the cytoplasmic
compartment.

10.7.2.4 Off-Target Effects
Off-target effects can be divided into three categories: (1) In the cell, the RNAi
process becomes saturated, resulting in upregulation of miRNA-regulated genes. To
solve this problem, siRNA can be mutated or a stronger siRNA can be used.
(2) Passenger silencing leads to the reduction of target genes that are partially or
fully homologous to the passenger sequence. To solve this problem, blunt end,
asi-RNA, si-siRNA, phosphorylated double target blocking siRNA, ss-siRNA or
aiRNA at the 5′ end of the passenger strand were used. (3) Seed region-induced
silencing leads to some downregulation of complementary genes (Naik et al. 2021).
This can be avoided by using seeds enriched with AU and the addition of seeds can
be avoided.


10.7.3 Applications of Nanocarriers for siRNA Delivery

size, quality, ability to protect siRNA from degradation, and biological behavior,
various NPs are currently being investigated for siRNA delivery in various
biological systems, cancer therapy (Table 10.4) (Dolly et al. 2023). Treatment of

naked siRNA in the host presents several challenges. siRNAs do not reach the target
at best because of the short half-life, rapid renal clearance, and rapid blood loss.
siRNA can be encapsulated in NPs or attached to the surface by covalent or
non-covalent bonds.
   Nanoparticles are designed and used as drug carriers because they provide
important beneﬁts in biotechnology and enable the spread of nanomedicine. Due
to the addition of anionic charge, free siRNA alone cannot infect cells and is
degraded in biological ﬂuids. The ideal tool should protect siRNA from RNase
degradation, increase cellular uptake, facilitate gene silencing, and be non-toxic.
Polycationic formulations that have been used to deliver siRNA include PEI,
polyarginine, polylysine, histoylated polylysine peptides, chitosan, and polyamide
dendrimers (Zhang et al. 2022a, b) (Table 10.4). Conjugation of nucleic acids to
nanoparticles has many beneﬁts, including preventing cellular damage, increasing
circulation time, improving cell membrane permeability, and avoiding endosomal
digestion. NPs can easily bind to various targets such as RGD peptide or transferrin
for target-speciﬁc siRNA delivery. Features of NPs for siRNA-level delivery
include: (1) better stability and quieter activity (2) improved pharmacokinetics,
(3) targeting of EPR effect, and (4) more than half of siRNA in long serum. Most
biodegradable formulations that release siRNA use polymeric materials that incor-
porate the siRNA into a polymeric core.


10.7.4 Nanocarriers for Co-delivery of siRNA with Anticancer Drug

The use of single drugs in cancer treatment is not very successful, so there is
increasing interest as the combination of anticancer drugs and siRNA can be
beneﬁcial in this way. Apoptosis has been deﬁned as an aberrant immune-mediated
cell death that can occur by two distinct but interconnected processes involving the
proteolysis of many cellular proteins by caspases. Mitochondrial or intrinsic path-
way (direct DNA damage, hypoxia, depletion of oncogenes and survival factors) and
death receptor or extrinsic pathway (extracellular). p53 is an important activator of
the intrinsic pathway. When combined with anti-inﬂammatory drugs, siRNAs
should reduce anti-inﬂammatory drugs, reduce anti-inﬂammatory drugs, and pro-
mote apoptotic pathways in cells (Khelghati et al. 2021). After siRNA release, the
concentration of anticancer drugs in cancer cells will increase, thereby increasing the
228                                                                       K. Abbasi et al.


anticancer effect. Multidrug resistance develops during long-term therapy and
describes environmental resistance to drugs that do not interact with different drug
models. The negative apoptotic pathway and drug proliferation are important
mechanisms for the progression of multidrug resistance (MDR) (Charbe et al.
2020; Zhu et al. 2021). In studies to improve doxorubicin (DOX) sensitivity,
siRNA can downregulate pathways such as STAT3, Notch1, Mcl-1 and Nrf2, and
siRNA can be used to inhibit the activity of P-gp to act as a cell membrane drug
transporter. results in increased DOX accumulation. Both DOX and siRNA were
incorporated into NPs, which promoted accumulation in cancer cells while
protecting siRNA from enzymatic degradation (Paskeh et al. 2022). Others have
developed cationic liposomes (CTL) for the delivery of paclitaxel (PTX) and
crizotinib (CRI) with siRNA (CRI-PTX-CTL-siRNA). CRI-PTX-CTL-siRNA was
shown to increase cellular uptake in MCF-7 cells and strongly suppress Bcl-xL
expression in cells (Li et al. 2022a, b). Many studies have been conducted to increase
the efﬁcacy of chemotherapy by blocking or avoiding drug efﬂux mechanisms
through the combination of antibodies and siRNA. The reduction of proteins by
siRNAs involved in the drug efﬂux increases the immune response in the tumor,
resulting in a stronger immune response.


10.7.5 Clinical Applications of siRNA-Based Nanotherapies

Ten siRNA treatments have been studied in the clinic to date (Table 10.3). Phase I
trial of all siRNA anticancer nanotherapeutics begins: The Phase I trial of CALAA-
01 and DCR-MYC in patients with solid tumors, multiple myeloma, and myeloma
has been terminated due to dose limitations, toxicities, and decision support.
ALNVSP02, Atu027, TKM-PLK1 and siG12D LODER completed the ﬁrst stage.
A phase I trial of siRNA-EphA2-DOPC has begun collecting samples but is not yet
complete, a trial of mesenchymal stem cell-derived exosomes using KRAS G12D
has yet to be discovered. Four siRNAs entered nanotherapeutic phase II; Atu027 and
TKM-PLK1 complete Phase II, while siG12D LODER is currently recruiting. A
phase 2 trial of DCR-MYC was performed in patients with hepatocellular carcinoma,
but the promoter was subsequently truncated. As of May 2021, there are no siRNA
anticancer nanotherapeutics complete Phase III trials (Hattab et al. 2021). Currently,
ﬁve siRNA drugs have been approved (patisiran, givosiran, inclisiran, lumasiran,
vutrisiran) and several others are in late stages of phase III clinical trials. Notably,
there is a clear focus on hepatocyte delivery, with GalNAc being the most popular
platform for siRNA bioconjugation and delivery, GalNAc bioconjugates are com-
paratively straightforward to synthesize and administer they show a good biocom-
patibility/toxicity proﬁle, and very high efﬁcacy. Among the approved siRNA drugs,
nanoparticles (LNPs) are only used in the case of patisiran (Friedrich and Aigner
2022) (For more information, visit the Cortellis Drug Discovery Intelligence Data-
base: https://www.cortellis.com/drugdiscovery/). There are 630 patents for these
four drugs. All clinical trials at various stages are the ﬁrst of their kind in open-
label human trials. These trials recruited participants with various types of cancer
10   Future Trends and Innovation in Nano Drug Delivery for Cancer Therapy,. . .   229


that were advanced and/or metastatic and did not respond to standard of care (SOC)
chemotherapy.


10.7.6 Current Challenges and Opportunities

The development of nanoformulated siRNA-based cancer therapy is one of the most
important and potentially revolutionary research areas in biomedicine. However,
there are many problems that need to be resolved before these treatments can be used
effectively in the clinic. siRNA molecules are small and poorly structured, allowing
them to be easily degraded and removed by the immune system. One of the biggest
problems is the lack of siRNA delivery systems that can reach and enter the target
site. Currently, most siRNA delivery systems are based on cationic lipids or
polymers that do not cross cell membranes well. Another challenge is to create
siRNA molecules that are resistant to degradation by nucleases, as well as other
molecules that can affect the activity of siRNA molecules.
    In addition, methods are needed to monitor the in vivo delivery, uptake, and
activity of siRNA molecules. Finally, creating siRNA molecules that target cancer
cells and developing ways to mitigate the effects of the target is an ongoing
challenge. These challenges need to be addressed for nanoformulation-based
siRNA therapeutics to be effective for cancer therapy.


10.7.7 Design Concerns for Future Development of RNAi-Mediated
       Anticancer Nanotherapeutics

The development of new nanoparticle delivery systems and the optimization of
siRNA formulations are important not only to improve cellular uptake of anticancer
nanotherapeutics, but also to improve their speciﬁcity and enable therapy. Several
problems with the development of new models for human trials should be consid-
ered: (1) Production of large nanoparticle particles (compared to small particles in
animal research) can affect the behavior and activity, aggregation, agglomeration,
off-target problem of nanoparticle and prevention; therefore safety limits should be
taken into account and the production of nanoparticles should be continuously
improved. (2) The diameter of nanoparticles was optimized and narrowed in the
range of 20–30 nm; many things like this show better reproduction and recreation in
humans. Simultaneous delivery of two or more siRNAs at delivery can improve
antitumor effect without increasing toxicity and off-target increase. (4) Replace
standard nanoparticles with biodegradable nanoplatforms as they have stable
proﬁles. (5) Nanoparticles (but not siRNA) can be functionalized with target ligands
(Hattab et al. 2021). While targeting may not improve tumor cell recruitment,
targeting ligands may address other biological concerns, including intracellular
trafﬁcking and intracellular siRNA escape. Identiﬁcation of novel nanoformulations
and delivery as well as speciﬁc oncogenes for siRNA targeting could lead to
effective treatment of these therapies (Cuellar et al. 2015).

10.8     Ultrasound Linked Nano-cancer Therapeutics

10.8.1 Introduction

Ultrasound is often used in cancer treatment, such as using ultrasound-guided biopsy
to collect tumors from the right place, diagnosing tumors with cheaper and easier
equipment such as MRI and CT, or removing tumors using high intensity. focused
ultrasound (HIFU) preparation. The efﬁcacy of this technique can be further
improved by combining it with various nanosystems to achieve: (1) better resolution
in ultrasound examinations, e.g., allowing visualization of angiogenic vessels,
(2) speciﬁc anti-inﬂammatory drugs. Drugs or gases that bind or encapsulate into
nanosystems and are released into the tumor under a control as in ultrasound
application, (3) cancer in the tumor uses less heat than HIFU (Alphandéry 2022).
Also, some nanosystems show tunable dimensions, i.e., to nanobubbles can turn into
microbubbles. This dual dimension is good because it leads to target aggregation of
nanobubbles through the EPR effect and the positive effect of ultrasound on
microbubbles in the same cell. Interestingly, the way the nanosystems protect the
tumor in principle can be adjusted by the correct selection of a wide range of
nanosystems and by adjusting the importance of ultrasound parameters, some of
which will come true, such as special effects, as they are all products. Electromag-
netic pollution is observed as cavitation is usually absent, but may cause tumor
damage (Fig. 10.7) (Alphandéry 2022). The combination of the nanometer system
with ultrasound is of particular interest. In fact, it combines a number of advantages.
First, by treating tumor-speciﬁc nanosystems through force, magnetic, or active use,
it could enable the treatment of cancer in a speciﬁc region of the tumor. Second, it
can also increase the sensitivity of cancer diagnosis by increasing the resolution of
ultrasound, such as ultrasound. The nano/microbubbles can help visualize the blood
vessels perfusing the tumor or provide a standard US assessment by other methods
such as PA, MRI or CT (Zhang et al. 2017a, b). In some cases, ultrasound can help
complete this plan by penetrating certain substances, such as the brain or skin, that
the nanosystem needs to diffuse before reaching the tumor (Tran et al. 2020). They
can also be excited to activate nanosystems in tumors in different ways, such as
cellular internalization of nanosystems in tumor cells or thermal or immunological or
controlled drug release (Timin et al. 2017; Canavese et al. 2018; Han et al. 2019).


10.8.2 Ultrasound Parameters Used for Cancer Therapy

Ultrasound is a mechanical wave that periodically oscillates at a frequency f greater
than the sound frequency, such as f > 20 kHz. These waves are usually produced by
sensors that convert electrical signals into transducers. Two types of ultrasound can
be distinguished from High Intensity Focused Ultrasound (HIFU), which operates at
high intensity i ~ 0.1–10 kW/cm2, using low-intensity ultrasound (LIU), which is
usually less powerful than HIFU and produces low-intensity ultrasound, for exam-
ple, i < 1–5 W/cm2 (Elhelf et al. 2018). On the other hand, HIFU can speciﬁcally

While HIFU is good at treating small tumors, it faces some problems in treating large
or vascularized tumors. For safety reasons, the intensity of ultrasound should be kept
below 0.05–0.50 W/cm2 to prevent tissue heating. Ultrasonic frequency f can be

Bhargava 2013), medium frequency 0.7–3.0 MHz, used in medicine (Mason 2011)
and low frequency 20–200 kHz (Schoellhammer and Traverso 2016) improve drug
delivery very deep depths. In order to achieve the desired results, ultrasound
frequency and intensity should be adjusted in cancer treatment.


10.8.3 Ultrasound Interactions with Nanoparticles

The effect of ultrasound associated with nanoparticles and cells facilitates the
distribution of drugs in cancer and increases the effectiveness of treatment. In
addition, nanoparticle ultrasonic drug delivery could overcome the limitations of
current cancer therapy. Because ultrasound can interact with nanoparticles, it is
possible to design drug-bearing nanoparticles that disintegrate in response to ultra-
sound and release the therapeutic payload. Therefore, ultrasound can be used to
make nanoparticles release drugs on demand, called ultrasound-mediated drug
release. In addition to hitting nanoparticles, causing drug release, ultrasound can
be used to change the body's pressure and also increase noise, cavitation, and local
hyperthermia (Tharkar et al. 2019). Ultrasound can improve clinical outcomes in
several ways, particularly:

• Triggering the release of the drug from nanoparticles,
• Promoting uptake and accumulation of nanoparticle in cells,
• Enhancing the penetration of nanoparticles in tumors.

Ultrasound exposure of nanosystems can lead to the generation of ROS and other

(NS) considered for the treatment of cancer in the presence of a backbone of

nanodroplets, i.e., hollow nanospheres containing oil or liquid, with a polymerized
layer. (2) Silica NPs with different porosity or mesoporosity levels, (3) such as
PMMA, PLGA or lipids or phospholipids to stabilize inorganic NPs in the form of
nanocrystalline or porous structures such as gold NPs, iron oxide NPs, TiO2
substances natural NPs such as NPs, Au-TiO2 NPs, MnOx NPs, bismuth NPs,
ZnO NPs, those containing the heme pigment biliverdin or albumin, (4) exosomes,
membrane vesicles, bacterial protein certain organisms such as vesicles,
polymersomes, pith—assembly of peptides isolated from their original biological

2022). In general, functional content is added by integrating into the outer or inner

It inhibits the tumor with the effect of EPR (Alphandery 2020).


10.9     Exosomes as Anticancer Drug Delivery Vehicles

10.9.1 Introduction

Exosomes are small bilayers between 50 and 150 nm in diameter, secreted by almost
all cells. They also play an important role in physiological or pathological processes
such as regulation of immunity, development of metabolic and neurodegenerative
diseases, growth and development of animals. Exosomes also play a role in tumor
growth and development by modulating extracellular factors in the tumor microen-
vironment, metastasis, and antitumor or antitumor effects. Exosomes are found in all
body ﬂuids, including cerebrospinal ﬂuid, blood, saliva, urine, amniotic ﬂuid,
ascites, and bronchoalveolar lavage ﬂuid (Kalluri and LeBleu 2020). Exosomes
carry a variety of cargo, including proteins, lipids, various RNAs, and DNA (Isaac
10   Future Trends and Innovation in Nano Drug Delivery for Cancer Therapy,. . .    235


et al. 2021). Because of the diversity and amount of burden in different diseases,
exosomes are widely used in the diagnosis and monitoring of diseases. Exosomes
with organelles, according to their biological functions and properties, are a versatile
drug for the treatment of cancer and other diseases. For example, curcumin (cur),
dopamine, paclitaxel (PTX and doxorubicin), and other compounds that are stable in
exosomes (Liao et al. 2019). As natural nanoparticle biocarriers, exosomes are
stable, membrane permeable, cell speciﬁc, and can even cross the blood-brain barrier
(BBB) (Ha et al. 2016). Compared with other biological products, exosome-based
therapeutic delivery has better efﬁcacy and fewer side effects. However, some
disadvantages of drug delivery vehicles are low efﬁcacy, strong heterogeneity,
short half-life, and low exosome loading efﬁciency, which limit their clinical
application. The development of new exosome preparation and drug delivery has
spurred the use of exosomes in cancer therapy.


10.9.2 Biogenesis and Uptake of Exosomes

Exosomes originate from endosomal structures and form early sorting endosomes
(ESEs) via plasma membrane invaginations. ESEs mature into late-separating
endosomes, exchange cargo with the Golgi apparatus, and bud inside to form
multivesicular bodies (MVBs) that encapsulate intraluminal vesicles (ILVs)
containing exosomal contents at this stage. MVBs can fuse with lysosomes or
autophagosomes to degrade or integrate with the plasma membrane to release
ILVs as exosomes. It contains many bioactive molecules, including exosomes,
proteins and nucleic acids. Tetraspanins CD9, CD63, and CD81 on the exosome
membrane are often used as exosome marker proteins (Yubo et al. 2022). Cells
recruit exosomes via caveolin-dependent endocytosis, clathrin-dependent endocyto-
sis, macropinocytosis, lipid rafts, phagocytosis, and direct fusion. In addition,
exosomes can enter cells in more than one way.


10.9.3 The Sources of Exosomes

Exosomes are found in all ﬂuids of the human body. However, the results of
exosomes isolated from body ﬂuids or culture medium are insufﬁcient to meet
clinical needs. Quality control is also a problem that limits its clinical application.
Exosomes from non-human sources such as bacteria, milk and plants are considered
a promising tool for cancer therapy. Due to their low toxicity and easy availability,
exosomes can also be used as a personal oral delivery vehicle. Milk-derived
exosomes are also considered a good delivery vehicle for hydrophilic and lipophilic
drugs, as well as chemotherapeutic drugs, and show no toxicity and immunogenicity
in mice.
236                                                                     K. Abbasi et al.


10.9.4 Isolation of Exosomes

For effective and safe drug delivery, exosomes must be isolated using efﬁcient and
reproducible isolation techniques. Exosome isolation technologies have been devel-
oped based on speed, size, and antibody properties (Sidhom et al. 2020). The purity,
amount and physicochemical properties of exosomes vary depending on the separa-
tion method, and the following methods are generally used for separation.

10.9.4.1 Ultracentrifugation
Ultracentrifugation is the most popular and widely accepted gold standard for
isolating exosomes. It includes differential ultracentrifugation and density gradient
ultracentrifugation. Differential ultracentrifugation separates exosomes from other
products by using different centrifugal force and duration during centrifugation,
mainly because different molecules have different particle sizes.

10.9.4.2 Ultrafiltration
Ultraﬁltration uses membranes with different pore sizes to separate particles into
smaller sizes. Ultraﬁltration alone or in combination with ultracentrifugation and gel
ﬁltration chromatography effectively separates exosomes.

10.9.4.3 Size Exclusion Chromatography (SEC)
SEC can be used to separate exosomes depending on the pore size that separates
macromolecules and particles by size. Components with small hydrodynamic radii
enter these pores, resulting in a longer retention time, while exosomes and other
products with large hydrodynamic radii are removed from the pores, resulting in a
shorter retention time.

10.9.4.4 Flow Field-Flow Fractionation (F4)
F4 is a hydrodynamic process that separates exosomes from different diameters.
Fractionation takes place in a rectangle in which the migrating ﬂow moves along the
axis of the ﬂow ﬁeld while the sample is held at the secondary ﬂow rate (cross ﬂow at
F4). The channel side acts as an outer surface which causes the structure to move
along the channel wall. Due to the diffusion effect, the sample is distributed at
different locations on the wall of the water ﬂow, which can be separated according to
the size of the sample. The delivery system of small particles has a higher mean wall
volume height than larger particles, resulting in earlier, exosome separation.

10.9.4.5 Hydrostatic Filtration Dialysis (HFD)
HFD is often used to isolate urinary extracellular vesicles by diffusing solutes
through cellulose ester dialysis membranes with deﬁned molecular weight cut-offs.
The solvent is also passed through membrane ﬁltration under hydrostatic pressure to
obtain the ﬁltration content. The good thing about the HFD is that it can be ﬁxed and
focused on quality standards and does not require the operation of special equip-
ment, making this process easy. HFD is signiﬁcantly better for a wide range of
sample volumes than differential centrifugation.
10   Future Trends and Innovation in Nano Drug Delivery for Cancer Therapy,. . .    237


10.9.4.6 Polymer-Based Precipitation
Polymer precipitation uses interactions between polymers such as polyethylene
glycol and water molecules and water molecules or salt solutions such as sodium
acetate to neutralize the negative charge of phosphatidylserine on the surface of
exosomes, thereby reducing the solubility and dispersibility of exosomes and the
formation of physical aggregates. Finally, the exosome pellet can be collected by
centrifugation.

10.9.4.7 Immunoaffinity Capture-Based Technology
There are many proteins and receptors in the exosome membrane. Immunoafﬁnity
capture uses the speciﬁc binding of receptors to their ligands or the immunoafﬁnity
of proteins and their speciﬁc antibodies to isolate exosomes. Surface receptors such
as CD9, CD63 and CD81 are considered biomarkers of exosomes and are often used
to remove exosomes through immune afﬁnity capture. The main technologies
developed from this principle are ELISA in microtiter plate format and afﬁnity
capture based on immunomagnetic beads.

10.9.4.8 Microfluidic-Based Exosome Isolation
Microﬂuidic-based exosome isolation is a promising method that uses microﬂuidic
devices to isolate exosomes based on various properties such as size, density, and
immunogenicity. In addition, nanowire trapping, acoustics, lateral displacement and
viscoelastic ﬂow etc. in microﬂuidic platforms. There are many new isolation
methods such as Compared with other existing exosome extraction methods, the
microﬂuidic method is faster, cheaper, requires less samples and reagents, can isolate
exosomes from the source and allow most of the exosomes to be separated by
microﬂuidics into the body's natural form.


10.9.5 Drug Loading on Exosomes

The drug transport mechanism of exosomes can be divided into pre-drug and post-
drug release, depending on whether the drug is transported directly by exosomes
(Table 10.5). As the name suggests, pre-secretory drug loading refers to drug loading
during exosome biogenesis before vesicle separation. By adding to the culture
medium, the cell's endogenous machinery is used to transport drugs to the exosomes
(Zhang et al. 2021). This creates the biggest problem, which is the inability to control
the drug encapsulated in exosomes. The process involves incubation and transfec-
tion of the drug with the brain. Post-secretory drug loading transports drug directly to
individual exosomes, which is more prominent and the encapsulation efﬁciency of
exosomes and drug loading are easier to control. Electroporation, sonication, freeze-
thaw cycles, extrusion, drug-exosome co-incubation, surfactant treatment and dialy-
sis are all in this mechanism.

10.9.5.1 Presecretory Drug Loading

10.9.5.1.1 Co-Incubation (Drug and Cells)
In the coincubation method, the drug is incubated with the brain so that the drug
enters the cell and is then secreted into the exosomal cargo. It is characterized by
simple operation, but the drug-loading efﬁciency is low, and the production and
release process of drug-loaded exosomes cannot be controlled. Some drugs can be
toxic and affect the state of the cell and the release of exosomes.
10   Future Trends and Innovation in Nano Drug Delivery for Cancer Therapy,. . .   239


10.9.5.1.2 Transfection
Transfection is the best way to package peptides, proteins, nucleic acids, and other
active molecules into exosomes. The target gene is introduced into cells by the
process of transfection to express the product of interest, which is then encapsulated
into exosomes by the cellular machinery. The loading efﬁciency is very low as the
loading process cannot be controlled. Direct drug transfer always leads to destruction
or infection of exosomes. HEK293 cells were transfected with plasmids and miRNA
and puriﬁed in culture supernatant to obtain miRNA-loaded exosomes transfected
with epidermal growth factor or GE11 peptide, which can target miRNA delivery to
epidermal growth factor receptor expressing cancer tissues.

10.9.5.2 Post Secretory Drug Loading

10.9.5.2.1 Electroporation
Electroporation uses short high voltage pulses to create pores in the exosome
membrane, allowing the drug to enter the exosome through the pores. This approach
is often used to improve the uptake of siRNA, metal oxide nanoparticles, and small
drugs. In particular, electroporation is limited to some RNAs, whereas miRNAs,
short hairpin RNAs (shRNAs), and mRNAs cannot be transported into exosomes
using this method.

10.9.5.2.2 Sonication
Exosomes puriﬁed from host cells and related substances are mixed and then
homogenized using an ultrasonic probe. The integrity of the exosome membrane is
disrupted by the mechanical shear force, allowing drugs to enter the exosome during
membrane deformation. After sonication, the tension of the exosome membrane was
reduced, which allowed the incorporation of the drug into the lipid bilayer, which
improved its loading capacity. In addition, the fact that the drug will adhere to the
surface of the exosomes may cause the drug to be broken down in the early period
and sustained release in the later stage.

10.9.5.2.3 Freeze-Thaw Cycle
The solution containing the exosomes and drug in a freeze-thaw cycle was incubated
at 37 °C, snap-crozene at -80 °C and thawed at room temperature; This process is
repeated for at least three cycles. This method is easy to implement, but the drug
loading efﬁciency is low. The drug-loaded exosome particles are larger and contain
multiple smaller exosomes that may result from aggregation.

10.9.5.2.4 Extrusion
Extrusion is a physical process. When the drug and exosome mixture was extruded
through a porous polycarbonate membrane with a certain pore size, the exosome
membrane was damaged and ruptured, and the drug in the mixture was evenly
loaded into the exosomes. This method has high loading efﬁciency and is considered
the most efﬁcient method for transporting water-soluble cargoes.
240                                                                      K. Abbasi et al.


10.9.5.2.5 Co-Incubation (Drugs and Exosomes)
During co-incubation of drugs and exosomes, they are only incubated at a certain
temperature, and the drug concentration gradient inside and outside the exosomes
pushes the drug into the exosomes by diffusion. This method only depends on the
hydrophobic contact and diffusion between the exosome membrane and the drug, so
the hydrophobicity of the drug is one of the determinants of loading. This method is
easy to implement, but the disadvantage is its low carrying capacity and the inability
of hydrophilic drugs to pass through the lipid layer of exosomes by passive
diffusion.

10.9.5.2.6 Surfactant Treatment
Surfactants such as saponins can dissolve exosome membrane molecules, thereby
forming pores and increasing membrane permeability. This approach improved the
drug loading capacity of exosomes.

10.9.5.2.7 Dialysis
In hypoosmotic dialysis, exosomes and capsules are transferred to a dialysis mem-
brane or dialysis tube and located in a near-impartial buffer, and then obtain drug
loaded exosomes are received by means of stirring dialysis. furthermore, the
exosomes within the dialysis machine may be pre-dehydrated in ethanol after
which rehydrated in an acidic buffer to lessen the pH inside the exosomes and
promote the formation of a pH gradient inside and outside the exosome membrane,
thereby substantially enhancing the loading efﬁciency of exosomes.


10.9.6 Exosomes Applications in Cancer Treatment

The excellent physicochemical and biological properties of exosomes make them
great potential drug delivery vehicles for the treatment of cancer. Exosomes serve as
excellent carriers for different therapeutics, including nucleic acids, proteins, small
molecules, and other nanomaterials. The involvement of exosomes as drug delivery
systems in cancer therapy for various drugs is summarized in Fig. 10.8.

10.9.6.1 Small Molecule Chemotherapy Drugs
Cur, an anti-inﬂammatory phytochemical, inhibits transcription of miR-21, which
induces PI3K/protein kinase B/phosphatase and tensin homolog (PTEN), nuclear
factor kappa B (NF-κB), and programmed cell death protein 4 pathway in
inﬂuencing apoptosis. It has an inhibitory effect on migration, cell proliferation,
resistance, and rooting, and ﬁnally on cancer cells. Cur’s poor water solubility, lack
of stability, and low bioavailability limit its application as a drug (Chen et al.
2015a, b). Stability and enzyme therapy, these properties make exosome-
encapsulated Cur an oral anti-inﬂammatory drug. Taxanes are a class of cytotoxic
diterpenoids commonly used to treat malignancies (Münz et al. 2018). The repre-
sentative drug is PTX, which is used to treat prostate cancer, breast cancer, breast
cancer, ovarian cancer, lung cancer, and breast cancer. Similar to cur, PTX is also
10   Future Trends and Innovation in Nano Drug Delivery for Cancer Therapy,. . .             241




Fig. 10.8 Application of exosome-based cancer therapy. Different therapeutic agents are delivered
by exosomes to treat cancer. We summarize the application of small molecule chemotherapeutics,
miRNA, antisense oligonucleotides, shRNA, circRNA, siRNA, protein, lncRNA, mRNA, and
aptamer in cancer therapy when exosomes are used as delivery vehicles (Yubo et al. 2022)


sparingly water soluble which limits its clinical applications. Exosomes have also
been used to transport PTX. PTX is delivered to tumor cells via exosomes secreted
by M1 macrophages. The study found that exosomes take advantage of transporters
to deliver more PTX to the tumor site while activating the NF-κB pathway, thereby
enhancing its antitumor effect (Wang et al. 2019a, b). By electroporation of a
mixture of doxorubicin (DOX) and exosomes, DOX-loaded exosomes were success-
fully processed, improving the therapeutic index of the drug and avoiding cardio-
vascular complications (Peng et al. 2019). Recently, incorporation of docetaxel into
exosomes by electroporation resulted in signiﬁcant tumor suppression in non-small
cell lung cancer models.

10.9.6.2 Therapeutic Nucleic Acid
The microRNA (miRNA) is a form of small, single-stranded RNA, 18–25
nucleotides long, found in all eukaryotic cells that can bind complementaryly to
the 3' untranslated region of mRNA, thereby negatively regulating the transcription
of the target gene. miRNA-based cancer therapy has many advantages, such as broad
regulation, which can effectively target the target while simultaneously regulating
other genes of interest to produce clinical results. In addition, miRNAs also have low
toxicity and low immunogenicity properties. However, miRNA-based drugs face
242                                                                     K. Abbasi et al.


many problems, such as poor blood quality and integrity, difﬁculty to enter the
tumor, and off-target effects. Exosomes can be used as a delivery vehicle to
ameliorate these problems. siRNAs are approximately 21 nucleotides long derived
from double-stranded RNA and induce gene silencing by sequence-speciﬁc cleavage
of all complementary mRNAs. As a natural drug delivery carrier, exosomes can
deliver siRNA to tissues or cells in vivo, regulate gene expression, and inhibit tumor
growth. It has the advantage of avoiding phagocytosis, cytotoxicity and low immu-
nogenicity (Chen et al. 2015a, b).

10.9.6.3 Other Therapeutic Compounds
Exosomes can also provide other types of therapeutics. For example, HEK-293T
cells were used to stably express the fusion protein of uracil
phosphoribosyltransferase and cytosine deaminase by transfection, and exosomes
loaded with the fusion protein were isolated and exosomes converted to 5-ﬂuoro-
exchangeable Cytosine prodrugs active forms exert anticancer effects (Mizrak et al.
2013). In addition to proteins and mRNAs, antisense oligonucleotides, long
non-coding RNA (lncRNA), shRNA, circular RNA (circRNA), and aptamers have
also entered cancer exosome therapy (Zhang et al. 2022a, b). Other nanomaterials
are also included in exosomes e.g., Superparamagnetic iron oxide nanoparticles
(SPION) loaded with melanoma-derived exosomes. Recently, exosomes from can-
cer patients were functionalized by amphiphilic polymer-modiﬁed gold
nanoparticles and bound to bovine serum albumin containing chlorine
6 (PMA/Au-BSA@Ce6) via electroporation, enabling deep penetration and higher
retention behavior in tumors (Eftekhari et al. 2023).


10.9.7 Targeted Delivery of Exosomes

Cell-speciﬁc exosomes exhibit a natural response for the cancer microenvironment,
which is important for the development of targets for nanomedicine (Basoglu et al.
2018). For example, macrophage-derived exosomes can target cancer cells via
LFA-1 protein expressed on their surface and ICAM-1 overexpressed in most
cancers. Similarly, exosomes from T cell lines are taken up by myeloid cells,
while mature DC-derived exosomes are highly enriched by T cell utilization
(György et al. 2015). Artiﬁcial modiﬁcation of exosome surface ligands may also
facilitate the development of receptor-mediated tissue targeting.


10.10 Conclusion

The chapter discuss the roles of nanoparticles and hybrid nanoparticles for drug
delivery in chemotherapy, targeted therapy, and immunotherapy and describe the
targeting mechanism of nanoparticle-based drug delivery as well as its function on
reversing drug resistance, compared to conventional drugs, nanoparticle-based drug
delivery has speciﬁc advantages, such as improved stability and biocompatibility,
10   Future Trends and Innovation in Nano Drug Delivery for Cancer Therapy,. . .                243


enhanced permeability and retention effect, and precise targeting. The application
and development of nano-system, which incorporates the combined properties of
different nanoparticles, has led this type of drug-carrier system to the next level. The
Chapter also focuses on combinations of siRNA with chemotherapeutic drug deliv-
ery systems for the treatment of cancer and gives an overview of several nanocarrier
formulations in both research and clinical applications. The use of various
combinations of ultrasounds and nano-systems for cancer treatment has been
reviewed, the current chapter introduces the biogenesis and uptake of exosomes
and compares different approaches for isolation and drug loading, focusing on the
application and current challenges of exosomes as drug delivery vehicles in cancer
therapy.
